--- Strana 6 ---
Preface
The exponential growth of artificial intelligence applications, particularly in
computer vision, has positioned Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) at
the forefront of technological advancement. CNNs have revolutionized
image processing tasks, such as classification, object detection, and
segmentation, enabling remarkable progress across industries including
healthcare, automotive, and telecommunications. However, the widespread
deployment of CNNs comes with significant computational challenges.
These networks require intensive processing power, memory, and energy,
making traditional CPU- and GPU-based systems increasingly inadequate
for real-time or resource-constrained environments.
The escalating demand for efficient CNN execution has led to a critical
need for specialized hardware solutions. Dedicated CNN hardware
accelerators, such as Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs),
Application-Specific Integrated Circuits (ASICs), and custom neural
processing units, have emerged as essential tools. These solutions offer
tailored architectures designed specifically to enhance the performance and
energy efficiency of CNN implementations. The transition toward
specialized hardware is not merely an optimization but a necessity to
sustain the growth and effectiveness of AI-driven technologies.
In addressing these challenges, this book aims to provide a
comprehensive exploration of CNN fundamentals, advanced hardware
implementations, and innovative optimization strategies. The book is
structured into three parts, addressing design, application, and security of
CNN, respectively. Each part of which provides foundational concepts,
practical methodologies, and illustrative case studies, supported by
extensive references.
Contents at Glance
Part I: CNN Design and Implementation Principles
Chapter 1 introduces the fundamentals of CNN architectures, detailing their
operational principles, layer functions, and typical structures, as well as
motivating the need for hardware acceleration.

--- Strana 7 ---
Chapter 2 provides an extensive tutorial covering CNN design and
implementation, both in software frameworks such as TensorFlow and in
hardware using SystemVerilog.
Chapter 3 discusses performance optimization methods, particularly
through approximate multipliers and error injection techniques, enhancing
computational efficiency without significant accuracy loss.
Part II: Applications of CNN in Hardware Security
Chapter 4 presents a detailed tutorial on simulating Denial-of-Service (DoS)
attacks in Network-on-Chip architectures using Gem5, providing insights
into security vulnerabilities and defenses of this communication
architecture, which will be subsequently used to integrated CNN
accelerators.
Chapter 5 explores practical CNN applications and security monitors for
detecting and localizing DoS attacks in Network-on-Chip environments,
highlighting the power of CNNs in hardware security contexts.
Chapter 6 compares various machine learning approaches for DoS
detection, showcasing cascading models and graph-based methods, along
with their respective performance evaluations.
Part III: Threat Analysis of AI Production Chain
Chapter 7 provides a comprehensive review of the security threats inherent
to each stage of the AI models production process, from initial data
acquisition to post-deployment archiving. Risks associated with software
and hardware implementation are also discussed.
Chapter 8 presents a case study on the mitigation of hardware Trojans in
CNN accelerators with exemplary hardware implementation, and detailed
performance evaluations.

--- Strana 8 ---
Book Audience
The intended audience includes graduate students, academic researchers,
hardware design engineers, and practitioners in the field of artificial
intelligence and embedded development. By bridging theoretical
knowledge and practical application, this book aspires to serve as both a
comprehensive textbook and a reference for ongoing research and
development efforts in CNN hardware acceleration.
I sincerely hope this work contributes to the understanding and
advancement of CNN hardware acceleration, encouraging further
innovation and development in this rapidly evolving field.
Basel Halak
Southampton, UK
July 2025

1.1 Introduction
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) [1] have emerged as a cornerstone
of modern deep learning, demonstrating unparalleled success across a
diverse array of applications, including computer vision, natural language
processing, and autonomous systems [2]. Their hierarchical structure,

--- Strana 18 ---
inspired by the human visual system, allows CNNs to learn spatial
hierarchies of features, making them exceptionally adept at handling
complex data [3]. However, the computational intensity of CNN workloads,
characterized by operations such as convolutions, pooling, and matrix
multiplications, presents significant challenges for traditional computing
platforms [4].
While general-purpose processors such as central processing units
(CPUs) and graphics processing units (GPUs) have driven early progress in
deep learning, their limitations have become increasingly evident as CNN
models grow in size and complexity [5]. CPUs, optimized for sequential
processing, struggle to parallelize the extensive computations required by
CNNs. GPUs, despite their parallel processing capabilities, face challenges
related to high power consumption, latency, and inefficiencies in handling
large-scale datasets. These constraints necessitate the development of
hardware accelerators tailored specifically to the unique demands of CNN
workloads [6].

--- Strana 19 ---
1.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter introduces into the principles, design, and implementation of
CNN hardware accelerators. It begins with an overview of CNN
architectures and their key components in Sect. 1.3, establishing the
foundation for understanding the computational requirements of these
models. Next, it examines the limitations of conventional platforms,
emphasizing the need for dedicated accelerators in Sects. 1.4.1 and 1.4.2.
Section 1.4.3 then explores existing hardware solutions, including GPUs,
application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs), field-programmable gate
arrays (FPGAs), and hybrid architectures, comparing their performance,
scalability, and energy efficiency. Section 1.4.4 highlights emerging trends
and challenges, such as energy-efficient designs, edge AI solutions, and the
Network-on-Chip (NoC) architectures in hardware design, offering insights
into the future of CNN hardware acceleration. Finally, Sect. 1.5 provides a
summary of this chapter.
1.3 Convolutional Neural Network
A convolutional neural network (CNN) is a class of deep, feed-forward
artificial neural networks predominantly employed in the analysis of visual
imagery [7]. They are specifically designed to process data with a grid-like
topology, such as images, represented as grids of pixels. CNNs serve as a
cornerstone technology in the field of computer vision and possess robust
capabilities for processing multimedia data. Their applications span diverse
domains, ranging from facial recognition to medical image analysis [8].
CNNs offer several distinct advantages that contribute to their
widespread applicability. First, parameter sharing enables feature detectors,
such as edge detectors, to function effectively across an entire image rather
than being restricted to specific sections, thereby reducing the model’s
parameters, improving computational efficiency, and minimizing
overfitting. Second, CNNs exhibit translation invariance, whereby
identified features can be recognized regardless of their spatial location
within the image. Finally, their hierarchical learning ability allows them to
tackle complex tasks, including image recognition, by progressively
learning patterns at different levels of abstraction [3].

--- Strana 20 ---
A typical convolutional neural network (CNN) architecture consists of
several key layers that work together to extract and process features
hierarchically. Convolutional layers serve as the backbone, detecting spatial
features like edges and patterns. Pooling layers reduce spatial dimensions to
preserve essential information while minimizing computation and
overfitting. Normalization layers, such as batch normalization, improve
training stability and generalization. Activation layers (e.g., ReLU,
Sigmoid, Tanh) introduce nonlinearity, enabling the network to learn
complex patterns [9]. Fully connected layers consolidate features for tasks
like classification, and the classification layer outputs the final predictions,
such as class probabilities. These components collectively allow CNNs to
excel in tasks like image recognition and object detection [10].
In summary, CNNs have revolutionized the field of computer vision by
providing an efficient and highly accurate framework for analyzing visual
data. Their architecture, inspired by the human visual system, enables the
automatic and adaptive learning of spatial hierarchies of features from input
images, making them indispensable tools for numerous visual data
applications.
1.3.1 Operational Principles of CNNs
Within the architecture of convolutional neural networks, image data is
initially input into CNN through the input layer. Following this, the network
employs a sequence of layers, including the convolutional layer, batch
normalization layer, ReLU layer, and pooling layer, to systematically
extract and learn features from the input images [9].
After features are extracted, the final layers (often fully connected
layers) classify the image based on the detected features. Once the network
produces an output, a loss function measures the difference between the
predicted output and the actual labels. Common loss functions include
cross-entropy for classification tasks and mean squared error (MSE) or
mean absolute error (MAE) for regression tasks [11].
Then, perform backpropagation and optimization. During this process,
training begins with the computation of gradients, utilizing the chain rule to
ascertain the gradient of the loss with respect to the network’s weights. This
is followed by a weight adjustment phase, where optimization algorithms,
such as stochastic gradient descent (SGD) or its advanced counterparts like
Adam and RMSprop, refine the weights to minimize the loss. This intricate

--- Strana 21 ---
dance of forward (from input to loss determination) and backward
(involving backpropagation and optimization) passes is iteratively executed
over several epochs, continuing until the network reaches an optimal or
satisfactory performance threshold [12].
CNNs are trained using backpropagation with gradient descent
optimization. The goal is to adjust the weights to minimize the difference
between the predicted output and the actual labels, typically represented by
a loss function. Regularization techniques, such as dropout and weight
decay, are often employed to prevent overfitting [13].
1.3.2 Input Layer
The input layer in a convolutional neural network (CNN) serves as the
initial stage where raw data is introduced into the model for subsequent
processing. Typically, the input layer accepts multidimensional data, such as
images represented by height, width, and channel dimensions. This layer
plays a critical role in defining the structure of the data pipeline, as it must
accommodate the specific dimensions and format of the dataset under
consideration. For instance, an input layer for grayscale images would
require a single channel, whereas for RGB images, it would require three
channels corresponding to red, green, and blue. The input layer neither
performs computations nor alters the data but serves as a conduit to prepare
and structure the input for downstream convolutional layers, ensuring
compatibility and facilitating efficient feature extraction in subsequent
stages [9].
1.3.3 Convolutional Layer
Convolutional layer is the core building block of a CNN. The layer’s
primary function is to detect local patterns using learned kernels (or filters).
When processing an image, these kernels slide (or convolve) around the
input data (such as an image) to produce a feature map, effectively
transforming the input data into a form that makes it easier for the network
to understand. The result of this convolution operation is a set of feature
maps that give the spatial arrangement for the detected feature at different
locations in the input [14].
1.3.3.1 Core Concepts of Convolutional Layer

--- Strana 22 ---
Kernels/Filters
 Kernels (also called filters) are small, trainable matrices
that slide across the input data to extract features such as edges, textures, or
more abstract patterns. Each kernel is applied to the input data via
convolution, producing a feature map as output. For example, a 3 × 3
kernel slides over an image, performing element-wise multiplication and
summing the results.
Stride
 Stride determines how much the kernel shifts at each step during
the convolution operation. Larger strides reduce the size of the output
feature map but may lose fine-grained details. Typical strides: S = 1 (no
skip) or S = 2 (downsampling).
Padding
 Padding is a technique used in convolutional operations to add
extra rows and columns around the edges of the input tensor, enabling the
preservation or modification of spatial dimensions after the operation.
Common types of padding include the following:
Valid Padding (no padding): In this approach, no additional rows or
columns are added, resulting in an output tensor smaller than the input
tensor due to the reduction caused by the kernel size.
Same Padding: Here, padding is added such that the spatial dimensions
of the output tensor match those of the input tensor. This is particularly
useful for maintaining consistency in the feature map size across layers.
Full Padding: This type extends the input tensor by padding to ensure that
every element of the input is fully convolved, leading to an output tensor
larger than the input tensor.
Formula for output size with stride S and padding P:
(1.1)
Where N = input size and F = filter size.
Padding choices significantly impact the receptive field, computational
efficiency, and the overall architecture of convolutional neural networks.

The convolution operation, a fundamental process in deep learning,
involves applying a kernel K to an input tensor X to produce an output
tensor Y . Mathematically, this is expressed as

Additionally, convolutional layers benefit from weight sharing, wherein
a single set of kernel parameters is applied across all spatial locations of the
input tensor. This parameter-sharing mechanism significantly reduces the
number of trainable parameters, thereby improving the model’s
computational efficiency and mitigating the risk of overfitting. A sample
diagram about convolutional layer is shown in Fig. 1.1.

1.3.3.3 Feature Maps and Receptive Fields
Feature maps are the outputs generated by applying a kernel to an input
tensor, effectively highlighting regions where the kernel’s learned pattern is
most prominent. These maps serve as representations of specific features
within the input data, facilitating the detection of patterns such as edges,
textures, or more complex structures [9].
Each value in a feature map corresponds to a receptive field, which
represents the specific region of the input image that influences that value.
As the network depth increases, the receptive fields expand, allowing
deeper layers to capture high-level, abstract features by aggregating
information from larger regions of the input. This hierarchical
Y [i, j] =∑

--- Strana 24 ---
representation is essential for learning complex patterns and achieving
strong performance in tasks such as object recognition and semantic
segmentation [15].
1.3.3.4 Types of Convolutions
Convolutions are versatile operations in deep learning, tailored to different
types of data and tasks.
1D convolutions are used for sequential data such as time series,
signals, or text, with applications in tasks like speech recognition [16].
2D convolutions, the most common type, are applied to image data,
processing 2D grayscale or RGB images to extract spatial features. For
processing 3D data, such as volumetric scans (e.g., CT or MRI) or video
frames [17].
3D convolutions introduce depth as a third dimension, capturing spatial-
temporal information [18].
Dilated (atrous) convolutions expand the receptive field by introducing
gaps between kernel elements, enabling models to capture broader
contextual information without increasing computational parameters, which
is particularly useful in semantic segmentation [19].
Lastly, depthwise separable convolutions decompose standard
convolution into two steps: depthwise convolution, which applies a separate
kernel to each input channel, and pointwise convolution, which uses a
1 × 1 kernel to combine channel outputs. This approach significantly
reduces computational cost while maintaining accuracy, making it a key
component in efficient architectures like MobileNet [20].
1.3.4 Pooling Layer
Pooling layers are an essential component of convolutional neural networks
(CNNs) used to reduce the spatial dimensions of feature maps. This chapter
provides a detailed understanding of their purpose, types, and mathematical
concepts.
1.3.4.1 Purpose of Pooling Layers
Pooling layers serve several critical purposes in convolutional neural
networks (CNNs). First, they facilitate dimensionality reduction by
reducing the size of feature maps, thereby decreasing computational costs
and memory requirements in subsequent layers. This ensures the network

--- Strana 25 ---
remains efficient, even with large input images. Second, pooling enhances
translation invariance by summarizing features within a region, making the
model robust to small shifts or distortions in the input. Lastly, pooling
contributes to preventing overfitting by reducing spatial dimensions, acting
as a form of implicit regularization that improves generalization [9].
1.3.4.2 Mathematical Perspective
Let xl ∈RH l×W l×Dl be the input to the l-th layer, which is now a pooling
layer. The pooling operation requires no parameter (i.e., wi is null; hence,
parameter learning is not needed for this layer). The spatial extent of the
pooling (H × W) is specified in the design of the CNN structure. Assume
that H divides H l and W divides W l and the stride equals the pooling
spatial extent, the output of pooling will be an order three tensor of size
H l+1 × W l+1 × Dl+1, with
(1.3)
This formula ensures precise control over the spatial dimensions of the
feature maps after pooling, which is essential for designing and
understanding CNN architectures. A sample diagram about pooling layer is
shown in Fig. 1.2.
Fig. 1.2 Max pooling layer
1.3.4.3 Padding and Stride in Pooling
Pooling layers often use stride and padding to control the operation’s
behavior and output dimensions.
H l+1 = H l
H ,
W l+1 = W l
W ,
Dl+1 = Dl

--- Strana 26 ---
The stride specifies the step size of the pooling window as it moves
across the feature map. A stride of 2, for example, reduces the spatial
dimensions by approximately half, enabling efficient dimensionality
reduction while summarizing features.
Padding adds extra rows and columns around the edges of the feature
map, similar to its role in convolution. This ensures that the pooling
operation includes the boundary regions of the feature map, preserving
spatial alignment and avoiding loss of information at the edges, especially
in cases where maintaining the feature map’s structure is crucial [9].
1.3.4.4 Types of Pooling
Pooling operations in convolutional neural networks (CNNs) come in
various forms, each designed to summarize feature maps in different ways
while reducing dimensions.
1. Max Pooling: This method retains the maximum value from a pooling
region, effectively highlighting the most prominent feature. For a
pooling window of size 2 × 2, the operation [
] →4 demonstrates
the selection of the maximum value. It is defined as Eq. 1.4:
(1.4)
where F is the window size.
Max pooling takes the maximum value from each F × F window
of the input and assigns it to the output position (i, j).
 
2. Average Pooling: In contrast, this method computes the average of
values within the pooling region, capturing the general trend of
features. Using the same 2 × 2 window, the operation [
] →2.5 is
defined as Eq. 1.5:
(1.5)
This formula defines average pooling in a neural network. Here, X
is the input feature map, and Y [i, j] is the output at position (i, j). A
square window of size F × F slides over the input, and the values
within the window are averaged. The result is assigned to the
corresponding position in the output Y .

--- Strana 27 ---
3. Global Pooling: Global pooling reduces each feature map to a single
value, either by taking the maximum (global max pooling) or the
average (global average pooling) over all spatial dimensions. This
approach is commonly used in architectures like ResNet to replace
fully connected layers, simplifying the model while maintaining
performance [21].
 
4. Adaptive Pooling: Adaptive pooling dynamically adjusts the output
size to a fixed dimension, regardless of input size, making it
particularly useful for handling variable-sized inputs.
For example, in PyTorch, nn.AdaptiveAvgPool2d((5, 5))
produces a 5 × 5 output from any input dimension [21].
 
These pooling techniques collectively contribute to efficient
dimensionality reduction, feature extraction, and model adaptability.
1.3.4.5 Advanced Pooling Techniques
Advanced pooling methods extend the capabilities of standard pooling to
address specific challenges in computer vision tasks [21]:
Region of Interest (ROI) Pooling: This technique is commonly used in
object detection models like Faster R-CNN to dynamically pool features
from regions corresponding to detected objects, enabling precise
localization and classification.
Feature Pyramid Pooling: Employed in networks such as PSPNet, this
method extracts multi-scale features by pooling at different scales,
enhancing the model’s ability to recognize objects of varying sizes and
improving performance in tasks like semantic segmentation.
Fractional Pooling: Designed to handle non-integer downsampling ratios,
fractional pooling interpolates between neighboring values, allowing
finer control over output dimensions and adapting to diverse input sizes
more flexibly.
These techniques enhance pooling’s adaptability, improving the
performance of CNNs in complex tasks like object detection, segmentation,
and multi-scale feature extraction.
1.3.5 Normalization Layer

--- Strana 28 ---
Normalization layers are essential for stabilizing and accelerating the
training of deep neural networks. This chapter provides a detailed
explanation of normalization, the types of normalization layers, and their
mathematical foundation.
1.3.5.1 Purpose of Normalization Layers
Normalization in neural networks involves adjusting the distribution of
data, such as activations or inputs, to improve training and performance. It
ensures stabilized learning by maintaining consistent distributions across
layers, allowing gradients to flow predictably and reducing the risk of
issues like exploding or vanishing gradients. Additionally, normalization
facilitates faster convergence by minimizing internal covariate shift, which
leads to smoother and more efficient optimization. Finally, it enhances
generalization, enabling the model to perform better on unseen data by
encouraging robust and consistent learning of features [15].
1.3.5.2 Types of Normalization
1. Batch Normalization (BN): Batch normalization normalizes
activations within a mini-batch by adjusting their distribution to have a
mean of zero and a variance of one. Specifically, it computes the batch
mean
(1.6)
and variance
(1.7)
where m is the mini-batch size. Each activation is then normalized
as
(1.8)
with a small constant ϵ added for numerical stability. Finally,
learnable parameters γ (scale) and β (shift) are applied to allow the
network to recover useful representations:
(1.9)
Batch normalization reduces internal covariate shift, accelerates
convergence, and provides implicit regularization. However, it may

emerged, presenting novel approaches to significantly enhance neural
network performance. Batch renormalization stands as a notable extension
of batch normalization (BN), specifically engineered to mitigate the
instability encountered with smaller batch sizes. This method introduces

entirely circumvent the need for explicit normalization layers. A prominent
example is FixUp initialization, which employs specialized parameter
initialization schemes and concomitant training adjustments. This
methodology enables the effective training of deep neural networks without
recourse to normalization, consequently reducing computational overhead
and simplifying the overall model architecture [25].
1.3.6 Activation Layer
Activation layers are a critical component of neural networks, introducing
nonlinearity to the model, enabling it to learn complex patterns, and
transforming raw inputs into useful features. This chapter explores the types
of activation functions, their mathematical formulations, characteristics, and
practical use cases.
1.3.6.1 Purpose of Activation Layer
The activation layer serves critical purposes in a neural network. It
introduces nonlinearity, allowing the network to model complex, nonlinear
relationships in data; without it, even a deep network would behave like a
linear model. Activation functions also perform feature transformation,
converting raw weighted sums into meaningful outputs for subsequent
layers to process. Additionally, they influence gradient flow during
backpropagation, playing a vital role in convergence and stability by
ensuring gradients are neither too large nor too small. These functions make
activation layers essential for effective learning and performance [9].
1.3.6.2 Types of Activation Functions

--- Strana 32 ---
1. Sigmoid Function: The sigmoid function, defined as
(1.17)
which maps inputs to the range (0, 1). It is often used in binary
classification tasks as it provides a probability-like output. Sigmoid
offers smooth gradients and bounded outputs, which are useful for
probabilistic interpretations. However, it suffers from the vanishing
gradient problem for large |x|, causing slow learning. It is typically
used in output layers of binary classifiers [26].
 
2. Hyperbolic Tangent (Tanh): The Tanh function, defined as
(1.18)
which maps inputs to the range (−1, 1). Unlike sigmoid, it is zero
centered, which aids in faster optimization. Tanh has strong gradients
for values near zero but also suffers from the vanishing gradient
problem for large |x|. It is commonly used in hidden layers of
traditional RNNs [27].
 
3. Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU): ReLU, defined as
(1.19)
It is the most widely used activation function due to its simplicity
and effectiveness. It preserves positive values while setting negative
values to zero, helping mitigate the vanishing gradient problem.
However, it may encounter the “dying ReLU” problem, where neurons
become inactive with zero gradients. ReLU is extensively used in
CNNs and DNNs [28].
 
4. Leaky ReLU: Leaky ReLU addresses the dying ReLU problem by
allowing a small gradient for negative inputs, defined as
(1.20)
Where α is a small positive constant (e.g., 0.01). This retains
computational simplicity while ensuring neurons remain active. It is a
useful alternative to ReLU when dying neurons are a concern [29].
 
5. Parametric ReLU (PReLU): PReLU extends Leaky ReLU by making
the slope α a learnable parameter during training. This flexibility
allows the network to adaptively adjust the negative slope for improved

6. Exponential Linear Unit (ELU): ELU, defined as
(1.22)
Where α > 0.
It introduces smooth transitions for negative values. It mitigates the
vanishing gradient problem and improves gradient flow, particularly in
deep networks. However, it has slightly higher computational costs and
is used where faster convergence is desired [30].
 
7. Swish: Swish, defined as
(1.23)
It is a smooth, non-monotonic activation function that often
outperforms ReLU in deep architectures like EfficientNet. It enhances
gradient flow but comes with a slightly higher computational cost.
Swish is commonly used in modern CNNs and transformer-based
models [31].
 
1.3.6.3 Comparison of Activation Functions
Choosing an activation function depends on the specific requirements of the
task and architecture. ReLU (rectified linear unit) is the most commonly
used activation function due to its simplicity, efficiency, and ability to
mitigate the vanishing gradient problem, making it a default choice for
many networks like CNNs and DNNs. Variants of ReLU, such as Leaky
ReLU and Parametric ReLU, address issues like the “dying ReLU”
problem. For outputs requiring bounded ranges, functions like Tanh or
sigmoid are more appropriate. In modern architectures, Swish has shown
improved performance in certain scenarios, especially when its higher
computational cost is acceptable [32]. Ultimately, selecting the best
activation function often relies on experimentation, as empirical results can
vary based on the dataset and application (Table 1.1).

1.3.7 Fully Connected Layer
Fully connected (FC) layers are fundamental components of neural
networks, especially in traditional architectures. They connect every input
neuron to every output neuron, enabling the network to learn global patterns
and make predictions.
1.3.7.1 Purpose of Fully Connected Layer
A fully connected (FC) layer is a dense neural layer where every neuron is
connected to all neurons in the previous layer, playing a critical role in
aggregating and utilizing learned features. FC layers combine spatially
distributed features extracted by convolutional layers into a compact
representation, enabling high-level decision-making tasks such as
classification or regression. They act as a transition point by flattening
feature maps into a 1D vector before further processing. FC layers provide a
global representation by integrating information from all preceding neurons,
making them essential for tasks requiring comprehensive feature
aggregation. However, they are computationally expensive due to the large
number of parameters, proportional to the product of the input size and the
number of neurons, which increases the risk of overfitting. Additionally, FC

--- Strana 35 ---
layers are vital for dimensionality transformation, reshaping data to suit
downstream tasks effectively [33].
1.3.7.2 Mathematical Perspective
The output of a fully connected (FC) layer is computed as
(1.24)
where W is the weight matrix of size
[output_neurons × input_neurons], x is the input vector, b is the bias
vector, and f is the activation function.
For an input vector x of size N and a fully connected (FC) layer with
M neurons, the layer’s functionality is defined by its weights and biases,
output computation, and parameter count. The weight matrix W has
dimensions M × N, representing the connections between each input and
neuron, while the bias vector b has size M, adding a trainable offset to each
neuron. The output is computed in two steps: first, a linear transformation is
applied, z = Wx + b, which aggregates the input features weighted by the
learned parameters. Then, the activation function f is applied to the result,
yielding the final output y = f(z).
The total number of parameters in the FC layer, which includes N × M
weights and M biases, is calculated as parameters = (N × M) + M. For
example, with an input vector of size 128 and an FC layer comprising 64
neurons, the parameter count would be (128 × 64) + 64 = 8256. This high
parameter count highlights the computational expense and potential
overfitting risk associated with fully connected layers, making them a
critical yet resource-intensive component of neural networks.
A sample diagram about fully connected layer is shown in Fig. 1.4.
y = f(Wx + b)

--- Strana 36 ---
Fig. 1.4 Fully connected layer
Fully connected layers play a crucial role in aggregating features and
making predictions. Despite their simplicity, they are computationally
intensive and prone to overfitting. Modern architectures often replace or
minimize their use with techniques like global pooling, attention
mechanisms, or sparse connections.
1.3.8 Classification Layer
In the end, a CNN often has a classification layer to make a prediction. If,
for instance, the task is to classify an image among ten categories, this layer
would output ten values, each one representing the probability of the image
belonging to that class. In convolutional neural networks (CNNs), fully
connected (FC) layers serve as a crucial bridge between the feature
extraction process and high-level decision-making tasks such as
classification or regression. The transition to FC layers involves flattening
the multidimensional feature maps produced by convolutional layers into a
one-dimensional vector. For example, a 7 × 7 × 64 feature map is reshaped
into a 3136-dimensional vector, enabling the network to process spatially
distributed features in a compact form. This step consolidates all the learned
spatial features, preparing them for classification or other downstream tasks
[9].
FC layers are frequently employed as the final layers in CNNs,
particularly in image classification tasks, where they act as output layers. In
these scenarios, the final FC layer typically consists of C neurons, where C

--- Strana 37 ---
represents the number of target classes. A softmax activation function is
then applied to this layer to produce class probabilities, ensuring the outputs
are interpretable and suitable for classification. By integrating spatially
learned features from convolutional layers into a global representation, FC
layers enable CNNs to make accurate predictions, highlighting their
essential role in combining feature extraction and task-specific decision-
making [15].
1.3.9 Typical Model Structure of CNNs
The fundamental principle underpinning convolutional neural networks
(CNNs) lies in their utilization of shared local receptive fields and weights.
This core concept enables efficient feature extraction from spatially
correlated data. The seminal work in this field, LeNet, introduced by LeCun
et al. in 1998, is widely recognized as the archetype of CNN architectures
[34].
Over the past decade, convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have
undergone significant evolution, driven by the need for improved
performance, scalability, and efficiency in computer vision tasks. Each
advancement in CNN architecture has introduced novel design principles,
addressing critical challenges such as vanishing gradients, computational
cost, and overfitting. Starting with AlexNet, which demonstrated the power
of deep learning on large-scale image datasets, modern architectures like
ResNet, DenseNet, and EfficientNet have pushed the boundaries of depth,
parameter efficiency, and computational optimization. This section explores
the innovations and design philosophies of key CNN architectures,
highlighting their contributions to the field and their practical applications
in solving complex visual tasks [35].
1. LeNet (1998):
The original LeNet model featured a straightforward architecture
comprising five convolutional layers directly linked to corresponding
pooling layers. Its initial application focused on the recognition of
handwritten and printed characters, demonstrating remarkable
performance for its time. It is worth noting that contemporary
implementations of LeNet often incorporate modifications from the
original design to suit a broader range of applications [34].
 

--- Strana 38 ---
2. AlexNet (2012):
AlexNet marked a milestone in deep learning by winning the 2012
ImageNet competition, demonstrating the potential of deep CNNs for
image classification. It introduced ReLU activation to accelerate
training, dropout to reduce overfitting, and leveraged GPUs for
efficient computation. The architecture consists of five convolutional
layers and three fully connected layers, using large filters such as
11 × 11 and 5 × 5. Despite its breakthrough, AlexNet’s large
parameter count (∼60 M) and computational requirements were
significant drawbacks [36].
 
3. VGG (2014):
The VGG network demonstrated that deeper networks with smaller
filters (3 × 3) could significantly improve performance. VGG-16 and
VGG-19 are the most common configurations, comprising 13–16
convolutional layers followed by three fully connected layers. Max
pooling is used for spatial downsampling. While VGG is simple and
effective, its parameter count is exceedingly high (e.g., 138 M for
VGG-16), making it resource-intensive in terms of computation and
memory [37].
 
4. Inception (GoogLeNet, 2014):
Inception networks introduced inception modules, which apply
convolutions of varying kernel sizes in parallel to capture multi-scale
features. The architecture reduces computation through 1 × 1
convolutions for dimensionality reduction. Auxiliary classifiers
improve gradient flow in deeper networks. Despite its efficiency and
strong performance, the complexity of its design poses implementation
challenges [38].
 
5. ResNet (2015):
ResNet introduced residual connections (skip connections) to
address the vanishing gradient problem, enabling the training of
extremely deep networks, such as ResNet-50 and ResNet-152. Residual
connections allow gradients to flow directly through layers by learning
residual mappings (F(x) + x), significantly improving training
dynamics and performance. However, the increased depth of ResNet
comes at the cost of high computational complexity [39].
 

--- Strana 39 ---
6. MobileNet (2017):
MobileNet was designed for mobile and embedded systems,
introducing depthwise separable convolutions to reduce computation.
Depthwise convolution applies a single filter per input channel,
followed by pointwise convolution to combine channels. This
lightweight design significantly reduces model size and complexity,
albeit with a trade-off in accuracy for highly constrained models.
Variants like MobileNetV2 and V3 further enhance performance and
efficiency [40].
The structure of MobileNet V2 is shown in Fig. 1.5.
Fig. 1.5 Structure of MobileNetV2
 
7. ShuffleNet (2017):
ShuffleNet targeted efficient computation on mobile devices by
introducing grouped convolutions and channel shuffle mechanisms.
Grouped convolutions divide channels into subsets for parallel
computation, while channel shuffle ensures information exchange
across groups. This design achieves a balance between accuracy and
computational efficiency, though its implementation is more complex
compared to simpler architectures [41].
The structure of ShuffleNet V2 is shown in Fig. 1.6.
 

--- Strana 40 ---
Fig. 1.6 Structure of ShuffleNetV2
8. GhostNet (2020):
GhostNet optimized lightweight CNNs by introducing ghost
modules, which generate additional feature maps through inexpensive
linear transformations instead of full convolutions. This reduces
computational cost and parameter count while maintaining competitive
accuracy. GhostNet is highly effective for resource-constrained devices,
though extreme compression may lead to slight performance
degradation [42].
The structure of GhostNet is shown in Fig. 1.7.
 

--- Strana 41 ---
Fig. 1.7 Structure of GhostNet
1.3.10 Innovative Techniques in CNN Architectures
Advancements in convolutional neural network (CNN) architectures have
been propelled by innovative techniques that address challenges like
vanishing gradients, parameter efficiency, computational cost, and feature
representation. This chapter explores these key innovations, detailing their
mechanics, mathematical foundations, and contributions to modern deep
learning.
1.3.10.1 Residual Connections
Residual connections, first introduced by ResNet, are a key innovation in
deep learning that simplify optimization and enable the training of very
deep networks [43]. Instead of learning direct mappings, residual
connections allow networks to learn residual mappings, where the output is
the sum of the input and a learned function. Mathematically, in a residual
block, the output is expressed as
(1.25)
Here, F(x) is the residual mapping, and x represents the input to the block.
This formulation ensures that the identity mapping is preserved, improving
gradient flow during backpropagation and addressing the vanishing gradient
problem common in deep networks.
The advantages of residual connections include the ability to train very
deep architectures, such as ResNet-152, without degradation in accuracy.
They also facilitate stable gradient propagation, allowing the model to
achieve better optimization and performance with increasing depth.
Residual connections are widely applied in state-of-the-art models,
including ResNet, Inception-ResNet, and transformer-based architectures,
demonstrating their versatility and importance in modern deep learning
[43].
1.3.10.2 Dense Connections
Dense connections, introduced by DenseNet, connect each layer in a dense
block to all its subsequent layers, ensuring that features from earlier layers
are reused throughout the network. This design promotes feature reuse,
y = F(x) + x

--- Strana 42 ---
encourages efficient gradient flow, and reduces redundant computation by
directly leveraging previously learned features [44].
Mathematically, the output of the l-th layer in a dense block is given by
(1.26)
Here, [x0, x1, … , xl−1] denotes the concatenation of the feature maps from
all preceding layers, and Hl represents the transformation function (e.g.,
convolution, batch normalization, activation). This formulation ensures that
each layer has direct access to all preceding feature maps, leading to
improved feature propagation and gradient flow.
Dense connections offer several advantages. They reduce parameter
redundancy by reusing features, making DenseNet more parameter efficient
compared to traditional architectures with feed-forward connections.
Additionally, they enhance gradient propagation, which facilitates better
learning in deeper networks.
Dense connections are widely applied in DenseNet for image
classification and are particularly effective in tasks that benefit from feature
reuse, such as segmentation, where multi-scale and detailed features are
crucial. This approach exemplifies how densely connected architectures can
achieve high performance with fewer parameters compared to conventional
deep networks [44].
1.3.10.3 Attention Mechanisms
Attention mechanisms dynamically focus on the most relevant parts of an
input, enhancing feature extraction and representation [45]. They play a
vital role in both convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and transformer
architectures by selectively emphasizing important spatial regions, feature
channels, or long-range dependencies. Key variants include spatial
attention, which highlights significant regions in the input for tasks like
object detection, channel attention, which amplifies critical feature
channels, and self-attention, which computes relationships between all input
elements, capturing global dependencies commonly used in transformers
[46].
Notable implementations include Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) blocks,
which recalibrate channel-wise feature representations. SE blocks use a
global pooled feature map to generate attention weights through the formula
s = σ(W2δ(W1z)), where W1 and W2 are learnable weights, δ is a ReLU
activation, and σ is sigmoid. These blocks are integral to models like
xl = Hl([x0, x1, … , xl−1])

--- Strana 43 ---
EfficientNet. Nonlocal networks extend this idea by computing
relationships across all positions in the input, capturing long-range
dependencies. Attention mechanisms are widely applied in models like
EfficientNet (SE blocks), Vision Transformers (ViT) (self-attention), and
YOLOv5 (spatial and channel attention), showcasing their adaptability and
effectiveness in modern deep learning [45].
1.3.10.4 Depthwise Separable Convolutions
Depthwise separable convolutions are an efficient variant of standard
convolutions, designed to reduce computational complexity while
maintaining accuracy. They decompose the standard convolution operation
into two distinct steps: depthwise convolution, which applies a separate
spatial filter to each input channel, and pointwise convolution, which
combines the outputs of depthwise convolution using 1 × 1 convolutions.
For an input with Cin channels, this approach performs spatial filtering and
channel combination separately, significantly reducing the number of
computations compared to standard convolutions [47].
The primary advantages of depthwise separable convolutions include
reduced computational cost and memory usage, making them particularly
effective for resource-constrained environments. Additionally, they preserve
spatial and channel information effectively, ensuring minimal trade-offs in
performance. These convolutions are widely adopted in efficient network
architectures like MobileNet, Xception, and EfficientNet, where optimizing
for speed and resource efficiency is critical. Their ability to balance
accuracy and efficiency has made them a cornerstone in modern lightweight
and scalable deep learning models [47].
Innovative techniques in CNNs have transformed the way neural
networks process and represent data. From residual and dense connections
that improve gradient flow to attention mechanisms and efficient
convolutions that optimize computational costs, these advancements have
enabled deeper, faster, and more accurate models. Each technique has found
its niche in modern architectures, forming the backbone of state-of-the-art
models used across industries [3].
1.3.11 Applications of CNNs
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have revolutionized numerous
fields by offering unparalleled performance in processing visual and spatial

--- Strana 44 ---
data. Their ability to learn hierarchical features and extract intricate patterns
has enabled groundbreaking applications across industries. This chapter
explores key real-world applications and case studies, demonstrating the
transformative impact of CNNs.
1.3.11.1 Real-World Applications and Case Studies
Image classification is a fundamental task in computer vision where
convolutional neural networks (CNNs) assign labels to images based on
learned features. By extracting hierarchical patterns from raw image data,
CNNs enable accurate categorization and identification across a wide range
of applications. In medical imaging, CNNs classify X-rays, MRIs, and CT
scans to assist in diagnosing diseases such as pneumonia or detecting
tumors. For autonomous vehicles, they identify road signs, pedestrians, and
objects to support safe navigation. In retail and e-commerce, CNNs
categorize products based on visual attributes, enhancing search and
recommendation systems [3]. A notable advancement in image
classification is the introduction of ResNet in 2015, which set new
benchmarks on the ImageNet dataset [48]. ResNet’s use of residual
connections allowed deeper architectures to be trained effectively by
learning residual mappings instead of direct mappings. This innovation
significantly improved classification accuracy while reducing overfitting,
highlighting the potential of deep architectures to advance the field of
image classification.
Object detection is a key task in computer vision that combines
classification and localization to identify objects in an image and draw
bounding boxes around them. This task extends beyond merely recognizing
what is in an image by also determining where the objects are located. It has
diverse applications across various domains. In surveillance, object
detection helps identify suspicious activities or unauthorized access in
security footage. For autonomous vehicles, it is essential for real-time
identification of vehicles, pedestrians, and obstacles to ensure safe
navigation. In agriculture, object detection aids in identifying pests and
diseases or estimating crop yield, improving efficiency and productivity
[49]. A significant milestone in object detection is the development of
YOLO (You Only Look Once), a single-stage detection pipeline that
revolutionized real-time object detection [50]. By combining detection and
classification into a single step, YOLO dramatically reduced inference time

--- Strana 45 ---
while maintaining high accuracy, making it suitable for applications
requiring low-latency detection. Its efficiency and reliability have made
YOLO a widely adopted framework in real-world scenarios demanding
real-time performance.
Face recognition systems are designed to identify or verify individuals
by analyzing facial features from images or video streams. These systems
have become integral in various domains. In security, face recognition is
used for access control and surveillance, ensuring only authorized
individuals can enter restricted areas. In consumer electronics, facial
unlocking features enhance convenience and security in smartphones and
laptops. In retail, face recognition enables personalized customer
experiences by analyzing facial data to understand preferences and
behaviors [51]. A notable advancement in this field is FaceNet, which
employs deep embeddings to map faces into a high-dimensional feature
space [52]. This approach allows for highly accurate face verification and
clustering of identities, enabling robust authentication systems and efficient
video analytics. FaceNet’s deployment in applications like secure
authentication and surveillance highlights its effectiveness in real-world
face recognition tasks.
Autonomous vehicles rely extensively on convolutional neural networks
(CNNs) for real-time scene understanding, integrating multiple vision tasks
to ensure safe and efficient operation. Core tasks include object detection
using models like YOLO for identifying vehicles, pedestrians, and obstacles
in real-time, semantic segmentation with architectures like DeepLab to
classify road scenes into meaningful regions and monocular depth
estimation using CNNs to predict depth information from single-camera
inputs. These capabilities enable autonomous systems to interpret complex
environments dynamically [53]. A prominent example is Tesla’s Autopilot,
which uses deep CNNs for multi-camera vision processing. This system
powers advanced driver assistance features such as lane keeping, obstacle
detection, and adaptive cruise control, forming the foundation for achieving
full vehicle autonomy. By combining these techniques, autonomous
vehicles demonstrate the potential of CNNs in transforming transportation
[54].
1.3.11.2 Challenges in Real-World Applications

--- Strana 46 ---
Despite their widespread success, convolutional neural networks (CNNs)
face significant challenges when applied to real-world scenarios. Scalability
is a major issue, as efficiently processing and managing large-scale data
remains a bottleneck in deploying CNNs in practical systems. Another
critical concern is robustness, where ensuring reliable performance under
diverse conditions, such as changes in lighting, noise, or adversarial attacks,
is essential for real-world reliability. Data privacy is also a growing
challenge, especially in sensitive domains like healthcare, where balancing
model performance with ethical considerations and privacy preservation is
paramount [2]. Lastly, hardware constraints pose difficulties in deploying
CNNs on edge devices or systems with limited computational resources,
necessitating model optimization and lightweight architectures. Addressing
these challenges is crucial for the seamless integration of CNNs into
practical applications [6].
1.3.11.3 Future Directions
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) continue to evolve, with several
promising directions shaping their future applications and developments.
One key trend is the integration with transformers, where CNNs are
combined with transformer architectures to handle multimodal tasks such as
image captioning and vision-language modeling, leveraging the strengths of
both for enhanced performance [55]. Another area of growth is federated
learning, which enables collaborative model training across decentralized
datasets while preserving privacy, particularly in sensitive domains like
medical imaging and financial analysis [56]. Additionally, real-time
processing is gaining attention, with efforts focused on optimizing CNNs
for low-latency applications such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality
(VR), and robotics. These advancements highlight the growing adaptability
of CNNs to meet the demands of diverse, cutting-edge applications [57].
CNNs have transformed industries with applications spanning
healthcare, autonomous systems, and creative domains. Their ability to
model complex patterns and integrate seamlessly with real-world systems
has unlocked unprecedented opportunities [3]. By exploring successful case
studies and addressing deployment challenges, this chapter highlights the
versatility and potential of CNNs in solving real-world problems.

--- Strana 47 ---
1.4 CNN Hardware Accelerator
1.4.1 Motivation for CNN Hardware Accelerators
The rapid proliferation of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) in
applications such as computer vision, natural language processing, and
autonomous systems has highlighted the limitations of traditional
computing platforms like central processing units (CPUs) and graphics
processing units (GPUs). While these platforms offer significant flexibility
and computational power, they are not inherently optimized for the unique
demands of CNN workloads [58]. This subsection introduces into the
primary limitations of these platforms and explores how dedicated hardware
accelerators address these challenges.
1.4.1.1 Limitations of CPUs and GPUs for CNN Workloads
CPUs are designed for general-purpose computing and excel at sequential
processing tasks. However, their inability to efficiently parallelize
operations limits their suitability for the highly parallel and data-intensive
computations required by CNNs [59]. GPUs, on the other hand, are well
suited for parallel workloads due to their architecture, which includes
thousands of cores optimized for simultaneous data processing [6].
In spite of the aforementioned advantages, GPUs are not without their
limitations. One significant concern is their high power consumption, a
characteristic shared with CPUs, which renders both unsuitable for
deployment in energy-constrained environments such as edge devices or
battery-powered systems. Furthermore, latency arising from the data
transfer between memory and processing units frequently presents a
bottleneck, particularly critical in real-time applications such as
autonomous driving and medical imaging. Lastly, processing large-scale
datasets or high-resolution inputs can severely strain the memory bandwidth
and computational resources of conventional platforms, inevitably leading
to a degradation in performance [60].
1.4.1.2 The Role of Hardware Accelerators
Hardware accelerators represent a significant advancement in
computational design, specifically engineered to overcome the inherent
limitations of general-purpose processing platforms. These specialized units

--- Strana 48 ---
bridge the critical gap between the demanding computational requirements
of convolutional neural network (CNN) workloads and the capabilities of
existing hardware by meticulously tailoring their architecture to the unique
characteristics of these operations [61]. Key attributes distinguishing
hardware accelerators include optimized computational units, which are
dedicated processing elements meticulously designed for the efficient
execution of ubiquitous CNN operations such as convolution and activation
functions. Furthermore, these accelerators prioritize reduced power
consumption through energy-efficient designs that effectively lower power
requirements without compromising computational performance. Crucially,
they incorporate custom dataflow architectures, which are meticulously
optimized to enhance data locality and significantly reduce memory access
overhead, thereby boosting overall efficiency and throughput [62].
1.4.1.3 Performance Metrics for Hardware Accelerators
The effectiveness of hardware accelerators is rigorously assessed through
the evaluation of several critical performance metrics. Throughput
quantifies the overall processing capability by measuring the number of
operations completed per unit of time. Latency, conversely, represents the
time required to process a single input, a metric of paramount importance
for applications demanding real-time responsiveness. Power efficiency
evaluates the computational performance achieved per unit of power
consumed, typically expressed as operations per watt, highlighting the
energy proportionality of the accelerator. Finally, scalability assesses the
accelerator’s capacity to sustain performance levels as the size of the
workload or the complexity of the hardware configuration increases [63].
1.4.2 Challenges in Hardware Design
Designing efficient hardware accelerators for CNN workloads involves
overcoming several technical challenges. This subsection highlights the
primary issues faced in hardware design and their implications for
performance and cost.
Designing hardware accelerators for CNN workloads involves several
significant technical challenges, primarily categorized into three areas.
First, handling large memory requirements is crucial. CNN models often
comprise millions of parameters and demand substantial memory for
storing intermediate feature maps during both inference and training.

--- Strana 49 ---
Efficiently managing these memory demands is critical for minimizing
memory bandwidth bottlenecks and reducing energy consumption. High
bandwidth is essential for data transfer between memory and processing
units; without it, contention and reduced throughput can occur.
Furthermore, accessing off-chip memory consumes significantly more
power than on-chip operations, necessitating innovative memory
hierarchies and caching strategies [64].
Second, efficiently managing data movement is a major concern. Data
movement, both within the accelerator and between the accelerator and
external memory, accounts for a substantial portion of power consumption.
Effective dataflow strategies are vital for minimizing latency and enhancing
locality. Optimizing interconnects and memory layouts can significantly
reduce the time it takes for data to reach processing units, while designing
architectures that maximize data reuse and minimize redundant transfers
can greatly improve data locality [65].
Finally, balancing performance with cost and area constraints is
paramount. Hardware accelerators must strike a delicate balance between
achieving high performance and adhering to practical design considerations.
Key trade-offs include cost efficiency, physical area, and the balance
between flexibility and specialization [66]. Achieving performance goals
without escalating manufacturing costs is particularly important for
consumer-grade devices. Simultaneously, designing compact accelerators to
meet integration requirements within System-on-Chip (SoC) platforms is
essential. Moreover, ensuring the accelerator can support a range of CNN
architectures without incurring significant performance overheads is a
critical aspect of balancing flexibility with specialization [67].
1.4.3 Existing CNN Accelerator Hardware Solutions
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have established themselves as a
foundational technology in modern deep learning applications, enabling
advancements in areas ranging from image and speech recognition to
autonomous systems and natural language understanding [2]. However, the
computational intensity of CNNs, which arises primarily from operations
like convolution and matrix multiplication, poses significant challenges for
conventional computing architectures. To address these challenges, a
diverse range of CNN hardware accelerators has been developed,
encompassing both general-purpose and domain-specific solutions [58].

--- Strana 50 ---
This section provides a comprehensive overview of existing CNN
accelerator hardware solutions, focusing on architectural innovations,
performance trade-offs, and practical implementations.
1.4.3.1 Details of the Proposed Hardware Implementation
CNN accelerators are broadly classified into three major categories:
general-purpose processors (e.g., GPUs), domain-specific accelerators (e.g.,
ASICs and FPGAs), and hybrid systems. Each category employs unique
architectural approaches to optimize performance for CNN workloads.
1. Graphics Processing Units (GPUs):
Graphics processing units (GPUs) have played a pivotal role in the
growth of deep learning by enabling highly parallel computations
critical for training and deploying convolutional neural networks
(CNNs). NVIDIA’s CUDA architecture has been particularly
transformative, providing a platform for the efficient execution of
matrix operations that are central to CNNs [68]. Modern GPUs, such as
NVIDIA’s Ampere architecture, further enhance deep learning
performance with specialized Tensor Cores, which are designed to
accelerate computations for tasks like training and inference. These
Tensor Cores support mixed-precision arithmetic (e.g., FP16 and
INT8), striking a balance between accuracy and computational speed,
and leverage sparsity, exploiting the redundancy in CNN models to
improve efficiency [69].
GPUs remain highly versatile, capable of handling both training
and inference across a wide range of neural network architectures.
Their flexibility and performance make them indispensable in deep
learning research and development, powering advancements in
applications from image classification to real-time object detection and
autonomous systems [6].
 
2. Application-Specific Integrated Circuits (ASICs):
Application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs) are custom-
designed chips optimized for specific computational tasks, offering
unmatched performance and efficiency for convolutional neural
network (CNN) workloads [70]. A notable example is the Google TPU
(tensor processing unit), which employs a systolic array architecture to
accelerate matrix multiplication, a core operation in CNNs [71]. TPUs
 

--- Strana 51 ---
feature dedicated hardware for both dense and sparse tensor operations,
on-chip memory buffers to reduce latency and power consumption, and
deliver high throughput for training and inference tasks, making them a
key component in large-scale deep learning applications [60].
For resource-constrained environments, edge TPUs are tailored to
provide a balance between performance and energy efficiency. These
chips are optimized for deployment in low-power devices, such as IoT
systems and mobile platforms, enabling CNNs to operate effectively in
real-time and off-grid scenarios. Together, TPUs and edge TPUs
illustrate the potential of ASICs in advancing deep learning by
addressing diverse computational requirements across cloud and edge
settings [72].
3. Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs):
Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) provide a flexible
alternative to application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs), offering
developers the ability to customize hardware configurations to meet the
specific demands of convolutional neural network (CNN) architectures.
One of their key advantages is reconfigurability, which allows FPGAs
to be reprogrammed for new algorithms or workloads, making them
highly adaptable to evolving deep learning techniques. Additionally,
their fine-grained parallelism enables efficient utilization of hardware
resources, optimizing performance for tasks like matrix multiplication
and convolutional operations in CNNs [73].
Prominent FPGA solutions, such as Xilinx’s Versal AI Core [74]
and Intel’s Stratix series [75], are increasingly being deployed in both
edge and cloud environments. These platforms leverage the flexibility
and efficiency of FPGAs to accelerate deep learning tasks in diverse
applications, from low-power IoT devices to high-performance data
centers, bridging the gap between adaptability and computational
power.
 
4. Hybrid Architectures:
Hybrid architectures leverage the strengths of multiple hardware
types, such as CPUs, GPUs, and ASICs, to build heterogeneous
systems optimized for end-to-end deep learning workflows. These
architectures are designed to balance computational efficiency,
 

--- Strana 52 ---
scalability, and flexibility across various stages of deep learning, from
data preprocessing to model training and inference [76].
One notable example is NVIDIA’s DGX systems, which integrate
high-performance GPUs with high-speed interconnects, enabling
efficient distributed training for large-scale models. These systems are
optimized for parallel processing, allowing researchers to accelerate
training times significantly [77]. Another example is AMD’s ROCm
ecosystem, which facilitates seamless CPU-GPU coprocessing. ROCm
provides an open platform for deep learning workflows, enabling
efficient data sharing and parallel computation between processors
[78].
Hybrid architectures have become essential for handling the
increasing complexity of deep learning tasks, offering tailored solutions
for diverse applications in both research and industry.
1.4.3.2 Benchmarks, Results, and Comparisons
Evaluating convolutional neural network (CNN) accelerators necessitates a
comprehensive assessment of key performance metrics, including
throughput (measured in operations per second, such as FLOPS for
floating-point operations) , latency (the time required for a single inference
or training iteration), energy efficiency (performance per watt, which is
critical for edge and mobile deployments), and scalability (the ability to
maintain performance across varying model sizes and workloads) [63].
Standardized benchmarks, such as MLPerf [79], are instrumental in
facilitating objective comparisons across diverse hardware platforms. Key
insights from these evaluations reveal distinct strengths among leading
accelerators. For instance, Google TPUs demonstrate superior performance
in large-scale model training, particularly for workloads like BERT and
ResNet-50, with their energy efficiency and seamless integration with
TensorFlow making them ideal for enterprise applications [71]. Conversely,
NVIDIA GPUs excel in versatility, supporting a broad spectrum of
workloads beyond CNNs. Ampere GPUs, in particular, showcase superior
performance for mixed-precision training, positioning them as a preferred
choice for research endeavors [69]. When comparing ASICs and FPGAs,
ASICs typically achieve higher peak performance for specific tasks, while
FPGAs offer greater adaptability and lower development costs, rendering
them suitable for prototyping and edge applications. These comparative

--- Strana 53 ---
results underscore the inherent trade-offs between different hardware
platforms, emphasizing that the optimal accelerator choice is frequently
determined by the specific requirements of the application.
1.4.4 Trends and Advancements in CNN Hardware
Accelerators
The field of CNN hardware accelerators is characterized by rapid
innovation, driven by the increasing complexity of deep learning models
and the demand for real-time, energy-efficient solutions [80]. Several key
trends and advancements are shaping this landscape.
As deep learning models continue to evolve, there’s a strong focus on
specialized architectures for emerging models. New hardware architectures
are being designed to meet the unique demands of transformer-based
models and sparse neural networks. These emerging architectures aim to
optimize performance and efficiency for workloads characterized by large-
scale matrix operations and irregular data access patterns [81]. One notable
example is NVIDIA’s Hopper architecture, which introduces advanced
features tailored for transformer-based models. Hopper includes
Transformer Engine, which leverages mixed-precision arithmetic and
optimized hardware pipelines to accelerate transformer workloads, such as
GPT and BERT, while maintaining high accuracy. It also supports dynamic
sparsity to improve efficiency when processing sparse neural networks [82].
These specialized architectures reflect a growing focus on optimizing
hardware for the next generation of machine learning models, addressing
their unique computational challenges and enabling more efficient training
and deployment at scale.
Another significant trend is energy-efficient computing. As
sustainability becomes a key priority in computing, hardware accelerators
are increasingly integrating energy-efficient technologies to reduce power
consumption while maintaining performance. These innovations aim to
address the high energy demands of modern deep learning workloads,
making them more environmentally and economically sustainable. One
promising approach is the use of nonvolatile memory (NVM) technologies,
such as ReRAM (Resistive RAM), which consume significantly less power
compared to traditional memory systems. By retaining data without
continuous power, NVM reduces energy consumption for data storage and
retrieval, especially in applications like edge computing and mobile AI [83].

--- Strana 54 ---
These energy-efficient technologies represent significant strides toward
greener AI solutions, aligning computational advancements with the
growing need for sustainable practices in technology.
The rapid expansion of IoT and edge devices has fueled the
development of edge AI solutions. These specialized accelerators are
designed to deliver low-power, high-performance inference directly on edge
hardware, enabling real-time AI processing without relying on cloud
connectivity. This addresses critical concerns around latency, privacy, and
energy efficiency. Notable examples include Apple’s Neural Engine,
integrated into the company’s custom processors, which provides efficient
on-device computation for tasks like image recognition, natural language
processing, and augmented reality. It is optimized for low power
consumption, ensuring seamless performance in devices like iPhones and
iPads [84]. Similarly, Qualcomm’s AI Engine powers advanced AI
capabilities in mobile and IoT platforms. By combining heterogeneous
processing units, including GPUs, CPUs, and DSPs, it supports versatile
workloads such as voice recognition, camera enhancements, and predictive
analytics [85]. Edge AI solutions like these enable powerful AI capabilities
in resource-constrained environments, making them essential for
applications in smart homes, autonomous devices, and industrial IoT
systems. They represent a shift toward more distributed AI architectures,
empowering devices to process and act on data locally with efficiency and
reliability.
Network-on-Chip (NoC) technologies are emerging as a critical
component in hardware accelerators, addressing the growing demands of
on-chip communication in deep learning and other high-performance
computing applications. As the complexity of hardware accelerators
increases, efficient NoC designs are essential to ensure smooth data flow,
low latency, and high throughput across processing cores and memory [86].
Key advancements in NoC architectures include the development of high-
bandwidth, low-latency interconnects that optimize data transfer between
processing elements, minimizing bottlenecks in communication-intensive
workloads [87]. Additionally, adaptive routing algorithms have been
introduced to dynamically manage workloads and reduce congestion,
enabling more efficient utilization of on-chip resources. Scalable NoC
designs further ensure that performance remains efficient across a range of
hardware sizes, from compact edge devices to large-scale data center

--- Strana 55 ---
accelerators [88]. These advancements are driving the next generation of
hardware accelerators, enabling them to handle the ever-increasing
demands of modern machine learning applications with improved efficiency
and scalability.
In summary, CNN hardware accelerators are evolving to meet the
demands of next-generation AI applications, balancing performance,
scalability, and sustainability. The ongoing integration of emerging
technologies and design paradigms promises to further enhance their
capabilities, paving the way for more efficient and accessible deep learning
solutions.
1.5 Conclusions
The rapid advancement of convolutional neural networks has reshaped the
landscape of artificial intelligence, driving transformative changes across
industries. However, the computational demands of these models have
outpaced the capabilities of traditional hardware, underscoring the critical
need for specialized hardware accelerators. This chapter has explored the
principles and implementation of CNN hardware accelerators, shedding
light on their pivotal role in overcoming the limitations of general-purpose
platforms.
By examining key components of CNN architectures and their
computational requirements, the chapter established a foundation for
understanding the unique challenges faced by hardware designers. Through
an in-depth analysis of existing solutions, including GPUs, ASICs, and
FPGAs, the chapter demonstrated how tailored architectures enhance
throughput, reduce latency, and improve energy efficiency. Furthermore,
emerging trends such as energy-efficient computing, edge AI, and network-
on-chip architectures were discussed, highlighting the innovative
approaches shaping the future of CNN hardware acceleration.
Looking ahead, the integration of advanced materials, energy-efficient
computing, and edge AI promises to further enhance the capabilities of
CNN accelerators. As artificial intelligence continues to evolve, the synergy
between algorithmic and hardware innovation will remain essential in
meeting the growing demands of next-generation applications. By
addressing current challenges and embracing emerging opportunities, CNN

--- Strana 56 ---
hardware accelerators will continue to drive progress in deep learning,
enabling breakthroughs across scientific, industrial, and consumer domains.

2. A Tutorial on CNN Design from Model
Training to Hardware Implementation
Peiyao Sun1   , Haosen Yu1   , Tom J. Kazmierski1    and Basel Halak1  
School of Electronics and Computer Science, University of Southampton,
Southampton, UK
