Email: Basel.Halak@soton.ac.uk
Keywords CNN design – TensorFlow – SystemVerilog – CNN training –
Hardware modeling – Software implementation – Hardware simulation –
Testbench design – Layer functions – Network evaluation – Dataflow architecture
– RTL design – FPGA implementation – CNN deployment – Deep learning
frameworks
2.1 Introduction
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are widely used in the field of image
classification. While many recent works and books focus on theoretical aspects,
there is a lack of detailed tutorials to guide beginners in building their first models.
However, for beginners in this field, whether focusing on hardware or software,
one of the primary challenges is the lack of a comprehensive and integrated
tutorial that guides them through the process of implementing their designs. This

--- Strana 64 ---
chapter aims to provide a clear, practical guide to help beginners quickly
understand mainstream training frameworks, understand key design
considerations, and implement their first CNN model effectively.
This chapter covers both software-level and hardware-level aspects of CNN
design. At the software level, it introduces Python-based frameworks such as
TensorFlow, while at the hardware level, how to utilize Vitis AI to deploy CNN
models on Xilinx platforms quickly and how to design custom CNN hardware
accelerators using SystemVerilog. By the end of this chapter, readers will have a
solid foundation for training CNN models and implementing them on hardware
platforms, which helps them to develop their first CNN-related implementation
quickly.
TensorFlow, developed by Google [1], and PyTorch, developed by Meta AI
[2], are the most popular learning frameworks in both research and industrial
fields. TensorFlow supports various hardware platforms such as CPUs, GPUs, and
TPUs. It combines static computation graphs for optimized performance with
Eager Execution for dynamic debugging and prototyping. It also offers
comprehensive Toolkits such as TensorBoard for visualization, TensorFlow
Serving for inference, and the TensorFlow Model Optimization Toolkit for
quantization and pruning [1, 3]. PyTorch’s advantage is its flexibility, offering a
dynamic computation graph that simplifies debugging and supports innovative
neural network designs. In addition, PyTorch is also highly integrated with
Python, performs well in terms of GPU-accelerated computations, provides tools
like TorchScript for model deployment, and supports ONNX for high
transferability [4].
Deploying neural networks directly on FPGAs using HDLs, such as
SystemVerilog, provides unique advantages in flexibility and customization.
While VITIS AI relies on predefined hardware architectures for CNNs, direct
implementation using HDLs allows developers to design customized CNN
hardware accelerators. This approach is particularly advantageous for creating
specialized hardware structures or implementing uncommon components, such as
utilizing TOSAM approximate multipliers [5] instead of normal accuracy
multipliers. For developers aiming to design highly customized neural network
accelerators, the use of HDLs remains an essential and irreplaceable option.

--- Strana 65 ---
2.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter provides a comprehensive tutorial on utilizing various tools and
frameworks for training and deploying CNNs. In Sect. 2.3, we detail the process
of using TensorFlow to train a CNN. Subsequently, Sect. 2.4 explores the
implementation of custom CNN hardware accelerators using SystemVerilog. The
example code is available in the GitHub repository.1
2.3 Software-Level CNN Model Training Based on
TensorFlow Framework
This section provides an overview of TensorFlow tutorials, focusing on key
aspects of training a network using TensorFlow. We will explore common layer
functions, the framework model structure, guidelines for writing code, and the
parameter definitions required for function implementation. All codes in this
section are Python Code, and TensorFlow will be imported by the code shown in
Fig. 2.1
Fig. 2.1 Importing TensorFlow
2.3.1 Layer Functions
2.3.1.1 Convolutional Layers
In widely used neural networks such as MobileNet, ResNet, and ShuffleNet, three
types of convolutional layers are employed: standard convolutional layers,
pointwise convolutional layers, and depthwise convolutional layers. This section
describes the structure and input parameters of the layer functions in TensorFlow
that correspond to these three types of convolutional layers. For a standard
convolutional layer and a pointwise convolutional layer, the layer function is
tf.keras.layers.Conv2D whose default and full structure is shown in Fig.
2.2.

--- Strana 66 ---
Fig. 2.2 TensorFlow Conv2D layer definition
Let us start with the basic parameter Settings, as shown in Fig. 2.2, including
filters, kernel_size, strides, padding, data_format, group, and activation.
Filter: Filter is the number of kernels in this layer, and the data format is int. A
common number of filter values is 16, 32, 64, 128, and 256.
kernel_size: The kernel size represents the size of the convolution kernel of this
layer, which can be in int format or tuple format with two elements. For kernel
size Kernel_size = 3 is equivalent to Kernel_size = (3,3), but if the Kernel of the
current convolution layer is not square, use the tuple format as needed, such as
Kernel_size = (3,5). The common value of the kernel size is 3,5,7,9, and 11.
padding: In the tf.keras.layers.Conv2D convolutional layer function,
padding takes two inputs: “valid” or “same.” “valid” means not padding the
input of the layer, while same means padding the layer so that the size of the
input layer’s two-dimensional space is equal to the size of the output layer’s
two-dimensional space. For the situation of the special padding, such as adding
a specific size of the padding, or the padding size is not the same in all
directions, you can use keras.layers.ZeroPadding2D function.
data_format: The data_format parameter in tf.keras.layers.
Conv2D specifies the arrangement of input dimensions. Two modes are
available: channels_last and channels_first. If not explicitly
defined, the default is channels_last. In TensorFlow, the input to
tf.keras. layers.Conv2D is a four-dimensional tensor representing

--- Strana 67 ---
batch size, height, width, and channels. For channels_last, the data format
is (batch size, height, width, channels), whereas for channels_first, the
format is (batch size, channels, height, width).
group: In tf.keras.layers.Conv2D, the group parameter specifies
whether to perform grouped convolution and determines the number of groups.
A group value of 1 indicates no grouped convolution, while higher values
divide the convolution operation into the specified number of groups. For
example, setting group = 2 splits the convolution into two groups. By default,
group is set to 1 if not explicitly specified.
activation: The activation parameter specifies whether an activation function is
applied to process the output of a convolution operation. Setting it to "None"
indicates that no activation function will be used. TensorFlow provides several
predefined activation functions, including "deserialize", "elu",
"exponential", "gelu", "get", "hard_sigmoid", "hard_silu",
"hard_swish", "leaky_relu", "linear", "log_softmax",
"mish", "relu", "relu6", "selu", "serialize", "sigmoid",
"silu", "softmax", "soft- plus", "softsign", "swish", and
"tanh". The desired activation function can be specified in the format
activation="relu".
use_bias: The use_bias parameter is of type bool. When set to True, it adds a
bias term to the data generated by the convolution. If use_bias=True and
the activation parameter is not None, the bias is added first, followed by
processing through the specified activation function.
The parameters kernel_initializer and bias_initializer specify
how the initial kernel weights and biases are generated. Supported initialization
methods are introduced in the Table 2.1
Table 2.1 Descriptions of common Keras weight initializers
Initializer
Description
TruncatedNormal Samples from a normal distribution, but values more than
 
two standard deviations from the mean are redrawn. Avoids extreme weights
Zeros
All weights initialized to zero. Not suitable for weights due to symmetry
Ones
All weights initialized to one. Sometimes used for bias initialization
GlorotNormal
Xavier normal initializer: normal distribution scaled
 
by the number of input and output units (fan-in, fan-out)
GlorotUniform
Xavier uniform initializer: same scaling principle as GlorotNormal,
 
but uses a uniform distribution

--- Strana 68 ---
Initializer
Description
HeNormal
Kaiming normal initializer: scales variance by fan-in.
 
Suitable for ReLU and variants
HeUniform
Uniform version of He initializer
Orthogonal
Initializes weights with orthogonal matrices. Preserves variance
Constant
Sets all weights to a user-specified constant value
VarianceScaling General-purpose initializer. Scaling can adapt to fan-in, fan-out,
 
and mode. Glorot, He, and Lecun are special cases
LecunNormal
Scaled normal distribution based on fan-in. For selu activation
LecunUniform
Uniform variant of LecunNormal
Identity
Initializes square weight matrices as identity. For recurrent layers
Additionally, user-defined initialization methods are supported.
The parameters kernel_regularizer, bias_regularizer, and
activity_ regularizer allow for the addition of regularization terms to the
kernel weights, biases, and outputs. Predefined regularization methods include:
User-defined regularization methods can also be applied. By the way, the
default mode of the regularization is none, which means the regularization is not
applied.
The parameters bias_constraint and kernel_constraint specify
the conditions to be applied to the trained bias and kernel weights during
optimization. The available constraint functions are introduced in Table 2.2.
Table 2.2 Descriptions of common Keras regularizers
Regularizer
Description
L1
Adds a penalty equal to the absolute value of the weights (λ ∑|w|)
 
Encourages sparsity and feature selection
L2
Adds a penalty proportional to the square of the weights (λ ∑w2)
 
Encourages smaller weights and reduces overfitting
L1L2
Combines both L1 and L2 penalties
 
Offers a balance between sparsity and weight decay
OrthogonalRegularizer Penalizes deviation from orthogonality between weight vectors
 
Useful for constraining weight matrices in some specialized models
The default mode is none, which means the constraint is not applied.
We have already provided an example of setting
tf.keras.layers.Conv2D with none of the arguments set to default mode,

--- Strana 69 ---
in the GitHub repository.2 tf.keras.layers.DepthwiseConv2D shares
most of its parameters with tf. keras.layers.Conv2D. The only differing
parameter is depth_multiplier, which is an integer that specifies the number
of output channels generated for each input channel in depthwise convolution.
2.3.1.2 Batch Normalization Layers
Now that we have covered some basic functions and settings of convolutional
layers, we will now look at functions related to normalization layers. In
TensorFlow, the related layer is called
tf.keras.layers.BatchNormalization. The full and default version is
shown in Fig. 2.3.
Fig. 2.3 BatchNormalization layer configuration
Many parameters in this layer are consistent with the settings of convolutional
layers, including regularization and initialization configurations. Therefore, this
section will focus on the parameters that are unique to this layer and are not
included in the convolutional layer settings. These parameters are axis,
momentum, epsilon, center, scale, and synchronized.
Synchronized: This parameter is only applicable when using the TensorFlow
backend. If set to True, it synchronizes the global batch statistics (mean and
variance) across all devices during each training step in a distributed training
strategy. If set to False (default), each replica uses its local batch statistics.

--- Strana 70 ---
Axis: An integer specifying the axis to be normalized, typically the feature axis.
For example, after a Conv2D layer with
data_format="channels_first," the value of axis should be set to
1. The default value is axis=-1.
Momentum: Defines the momentum for the moving average used to compute
the mean and variance. The default value is momentum=0.99.
Epsilon: A small float value added to the variance to prevent division by zero
during normalization. The default value is epsilon=0.001.
Center: If set to True (default), the layer adds an offset (β) to the normalized
tensor. If set to False, β is ignored.
Scale: If set to True (default), the normalized tensor is multiplied by a scale
factor (γ). If set to False, γ is not used. This can be disabled when the
subsequent layer is linear, as scaling will be handled by that layer.
We have already provided an example of setting tf.keras.layers.
BatchNormalization with none of the arguments set to default mode, in the GitHub
repository.3
2.3.1.3 Activation Layers
Next, we will introduce some commonly used activation layers, including ReLU
and Softmax. These two kinds of activation layers are called
tf.keras.layers.ReLU and tf.keras.layers.Softmax. The full and
default versions of ReLU and Softmax are shown in Figs. 2.4 and 2.5.
Fig. 2.4 ReLU activation layer configuration
Fig. 2.5 Softmax activation layer configuration

--- Strana 71 ---
The parameters in tf.keras.layers.ReLU include max_value,
threshold, and negative_slope, which are explained as follows:
max_value: A float value (≥0) that specifies the maximum activation value.
If set to None, the activation value is unlimited. Setting this parameter can
achieve certain variants of ReLU, such as ReLU6, which helps to limit
excessively large values in neural networks. The default value is None.
negative_slope: A float value (≥0) that defines the negative slope
coefficient. The default value is 0.0. Configuring this parameter enables
variants of ReLU, such as Leaky ReLU, which helps address the “dying
neuron” problem by allowing small gradients for negative inputs.
threshold: A float value (≥0) representing the threshold for threshold
activation. Inputs below this threshold are either set to zero or multiplied by the
negative_slope coefficient. The default value is 0.0.
For the tf.keras.layers.Softmax function, it has only one input
parameter, axis. This parameter can be an integer or a list of integers, specifying
the axis (or axes) along which the softmax normalization is applied.
2.3.1.4 Pooling Layers
Next, we will introduce functions related to pooling layers. Specifically, we will
discuss the functions for four commonly used pooling layers: average pooling
layer, max pooling layer, global average pooling layer, and global max pooling
layer. These functions are named tf.keras.layers.AveragePooling2D,
tf.keras.layers.MaxPool2D, tf.keras.layers. GlobalAveragePooling2D, and
tf.keras.layers. GlobalMaxPool2D, respectively. Their default configurations and
full parameter presentation are shown in Figs. 2.6, 2.7, 2.8, and 2.9.
Fig. 2.6 AveragePooling2D layer configuration

--- Strana 72 ---
Fig. 2.7 MaxPool2D layer configuration
Fig. 2.8 GlobalAveragePooling2D layer configuration
Fig. 2.9 GlobalMaxPool2D layer configuration
For tf.keras.layers.AveragePooling2D and
tf.keras.layers.MaxPool 2D, the parameters padding,
data_format, and strides have the same configuration methods and data
types as the parameters with the same names in tf.keras.layers.Conv2D.
Similarly, the parameter pool_size is identical to kernel_size in its
functionality. The pool_size specifies the size of the filter or kernel used in the
pooling operation.
Additionally, a new parameter, name, is introduced. This parameter allows the
user to assign a specific name to the pooling layer by setting its value. The un-
default mode of these two-layer functions is represented on GitHub.
For tf.keras.layers.GlobalAveragePooling2D and
tf.keras.layers. GlobalMaxPool2D, a new parameter, keepdims, is

--- Strana 73 ---
introduced. This parameter is a boolean value that controls whether the height and
width dimensions of the output are retained:
If keepdims=True, the output shape retains the height and width dimensions
with a size of 1. The output shape will be (batch_size, 1, 1, channels).
If keepdims=False (default), the height and width dimensions are
compressed, and the output shape will be (batch_size, channels).
2.3.1.5 Fully Connected Layer
In TensorFlow and Keras, fully connected layers are commonly referred to as
dense layers, represented by the class tf.keras.layers.Dense. The default
configuration is shown in Fig. 2.10.
Fig. 2.10 Dense layer configuration
For tf.keras.layers.Dense, the following parameters share the same
data type and configuration methods as in tf.keras.layers.Conv2D:
activation
use_bias
kernel_initializer
bias_initializer
kernel_regularizer
bias_regularizer
activity_regularizer
kernel_constraint
bias_constraint

--- Strana 74 ---
In addition, two new parameters are introduced:
units: Specifies the dimensionality of the output space (i.e., the number of
neurons). This parameter determines the size of the output tensor. For example,
setting units=128 indicates that the layer will output a tensor of size 128.
lora_rank: A parameter used for low-rank decomposition (e.g., in LoRA
techniques) for efficient training of large models. Type: int or None. Default:
None. When set to a value other than None, low-rank decomposition is used to
optimize weight updates, reducing the number of parameters and computational
overhead.
An example of a non-default configuration for this layer is shown on GitHub.
2.3.2 Network Training and Evaluation
In the previous sections, we introduced how common layers in CNN networks are
defined in TensorFlow. Next, we will demonstrate how to construct, train, and
evaluate a custom network using the layer functions discussed above. Specifically,
we will use MobileNet as a foundation to illustrate the process of training a CNN
network, including both transfer learning and training from scratch.
2.3.2.1 Transfer Learning
Transfer learning refers to fine-tuning a pretrained model to quickly obtain a
model that meets specific objectives. TensorFlow provides a wide range of
pretrained models, offering an excellent foundation for implementing transfer
learning. Next, we will use a pretrained model provided by TensorFlow to train
our model on the CIFAR-10 dataset [6].
A. Load CIFAR-10 Dataset
The CIFAR-10 dataset consists of 60,000 32 × 32 RGB images divided into ten
classes. We use tf.keras.datasets.cifar10.load_data() to load the
dataset, splitting it into 50,000 training samples and 10,000 test samples. This step
is shown in Fig. 2.11.
Fig. 2.11 Load CIFAR-10 dataset
B. Data Preprocessing
Before retraining the network, the loaded data should be preprocessed. To prepare
the data for MobileNet:

--- Strana 75 ---
The images are normalized using preprocess_input from
tensorflow.keras.applications.mobilenet. The input pixels
will be scaled between −1 and 1.
The labels are converted into a binary class matrix format using
tf.keras.utils.to_categorical.
The detailed code is shown in Fig. 2.12.
Fig. 2.12 Data preprocessing
C. Load Pretrained MobileNet
Next, we need to load the pretrained MobileNet model. For loading the MobileNet
model pretrained on ImageNet, we need to use:
weights=’imagenet’: Load weights trained on the ImageNet dataset.
include_top=False: Exclude the top classification layers.
input_shape=(32, 32, 3): Match the input shape of CIFAR-10 images.
The base model’s weights are frozen to retain the learned features during training,
which means the weights of the convolutional layer will not be updated. The code
of this part is shown in Fig. 2.13.
Fig. 2.13 Load pretrained MobileNet
D. Build the Custom Model
Based on the MobileNet base, we need to add a global average pooling layer and
fully connected layers to fit the CIFAR-10 dataset:
A GlobalAveragePooling2D layer to reduce spatial dimensions.

--- Strana 76 ---
A dense layer with 128 units and ReLU activation for high-level feature
learning.
An output layer with 10 units and Softmax activation for classification.
The detailed code is shown in Fig. 2.14.
Fig. 2.14 Build the custom model
E. Compile the Model
After completing the above operations, the network’s basic structure is
established. Next, the model is compiled using the Adam optimizer, categorical
cross-entropy loss, and accuracy as the evaluation metric. The code is shown in
Fig. 2.15.
Fig. 2.15 Compile the model
F. Train the Model
After compilation, the model can be trained. The configuration of the training is:
ten epochs with a batch size of 64. Reserve 20% of the training data for validation.
The code is shown in Fig. 2.16.

Fig. 2.18 Make predictions
2.3.2.2 Training from Scratch

2.4 Hardware-Level CNN Model Implementation on
System Verilog
In this section, we will provide a detailed tutorial to guide new CNN hardware
developers in quickly developing their first CNN hardware accelerator. This article
will show how to use System Verilog to build a customized small CNN hardware
accelerator digital logic circuit.
This section will introduce the preparation step and divide a CNN hardware
accelerator into smaller modules. These modules include the data reading module,
the convolution layer module, the pooling layer module, the fully connected layer

--- Strana 82 ---
module, the activation layer module, and the classification module. The overview
structure of the following CNN hardware accelerator is shown in Fig. 2.23.
Fig. 2.23 The structure of a sample CNN hardware accelerator
2.4.1 Preparation
The preparation step can be regarded as a fixed-point quantization process, as
illustrated in Fig. 2.24. This process is applied to all data utilized within the CNN
hardware accelerator. The overall workflow of dynamic fixed-point quantization
during CNN inference is as follows: a set of test images is fed into the CNN model
to perform inference, during which the output feature maps of each layer are
captured. For every layer, the maximum absolute value is calculated across all
feature maps, weights, and biases. The integer bit width is then determined based
on the number of bits required to represent the integer part of this maximum value
(including the sign bit), while the remaining bits, determined by the total bit width
supported by the hardware, are allocated to the fractional part. Using this fixed-
point format, all weights and biases are quantized and exported in binary form for

--- Strana 83 ---
deployment on hardware. Additionally, the pixel data to be fed into the CNN
hardware accelerator must also be converted into this fixed-point binary format. It
is important to note that the pixel data should be stored in memory in a column-
by-column sequence.
Fig. 2.24 Preparation step: Fixed-point quantization
2.4.2 Data Reading Module
The data reading module can be divided into three parts: the address extraction
module, the image read module(image buffer), and the weight extraction module.
2.4.2.1 Address Extraction Module
Figure 2.25 presents the pseudocode for the address extraction module, which
determines the required data addresses for input images in subsequent

--- Strana 84 ---
computations. To facilitate efficient fusion of adjacent layers, the address
extraction sequence is carefully designed such that each time a block of
conv1_kernel_size × conv1_kernel_size pixels is extracted, the first
convolutional layer produces one output feature. Likewise, whenever the first
convolutional layer generates a block of
pool1_kernel_size × pool1_kernel_size features, the first pooling layer yields
one pooled feature. This pattern continues: each time the first pooling layer
outputs a conv2_kernel_size × conv2_kernel_size block, the second
convolutional layer computes one feature; and for every
pool2_kernel_size × pool2_kernel_size features generated by the second
convolutional layer, the second pooling layer produces a single output.

--- Strana 85 ---
Fig. 2.25 Module: Address extract

--- Strana 86 ---
To achieve this, we use the indices of the output feature map produced by the
second pooling layer as the basis to back-calculate the pixel addresses needed for
extraction. The pseudocode in Fig. 2.25 details this operation. The main
parameters include the input image width (image_size), the kernel sizes for
both convolution layers (kernel_size1, kernel_size2), and the pooling
window sizes (pool_size1, pool_size2). The variables row_out2 and
col_out2 represent the indices of the output feature map after the second
pooling layer, while other indices (i, j, i_p, j_p, i_c, j_c, i_p2, j_p2)
enumerate positions within their respective kernel or pooling windows.
The address for each pixel is then calculated using a linear indexing formula,
as shown in Eq. 2.1, which uniquely maps the multidimensional loop variables to
a one-dimensional address within the flattened image or feature map:
(2.1)
This hierarchical and parameterized address generation scheme enables
support for varied kernel and pooling sizes without modification. Such an
approach is particularly advantageous for pipelined processing in CNN hardware
accelerators.
2.4.2.2 Image Read Module
The calculated address from Fig. 2.25 is provided to the image read Module,
which retrieves the corresponding pixel data from memory. In addition to
supplying data for subsequent computations, the image read module also serves as
a buffer for storing input pixel data from external sources. The module stores
incoming pixel data into the buffer when the input flag store is set. Conversely,
when store is 0, read is set, and the internal counter reaches
image_size × image_size, the module fetches pixel data based on the input
address and supplies it to downstream processing. The detailed working principle
of this module is shown in Fig. 2.26
J = j + (jp−1) + pool_size1 ×[(jc−1) + (jp2−1)+
pool_size2 × (col_out2−1)]
I = i + (ip−1) + pool_size1 ×[(ic−1) + (ip2−1)+
pool_size2 × (row_out2−1)]
address = J + image_size ⋅(I−1)

--- Strana 87 ---
Fig. 2.26 Module: Image read
2.4.2.3 Extracting Weight
1. Module Definition of Kernel_Conv
The kernel_conv1 module, as shown in Fig. 2.27, is responsible for managing
and outputting the weights and biases required by the first convolutional layer of
the accelerator. Upon initialization or reset, the module loads all kernel weights
and bias values for each output channel from external files into internal buffers.

--- Strana 88 ---
Fig. 2.27 Module: Buffer of weight for the first convolutional layer

--- Strana 89 ---
During operation, when the start signal is set, the module sequentially
outputs the kernel weights for each output channel. It uses an internal counter to
iterate through all weight values associated with each kernel. For each clock cycle
with start active, the counter increments, and the corresponding weight value
for each output channel is provided at the output. If the counter reaches the
maximum kernel size (kernel_size1 × kernel_size1), it wraps around
and restarts from the first weight value. The associated bias for each output
channel is also provided at the output in parallel with the weights.
If either the module is not in the active state (start unset) or a reset is
triggered (n_reset is low), the output weights and biases are cleared to zero,
and the internal counter is reset. This ensures that weight streaming only occurs
during the appropriate active periods and is robust to resets. In addition, the
kernel_conv2 module, as shown in Fig. 2.28, is responsible for managing and
streaming the weights and biases required by the second convolutional layer in the
accelerator. At initialization or resetting, the module loads all kernel weights and
bias values for each output channel from external files into internal buffers. The
kernel weights are organized such that each output channel contains a three-
dimensional weight tensor covering all input channels and all spatial positions
within the kernel.

--- Strana 90 ---
Fig. 2.28 Module: Buffer of weight for the second convolutional layer

--- Strana 91 ---
During normal operation, when both the start and ready signals are set,
the module sequentially outputs the kernel weights for every combination of
output and input channels. An internal counter tracks the current position within
the kernel window for each channel pair. On each clock cycle with start and
ready active, the counter increments and extracts the weights from the internal
buffers based on the counter value. If the counter reaches the kernel window size
(kernel_size2 × kernel_size2), it wraps around and begins a new cycle.
The corresponding bias value for each output channel is provided in parallel with
the weights.
If a reset (n_reset) occurs, the internal counter is reset, and all output
weights are cleared to zero, ensuring a well-defined initial state. If start or
ready is not set, the module maintains its current state, and the output weights
remain at zero.
2. Module Definition of WEIGHT_FULL
The weight_ful1 module, as shown in Fig. 2.29, is responsible for providing
the weights to the current computation in the first FC layer. All weights are
organized as a one-dimensional array, which is initialized by loading data from an
external file (such as weight_ful.txt). The module receives as inputs the
index of the current computation-related output neuron (count_ful) and the
index of the current computation-related input neuron (count_finish). The
main configuration parameters include the number of input channels
(input_channels), the number of input neurons per channel
(input_neurons), the total number of output neurons (output_neurons),
and the total number of weights (total_weights
= input_channels × input_neurons × output_neurons).

--- Strana 92 ---
Fig. 2.29 Module: Buffer of weight for the first fully connected layer
During operation, if count_ful = 0, the output weights for all input channels
(weight_ful1) are set to zero. Otherwise, for each input channel ch, the
module calculates a base address using.
and then computes the full address as
The output weight for channel ch is then given by
base = total_weights −(ch−1) × input_neurons × output_neurons
address = base −(count_finish−1) × output_neurons −count_ful

--- Strana 93 ---
The weight_ful2 module, as shown in Fig. 2.30, is responsible for
providing the appropriate weights for the calculation in the second fully connected
layer. All weights are stored in a one-dimensional array, which is initialized by
loading data from an external file (such as weight_ful2.txt). During
operation, the module receives the indices of the current input neuron (count_2)
and output neuron (count_1), as well as an enable flag (flag_ful2). When
the enable flag is not set, the output weight is set to zero. When the enable flag is
set, the module computes the corresponding address in the flattened weight array
according to the formula
address = total_weights −count_1 −output_neurons × (count_2−1)
,where total_weights = input_neurons × output_neurons. The
corresponding value from the weight array is then provided at the output.
weight_ful1[ch] = weight[address]

--- Strana 94 ---
Fig. 2.30 Module: Buffer of weight for the second fully connected layer
2.4.3 Convolution Layer Module
This part will introduce the basic element of the convolution layer, the
convolutional processing element, and how to combine multiple convolution
processing elements to build a convolutional layer module.
2.4.3.1 Convolution Processing Element
The conv_pe module, as shown in Fig. 2.31, implements a multichannel
convolution PE that computes the output feature value for one output channel. The
main parameters are the kernel size (kernel_size) and the number of input
channels (input_channel).

--- Strana 95 ---
Fig. 2.31 Module: Convolutional processing element

--- Strana 96 ---
Inputs to the module include the current input feature values for all channels
(input_featuremap [input_channel]), the corresponding weights
(weight [input_channel]), a bias term (bias), the active-low reset and
clock signals (n_reset, clk), a start control signal (start), and a handshake
signal from the pooling layer (ready_pool) indicating that valid input data is
available.
Internally, the module uses an accumulator (sum) to store the partial
convolution result and a counter (counter) to track the number of multiply-
accumulate operations performed. On each clock cycle, if reset is triggered, the
accumulator and counter are cleared. When computation starts (start is high), if
the counter reaches the kernel size, the module resets the counter and accumulator
to begin a new computation window. Otherwise, when ready is set, the counter
is incremented and the accumulator is updated as follows:
If ready is not set, both the counter and accumulator hold their current values.
The combinational output logic assigns the completion flag (flag) to 1 and
outputs the computed feature (output_featuremap) when the counter reaches
the kernel size, where the output is given by the accumulated sum plus bias:
Otherwise, the flag is not set, and the output is set to zero.
2.4.3.2 Convolution Layer
The conv_layer module, as shown in Fig. 2.32, implements a parallel
convolution computation for a multichannel input using multiple PEs. It receives
as inputs the current input feature value (input_feature), a set of weights and
biases for each output channel (weight[num_channels] and
bias[num_channels]), clock and reset signals (clk, n_reset), a start
control signal (start), and a handshake input (ready_in) from the preceding
image read module to indicate data availability.
sum = sum +∑
input_channel
i=1
input_featuremap[i] × weight[i]
output_featuremap = sum + bias

--- Strana 97 ---
Fig. 2.32 Module: First convolutional layer

--- Strana 98 ---
The number of output channels is given by the parameter num_channels,
and each kernel is assumed to be square with size kernel_size.
Internally, the module instantiates num_channels convolution PEs
(conv_pe), each responsible for computing the convolution operation for one
output channel. For each channel ch (1 ≤ch ≤num_channels), the PE is
provided with the current input feature, the corresponding weight and bias, and
control signals, and produces an output feature value (feature[ch]) along with
a done flag (flag[ch]).
After all PEs have finished their computations (i.e., all flag[ch] are set and
every PEs generates a new feature data), the module outputs the results from each
PE on output_feature[ch] and sets the ready signal (ready) to high,
indicating that valid output data is available. If only a subset of flag[ch]
signals are set, this indicates an error condition, and the module outputs an error
message and sets ready to low. If none of the PEs have finished, the outputs are
held at zero, and ready remains low.
The conv_layer2 module, as shown in Fig. 2.33, implements a parallel
multichannel convolution operation for a set of input feature maps. The inputs
include the current input feature map values
(input_feature[in_channels]), a flattened array of weights for all
convolution kernels (weight[kernel_num*in_ channels]), and bias
values for each output channel (bias[kernel_num]). The module is
synchronized by clock and reset signals (clk, n_reset), a start signal (start),
and a handshake signal from the preceding pooling layer (ready_pool) to
indicate when the input data is ready for processing.

--- Strana 99 ---
Fig. 2.33 Module: Second convolutional layer

--- Strana 100 ---
The main parameters are the number of input channels (in_channels), the
number of output channels or kernels (kernel_num), and the size of each
convolution kernel (kernel_size, assumed square).
Internally, the module instantiates kernel_num convolution processing
elements (conv_pe), each receiving its slice of the weights and the
corresponding bias. For kernel k (1 ≤k ≤kernel_num), the PE is connected to
the complete set of input channels, receives the relevant weights (weight
indexed for kernel k), and produces an output feature value (feature[k]) along
with a completion flag (flag[k]).
When all completion flags (flag[k]) are set, the module outputs the results
from all PEs on output_feature[k] and sets the ready flag (ready) to
indicate valid output data. If only a subset of completion flags are set, this
indicates an error, and the module sets ready low and displays a runtime error
message. If none of the PEs have completed, all output features are held at zero,
and ready remains low.
2.4.4 Pooling Layer Module
This part will introduce the basic element of the MaxPooling layer, the
MaxPooling processing element, and how to combine multiple MaxPooling
processing elements to build a MaxPooling layer module.
2.4.4.1 MaxPooling Processing Element
The pool_pe module, as shown in Fig. 2.34, implements a processing element
for max pooling over a fixed-size window. The main parameter is the pooling
window size (pool_size).

--- Strana 101 ---
Fig. 2.34 Module: Max pooling processing element

--- Strana 102 ---
Inputs to the module include the input value for the current window position
(input_featuremap), clock and active-low reset signals (clk, n_reset), a
start signal (start) to initiate pooling, and an enable signal (enable) to
advance the pooling operation.
Internally, the module maintains a register (max_value) that tracks the
maximum value in the current pooling window and a counter (counter) to track
the number of values processed. If reset is triggered, both registers are cleared.
During operation, if start is high and the counter equals the pooling window
size, the module resets the counter and initializes max_value to the current input
value (starting a new window). Otherwise, when enable is high, the counter is
incremented. The module compares the input value to max_value, updating it if
the new value is larger.
The combinational output logic assigns the completion flag (flag) to high
and outputs the maximum value (output_featuremap) when the pooling
window is complete (i.e., counter equals pool_size). Otherwise, the flag is
low, and the output is set to zero.
2.4.4.2 MaxPooling Layer
The pool_layer module, as shown in Fig. 2.35, implements a parallel pooling
operation over a multichannel input feature map. It receives as inputs the current
feature values from the previous layer (input_feature[num_channels]),
clock and active-low reset signals (clk, n_reset), a start signal (start) to
initiate pooling, and an enable signal (ready) indicating that input data is
available.

--- Strana 103 ---
Fig. 2.35 Module: Max pooling layer
The module parameter num_channels defines the number of channels
processed in parallel. Internally, the module instantiates num_channels pooling

--- Strana 104 ---
processing elements (pool_pe), each operating independently on its
corresponding input channel. For each channel ch (1 ≤ch ≤num_channels),
the PE receives the relevant feature map value, clock and reset, and control signals
and produces an output feature (feature[ch]) and a completion flag
(flag[ch]).
The combinational output logic monitors the completion flags. When all
flag[ch] signals are set, indicating that all pooling elements have finished
processing, the module outputs the results from all PEs on output_feature[ch] and
sets ready_pool to high to indicate valid output data. If only a subset of the
completion flags are high, this represents an error state; in this case, the module
outputs an error message, the output feature is undefined, and ready_pool is set
low. If none of the PEs have completed, all output features are held at zero, and
ready_pool remains low.
2.4.5 Activation Layer Module
This part will introduce the basic element of the ReLU6 layer, how the ReLU6
processing element works, and how to combine multiple ReLU6 processing
elements to build a ReLU6 layer module.
2.4.5.1 ReLU6 Processing Element
The Relu6_pe module, as shown in Fig. 2.36, implements the ReLU6 activation
function for a given input. The main parameter is the upper bound for the ReLU6
activation (relu6_upper_bound), which represents the value 6.0 in the
system’s fixed-point data format.

--- Strana 105 ---
Fig. 2.36 Module: ReLU6 processing element
Inputs include the value to be activated (input_featuremap), clock and
active-low reset signals (clk, n_reset), a start control signal (start), and an
enable signal (enable).
On each clock edge or failing edge of n_reset, the following logic is
executed. If reset is triggered (n_reset is low), the output
(output_featuremap) and the output flag (flag) are cleared. If the start
signal is not high, both outputs remain cleared. Otherwise, the flag is set according
to the value of enable, and the module computes the ReLU6 activation as
follows: if input_featuremap is positive and less than
relu6_upper_bound, the output is set to the input value. If the input exceeds
the upper bound, the output is saturated to relu6_upper_bound. If the input is
negative or zero, the output is set to zero.

--- Strana 106 ---
2.4.5.2 ReLU6 Layer
The Relu6_layer module, as shown in Fig. 2.37, applies the ReLU6 activation
function in parallel to each channel of an input feature map. The inputs include the
feature values for all channels (input_feature[num_channels]), clock
and active-low reset signals (clk, n_reset), a start signal (start) to initiate
activation computation, and an enable signal (ready) indicating data availability.

--- Strana 107 ---
Fig. 2.37 Module: ReLU6 layer

--- Strana 108 ---
The number of channels is specified by the parameter num_channels,
which determines how many activation processing elements (PEs) are instantiated.
Each channel has a corresponding instance of the Relu6_pe module. For each
channel ch (1 ≤ch ≤num_channels), the PE processes its respective input,
providing an activated output (feature[ch]) and a completion flag
(flag[ch]).
The combinational output logic checks all completion flags. If all flag[ch]
signals are high, indicating that all activation PEs have finished computation, the
module outputs the activated values on output_feature[ch] and asserts the
ready_activation signal. If only some flags are high, this indicates an error
condition; the module outputs an error message, sets the output to an undefined
state, and deasserts ready_activation. If none of the PEs have finished, the
outputs are held at zero, and ready_activation remains low.
2.4.6 Fully Connected Layer Module
This part will introduce the basic elements of the fully connected layer, the fully
connected processing element, and how to combine multiple fully connected
processing elements to build a fully connected layer module.
2.4.6.1 Fully Connected Processing Element
The full_pe module, as shown in Fig. 2.38, implements a single multiply-and-
accumulate (MAC) unit for a FC layer, typically used in neural network
accelerators. It receives as inputs the value of the current input neuron
(input_featuremap) and the corresponding weight (weight), both
represented in fixed-point format and clock (clk), active-low reset (n_reset),
and a start signal (start) to control operation.

--- Strana 109 ---
Fig. 2.38 Module: Fully connected processing element
On each rising edge of the clock or falling edge of n_reset, the main logic
operates as follows: if reset is active (n_reset is low), the internal multiplication
register (mul) is cleared. If the start signal is not high, mul remains zero.
Otherwise, the module computes the product of the input feature and weight and
stores it in mul.
The output (output_featuremap) is generated by truncating or rounding
the multiplication result to match the fixed-point hardware design requirements.
2.4.6.2 Fully Connected Layer
The full_layer module, as shown in Figs. 2.39 and 2.40, implements a
parallel FC layer. In each clock cycle, it receives a vector of input neurons from
the previous layer (input_feature[in_channels]) and their
corresponding weights (weight_ful1[in_channels]). The parameter
in_channels determines the number of input neurons processed in parallel.

--- Strana 110 ---
The module computes and accumulates contributions to every output neuron
(output_feature[out_neurons]) across all input batches.

--- Strana 111 ---
Fig. 2.39 Module: First fully connected layer part 1
Fig. 2.40 Module: First fully connected layer part 2

--- Strana 112 ---
For each batch, the module instantiates multiple parallel processing elements
(full_pe), each calculating the product of an input and its corresponding
weight. The outputs from these units are summed and accumulated into registers
(neuron_hidden) for each output neuron, utilizing the property of FC layers, where
input neurons contribute to every output neuron through linear superposition. The
accumulation is managed by internal counters (count, count2) that iterate over
output neurons and input batches, respectively.
The finish signal is used to reset accumulators and counters when all FC
computations for the current inference are complete. As new input data arrives
(ready is high), the counters work and accumulation proceeds. When all inputs
and outputs have been processed, the module asserts the output valid flag
(ready_ful1) and outputs the final results for all neurons as the sum of the
accumulated value and its corresponding bias.
In summary, the module’s core logic is that each group of input features is
processed in parallel; their weighted sums are accumulated into all output neurons.
Once the computation is complete, each neuron’s result is finalized by adding a
bias.
The full_layer2 module, as shown in Figs. 2.41 and 2.42, implements a
parameterized fully connected (FC) output layer, where each output neuron
accumulates weighted contributions from all input neurons. The input feature
vector (input_feature [IN_SIZE]) is first latched into registers
(hidden_neurons [IN_SIZE]). The output neurons (output_neurons
[OUT_SIZE]) each have an accumulator to sum the results over all input
neurons, and the per-neuron bias (bias[OUT_SIZE]) is loaded from file.

--- Strana 113 ---
Fig. 2.41 Module: Second fully connected layer part 1

--- Strana 114 ---
Fig. 2.42 Module: Second fully connected layer part 2
The module uses a single processing element (full_pe) to compute the
product of the current input neuron and its corresponding weight

--- Strana 115 ---
(weight_ful2), providing the result (feature1). Two counters, count_1
and count_2, are used to iterate through output neurons and input neurons,
respectively. Each time flag is high, the output neuron index (count_1_1) is
incremented, and the current product is accumulated into the corresponding output
neuron accumulator.
The sequential logic cycles through each output neuron for every input neuron,
accumulating their weighted sums. When all output neurons have been updated for
a given input, the module advances to the next input neuron. When all input and
output neurons have been processed (count_1 == OUT_SIZE && count_2
== IN_SIZE), the finish flag is set, and the final outputs are computed by
adding bias to each output neuron accumulator.
The outputs flag_ful2, count1, and count2 provide control/status for
external interfacing and weight management modules.
2.4.6.3 Classification Layer Module
The classify module, as shown in Fig. 2.43, implements the final decision
logic for a neural network classifier. It receives as input a vector of scores
(input_feature[num_classes]) for each category (e.g., ten classes for
digit recognition), along with clock (clk), input ready (ready), and active-low
reset (n_reset) signals.

--- Strana 116 ---
Fig. 2.43 Module: Classify
Internally, the module performs a maximum search to determine the index of
the class with the highest score. The combinational logic scans through all input
scores, keeping track of the maximum value found. Sequential logic then updates
the output registers on each clock edge or reset: if reset is triggered or input is not
ready, the outputs (categories, one_end) are cleared. When valid input is

--- Strana 117 ---
present (ready is high), the module sets the one_end flag and outputs the index
(categories) of the class with the maximum score.
This module is typically used at the end of the neural network computation to
convert raw class scores into a final classification result.
2.4.7 Top-Level Entity
The gdp_net module, as shown in Figs. 2.44, 2.45, and 2.46, describes the top-
level architecture of a complete pipelined neural network accelerator, structured as
a sequence of parameterizable hardware modules. The main input signals include
the global system start (start), clock (clk), active-low reset (n_reset), an
input pixel data stream (input_data), and a store flag for data buffering.

--- Strana 118 ---
Fig. 2.44 Module: Top-level entity of accelerator part 1

--- Strana 119 ---
Fig. 2.45 Module: Top-level entity of accelerator part 2

--- Strana 120 ---
Fig. 2.46 Module: Top-level entity of accelerator part 3
The inference datapath is organized as follows: pixel addresses are generated
using an address_extract module; input data is buffered and synchronized
by image_read. Two sets of kernel and bias loaders (kernel_conv1,
kernel_conv2) initialize convolutional layer weights. Each convolutional layer
(conv_layer, conv_layer2) is followed by an activation layer
(activation_layer) and a pooling layer (pool_layer), maintaining the
typical convolution-activation-pooling sequence.
The output of the second pooling layer is forwarded to a fully connected layer
(full_layer1), with weights supplied by weight_ful1. This is followed by
a nonlinearity (activation_layer_full) and a final fully connected layer
(full_layer2) with weights from weight_ful2. The final classification
result is produced by an argmax classifier (classify), which outputs the
predicted category and a one-shot end flag (one_end).

--- Strana 121 ---
Handshaking signals and ready flags ensure strict pipeline synchronization:
each module waits for input-valid flags from the previous stage before beginning
computation, enforcing a robust, data-driven hardware pipeline. All weight and
bias memories are initialized from external files, enabling flexible deployment for
various network architectures. Key structural parameters such as kernel and
pooling sizes are fully configurable.
2.4.8 Testbench Design
The testbench, as shown in Fig. 2.47, for the gdp_net module verifies system-
level functionality using a series of test images and expected category labels. The
key parameters are the image size (image_size), the total number of test cases
(num_tests), a 2D array of test images (input_image [num_tests]
[image_size* image_size]), and the expected classification result for
each image (expected_ category [num_tests]).

--- Strana 122 ---
Fig. 2.47 Module: Testbench design
For each test case, the testbench logic is as follows:

--- Strana 123 ---
1. System reset is applied by deasserting n_reset, followed by several clock
cycles to ensure all internal states are cleared. The start and store signals
are initialized appropriately.
 
2. The test image is loaded into the system pixel by pixel: each clock cycle, a
new pixel from input_image is presented on the input data line while
store is high.
 
3. Once the entire image is loaded, store is low and start is high to trigger
inference.
 
4. The testbench then waits until the one_end output from the DUT indicates
that classification is complete.
 
5. The predicted category (categories) is compared to the expected value
(expected_category) for the current test case. Pass/fail status is reported
for each case, and an error counter is incremented if any failures occur.
 
6. The process repeats for all test cases. At the end of testing, the testbench prints
a summary of the total number of tests, passes, and failures.
 
2.5 Conclusions
In this chapter, we presented a comprehensive approach to training CNNs at the
software level using TensorFlow, as well as designing a simple neural network
using System Verilog for hardware implementation. By integrating these
methodologies, we aim to provide an accessible framework for researchers and
developers to train neural networks and deploy them on FPGAs for performance
analysis. This chapter serves as a foundational resource, supporting software-
based neural network development and hardware-oriented implementation,
thereby facilitating further exploration and innovation in FPGA-based deep
learning acceleration.

3. Performance Optimization of CNN Hardware
Peiyao Sun1   , Haosen Yu1   , Tom J. Kazmierski1    and Basel Halak1  
School of Electronics and Computer Science, University of Southampton,

Keywords Performance optimization – Approximate multipliers – Error injection – Error

3.1 Introduction
Approximate computing (AC) has become an increasingly attractive approach for enhancing
the hardware efficiency of CNN accelerators, especially in the context of embedded systems
where resource constraints are critical [1]. Among the many approximate computing
techniques, approximate multipliers (AxMs), which can trade off between the hardware
consumption and accuracy performance [2–4], have received significant attention due to the
fact that multipliers are widely utilized in the CNN hardware accelerators [5].
Because of the wide variety of available AxMs [6–8], selecting the most appropriate type
for a given CNN architecture is a fundamental step in designing an AxM-based CNN
hardware accelerator. When addressing this task, designers need to carefully consider several
key questions to ensure that approximate units are used effectively:
How can approximate components be matched to the error tolerance levels of different
CNN layers or operations?
Which exact units in the hardware design can be replaced by approximate versions
without introducing unacceptable loss in accuracy?

--- Strana 126 ---
To address these design considerations, a systematic approach is required. This chapter
introduces a structured methodology known as error matrix-based error injection (EMEI)
[9]. EMEI serves as a practical tool for designers to evaluate and deploy existing AxMs
within CNN accelerators. By using pre-characterized error rate matrices, the technique
facilitates informed decision-making when integrating approximation into processing
elements (PEs).
As a case study, the TOSAM approximate [6] multiplier is introduced and applied within
a MobileNetV2-based CNN model trained on the CIFAR-10 dataset [10] and GTSRB [11].
This example demonstrates how AxMs with varying error profiles can be strategically
assigned across different PEs, balancing efficiency and inference accuracy. Through this
case-driven approach, the chapter provides a hands-on understanding of how approximation
techniques can be systematically applied in modern CNN hardware design.

--- Strana 127 ---
3.2 Chapter Overview
In this chapter, we begin by introducing several types of approximate multipliers that are
commonly used in CNN hardware accelerators, as discussed in Sect. 3.3. Section 3.4 then
describes the EMEI method, which provides a practical means for selecting suitable
approximate multipliers with varying precision levels, while aiming to minimize hardware
overhead. Finally, Sect. 3.5 presents a set of experimental results that demonstrate the
effectiveness and practical benefits of applying the EMEI method in CNN accelerator
design. The example code is available in the GitHub repository.1
3.3 Approximate Multiplier
In the study referenced as [12], the authors introduce an innovative approach to enhancing
the efficiency of CNN hardware accelerators through the adoption of AC technologies.
Specifically, they propose the substitution of precise hardware components with approximate
hardware components. Given that convolutional layers are responsible for most of the
computational workload in CNN accelerators [13], the implementation of AxMs, instead of
precise ones, significantly reduces the hardware costs of the CNN hardware accelerator.
Emerging research in AxM technologies focuses on three methodologies: data truncation [6],
error compensation [7], and the employment of 4:2 compressors [8]. This section will
introduce works within each of these three domains.
3.3.1 Data Truncation
TOSAM AxM [6] is a type of energy-efficient, truncation, and rounding-based AxM that
supports multiple precision levels. It reduces the computational workload of the multiplier
by truncating input operands based on the position of their leading one-bit. This design
significantly enhances energy consumption and area efficiency while maintaining acceptable
accuracy levels. The methodology involves rounding input operands to the nearest odd
number, which improves total accuracy and scalability, enabling higher precision with
increased operand widths.
Compared to existing precise and approximate multipliers, the proposed TOSAM AxM
achieves superior performance in terms of delay, area, and energy consumption. This makes
it an attractive solution for error-resilient applications, including image processing,
sharpening, and classification [6].
The functionality of the TOSAM AxM is defined in Eq. 3.1. Here, (YA)t and (YB)t
denote the values obtained when A and B are truncated t bits after the first occurrence of a
“1” in their binary representation. (YA)APX and (YB)APX represent the values when A and
B are truncated h bits following the initial “1” bit, with an additional “1” appended to the
right, resulting in h + 1 bits in total. kA and kB refer to the positions of the first “1” in A and
B, respectively [6]:
(3.1)
The structure of the TOSAM AxM is depicted in Fig. 3.1. This architecture comprises
several key components:
Approximate Absolute Unit: Calculates the absolute value of the inputs.
A × B ≈2kA+kB × (1 + (YA)t + (YB)t + (YA)APX × (YB)APX)

--- Strana 128 ---
Sign or Zero Detector Unit: Determines whether the result is zero and identifies the sign
of the result.
Leading One Detector Unit: Locates the position of the first occurrence of “1” in the
inputs.
Truncation Unit: Truncates the least significant bits of the operands.
Arithmetic Unit: Performs the primary arithmetic computation based on the truncated
inputs.
Shift Unit: Scales the result from the arithmetic unit to recover the final value.
Compatible Module: Selects the suitable accuracy level for the AxM based on input
values.
Fig. 3.1 The structure of the approximate TOSAM multiplier
3.3.2 Error Compensation
In work [7], a low-power, high-performance AxM with configurable partial error recovery
was introduced. This design utilizes approximate adders that limit carry propagation to
neighboring bits for fast partial product accumulation. By employing configurable error
recovery, the design achieves varying levels of accuracy through the use of different
numbers of most significant bits (MSBs) for error reduction.
Additionally, this approach allows for the adjustment of the number of MSBs involved in
error recovery calculations to offer different levels of computational accuracy. The
functionality of the approximate adder is defined by Eq. 3.2, which computes one bit of the
sum result with inputs Ami and Bmi for each bit of the input operands [7]. The error
recovery signal for each adder and the total error recovery signal for the multiplier are given
by Eqs. 3.3 and 3.4, respectively.
Furthermore, an error recovery function (Eq. 3.5) adjusts the final output. In this context,
Sm′
i represents the sum result’s bit, Emi denotes the error recovery signal’s bit for each
adder, E is the total error recovery signal, S is the corrected output, and Eap is the
approximated error recovery signal obtained after truncating less significant bits.
Figure 3.2 illustrates an 8-bit AxM based on this design, which requires the five most
significant bits of the error recovery signal for effective functionality. Each circle in Fig. 3.2

3.4 Error Matrix-Based Error Injection
3.4.1 Error Matrix of Approximate Multiplier Determination
The. working principle of TOSAM was introduced in Sect. 3.3.1. The six accuracy levels are
represented by bit retention post-truncation as (h, t). These levels are (0, 0), (0, 2), (1, 4),
(2, 6), (3, 7), and (5, 9). The error distribution matrix for each level is derived by comparing
approximate outcomes with exact values, resulting in an error ratio. This comparison is
expressed in Eq. 3.11.
In Eq. 3.11, N denotes the number of fractional bits in the binary fixed-point format. The
variables A and B represent the values obtained by directly converting the binary
representations of the multiplier and multiplicand into base-10 integers without considering
the decimal point. Ap(A, B) refers to the result of the approximate multiplication. As
evident from Eq. 3.11, the length of fractional bits does not impact the error distribution.
Therefore, for matrix calculations, both multipliers and multiplicands are treated as integers
within the range 0 to 215−1.
The error rate distributions for these six levels of TOSAM AxMs are illustrated in Fig.
3.4:

3.4.2 Error Injection for Determination of Suitable Approximate Multiplier’s
Accuracy Level
The utilization of error distribution matrices is essential for selecting the optimal AxM and
its precision across various conditions. A singular selection may not effectively balance
hardware efficiency with the required accuracy level. Hence, employing a combination of
AxMs with varying precision levels becomes necessary. Inspired by image processing
techniques [14], where feature maps are divided into regions of high and low significance,
we propose processing data within regions of high significance using high-precision PEs,
while data in regions of low significance are processed by low-precision PEs. Each feature
in feature maps is computed by the operations in the preceding layer, and the significance of
these features determines the precision of multipliers for their production in the earlier layer.
To select suitable AxM precision levels and arrange them effectively, the EMEI
framework includes two main steps: (1) The selection of two AxM types with high and low
accuracy levels (2) The partitioning of importance-based segments within feature maps in
CNNs
3.4.2.1 Selection of Suitable Approximate Multipliers’ Accuracy Levels
To determine the most suitable AxMs and their accuracy levels, we start by assuming that
AxMs replace all precise multipliers in CNN accelerators. This means that multiplications

--- Strana 132 ---
within convolutional, normalization, and fully connected layers will include injected error, as
specified in Eq. 3.12:
(3.12)
In this equation, M represents the error rate distribution matrix for the AxM at the
evaluated accuracy level, and M(A) refers to the data located at coordinate A within the
matrix M.
The methodology for selecting the optimal AxMs and their precision levels is as follows:
1. Order the potential AxMs in descending order of their error rates.
 
2. Begin the process of injecting error rates using matrices with the lowest error rates.
 
3. Monitor the CNN’s classification accuracy. Stop the error injection process if the
classification accuracy falls below the predefined acceptable accuracy level.
 
4. Select the current precision level of the AxM as the low-precision option for lower-
accuracy PEs in the CNN hardware accelerator. The adjacent higher-precision mode of
the AxM is selected as the high-precision option for higher-accuracy PEs in the CNN
hardware accelerator.
 
3.5 Experimental Setup and Experiment Result
To evaluate the effectiveness of EMEI, we developed two CNN models based on the
MobileNetV2 architecture. The detailed structure of these CNNs is shown in Figs. 3.5 and
3.6. For training, we utilized a subset of the CIFAR-10 dataset containing high-clarity
images, consisting of 37,074 images for the training set and 7,300 images for the testing set,
trained using the CNN shown in Fig. 3.5. Additionally, we employed the GTSRB dataset,
with 35,209 images for the training set and 12,630 images for the testing set, trained using
the CNN in Fig. 3.6 to demonstrate the performance of EMEI.
Ap(A, B) ≈A × B × M(randi(size(M)))

--- Strana 133 ---
Fig. 3.5 The structure of the CNN for the CIFAR-10 dataset
Fig. 3.6 The structure of the CNN for the GTSRB dataset
The architecture of the CNN hardware accelerator employed in our study is illustrated in
Fig. 3.7. Within this architecture, the controller module controls the operational status of
each layer’s components. Each layer includes multiple PEs, and the controller ensures
accurate memory addressing for the input and output of each PE. The input and output of
each PE are not directly sent to the memory; instead, they are stored in a buffer and then
transferred to memory through DMA. The data processed by this hardware accelerator is
represented as signed 16-bit fixed-point numbers, which include one sign bit, six integer bits,
and nine fractional bits.

--- Strana 134 ---
Fig. 3.7 The structure of the CNN hardware accelerator
Instead of employing precise multipliers, our system utilizes TOSAM AxMs. The
selectable modes for the TOSAM AxMs are (h, t) = (0, 0), (0, 2), (1, 4), (2, 6), (3, 7), and
(5, 9). Figure 3.4 presents the histogram of the error distribution matrix utilized in our
experiments. For this investigation, we set a minimum acceptable classification accuracy
threshold of 85% for the CIFAR-10 CNN and 93% for the GTSRB CNN.
3.5.1 Approximate Multiplier and Accuracy Determination
Based on the methodology described in Sect. 3.4, we injected errors into every
multiplication operation within the CNN model, following the error distribution matrix of
the AxM across various levels of accuracy, as shown in Fig. 3.4. The classification
accuracies generated from the application of different error matrices are illustrated in Figs.
3.8 and 3.9. Given that the threshold for the lowest permissible classification accuracy was
set at 85% and 93% for CIFAR-10 and GTSRB, respectively, the (h, t) mode selected for
the high-accuracy AxM was determined to be (2, 6), while the mode chosen for the low-
accuracy AxM was (1, 4). This selection was based on the observation that, at the accuracy
level (h, t) = (1, 4), the classification accuracy remained just below the threshold value.
Following the instruction in Sect. 3.4, this accuracy level was selected as the low-accuracy
multiplier, and the adjacent higher level, (h, t) = (2, 6), was chosen as the high-accuracy
multiplier.

--- Strana 135 ---
Fig. 3.8 Varied classification accuracies observed in CIFAR-10 systems employing approximate multipliers at distinct
accuracy levels
Fig. 3.9 Varied classification accuracies observed in GTSRB systems employing approximate multipliers at distinct
accuracy levels
Following the instructions in Sect. 3.4, the expansion of the less important regions within
the feature map was executed, initiating from the left toward the right, then from top to

--- Strana 136 ---
bottom, followed by right to left, and finally from bottom to top. This expansion of
nonessential regions generally leads to a decrease in classification accuracy. This trend in
CIFAR-10 and GTSRB systems is represented in Tables 3.1 and 3.2. According to the
guidelines specified in Sect. 3.4, when the classification accuracy drops below the
predefined threshold, the expansion of the unimportant region is reversed by one grid in the
recent direction of expansion.

under the constraint of a minimum acceptable accuracy, EMEI incurs low hardware

3.6 Conclusions
In this chapter, we introduce an error injection methodology that leverages the error rate
distribution matrix of AxMs. Before detailing the methodology itself, we first review the
operational principles and structural designs of several commonly used AxMs, including
TOSAM, error compensation-based designs, and multipliers built using approximate 4:2
compressors. These foundational concepts help to establish a clear understanding of how
different AxMs function and why they are relevant for optimizing CNN hardware
accelerators.
The error injection methodology offers a structured approach for assigning suitable
precision levels of AxMs to individual PEs in a CNN accelerator. This approach enables
designers to meet the minimum classification accuracy required by the target model while
improving overall hardware efficiency through a balanced use of high- and low-precision
multipliers. The advantages of this method become particularly apparent when the hardware
cost difference between adjacent AxM precision levels is significant.
Experimental validation shows that this precision assignment strategy can maintain close
alignment between hardware-level and software-level performance, with classification
accuracy differences remaining within 3% in typical cases.
Appendix: Performance of Error Matrix-Based Error
Injection

--- Strana 139 ---

4. A Gem5 Tutorial on Simulating DoS
Attacks in Network-on-Chip
Haoyu Wang1    and Basel Halak1  
School of Electronics and Computer Science, University of
Southampton, Southampton, UK
 
Haoyu Wang

Keywords DoS attacks – Network-on-chip (NoC) – Gem5 simulator –
Microarchitecture simulation – Security vulnerabilities – Flooding attacks –

Hardware simulation – Garnet network model – Attack modeling – Traffic
