4.1 Introduction
This chapter serves as a hands-on tutorial focused on security in network-
on-chip (NoC) systems, which are widely used communication fabrics in
large-scale chips due to their scalability, lightweight design, and efficient
communication. As NoCs handle a substantial portion of on-chip
communication, they are increasingly exposed to diverse security threats.
We begin by introducing the microarchitecture and design principles of
NoC-based multiprocessor system-on-chip (MPSoC) platforms. Next, we
provide an overview of NoC-specific security issues, including
unauthorized access, data corruption, and denial-of-service (DoS) attacks. A

--- Strana 142 ---
comprehensive literature review offers context on common attack vectors
and current countermeasures. We then introduce a practical implementation
of a DoS flooding attack model in the Gem5 Garnet simulator, enabling
readers to reproduce and evaluate realistic threat scenarios. Finally, we
summarize recent advances in NoC security architecture, highlighting
promising directions for future research.
To better understand the urgency of securing NoC architectures, it is
important to consider recent trends in hardware development. Machine
learning models have demonstrated remarkable performance in
classification and detection tasks, becoming vital tools in a wide range of
applications. While their application in cybersecurity has been extensively
explored, their use in hardware security remains relatively underdeveloped
[1]. Meanwhile, the demand for large-scale computing chips is rapidly
increasing to meet the needs of modern computational tasks, such as
training and inference for large language models (LLMs) [2]. Companies
like Nvidia and AMD have responded by designing high-performance data
server chips. However, the significant data throughput and computational
intensity of these workloads introduce new opportunities for adversaries to
launch sophisticated and covert attacks [3, 4]. These threats emphasize the
growing importance of developing secure hardware systems, particularly
through the use of machine learning techniques to enhance resilience
against attacks at the architectural level.

--- Strana 143 ---
4.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter begins with an introduction to the microarchitecture of
network-on-chip (NoC)-based multiprocessor system-on- chip (MPSoC)
platforms in Sect. 4.3. We describe core components such as routers and
network interfaces, common communication protocols, and typical
architectural designs. This section also introduces tools and methodologies
for modeling and simulating NoC systems, equipping readers with practical
techniques for analyzing performance and functionality—an essential
foundation for subsequent security analysis.
Section 4.4 focuses on the security challenges associated with NoCs. It
starts with a classification of common attack types, including
eavesdropping, spoofing, denial-of-service (DoS), and side-channel attacks.
Section 4.4.2 provides a step-by-step tutorial on reproducing a DoS
flooding attack using the Gem5 Garnet simulator. This hands-on guide
demonstrates how to implement and evaluate custom threat models,
offering valuable insights into real-world NoC vulnerabilities. Finally, Sect.
4.4.3 reviews current countermeasures for NoC security, highlighting their
strengths and limitations, and paving the way for future research in secure
NoC design.
4.3 Microarchitecture of Network-on-Chips
The concept of network-on-chip (NoC) as a key enabler for designing
multiprocessor system-on-chips (MPSoCs) has evolved significantly,
representing a major shift away from traditional bus-based and point-to-
point communication architectures in SoCs. Initially conceived as a scalable
solution for managing complex on-chip communication, NoCs gained
prominence with the pioneering work of William Dally, who introduced the
theoretical framework for packet-switched micro-networks. This innovation
marked a paradigm shift in the way SoC designs were conceptualized [5].
Building on this foundation, Luca Benini and Giovanni De Micheli further
emphasized NoCs as a transformative “new paradigm” for SoC design,
highlighting their critical role in addressing the communication demands of
increasingly complex and heterogeneous SoCs [6].
Figure 4.1 illustrates a typical mesh NoC architecture. In this design, the
NoC comprises nodes interconnected through links or channels. Each node,

--- Strana 144 ---
often referred to as a tile in chip design, typically includes a router, a
network interface, and various intellectual properties (IPs), such as CPUs,
caches, or specialized accelerators. Communication between nodes involves
packaging data, instructions, and information from one IP into packets,
which are then transmitted across the network. Depending on the link
width, these packets may be divided into smaller units called flits. Figure
4.1 also shows an example packet structure, derived from the Netrace
dataset [7], which demonstrates the standardized format used for efficient
packing and unpacking by routers and network interfaces based on a unified
architecture.
Fig. 4.1 A mesh-based NoC architecture
Different topologies significantly influence the structure and
performance of a NoC [8]. Topology refers to the arrangement of nodes and
channels within the network. Selecting an appropriate topology for chip
development depends on various factors, including complexity, density,
workload, and PPA (performance, power, and area). While the mesh
topology is commonly employed in MPSoCs, many other topologies exist
and can be broadly categorized as either direct or indirect. In direct
topologies, each router acts as both a source and destination for traffic, as
exemplified by ring, mesh, and torus topologies, which are prevalent in
NoCs. Conversely, indirect topologies distinguish between routers and
terminal nodes, with only the terminal nodes functioning as sources or sinks
for traffic.
The router is a critical component in a NoC, facilitating information
transfer, traffic arbitration, and control at each node. It is typically

--- Strana 145 ---
responsible for three primary functions: routing, flow control, and
switching. Consequently, its micro-architecture has a significant impact on
the NoC’s performance, particularly regarding per-hop delay and energy
consumption. A standard router microarchitecture, shown in Fig. 4.2,
includes essential components such as virtual channels (VCs), routing logic,
allocators, a crossbar switch, and buffers. VC are not required for all NoC
configurations; however, they play a crucial role in improving performance
when traffic density becomes too high for fixed, non-virtual channels to
handle. VCs enable NoCs to implement a dynamic and adaptive channel
allocation strategy [9]. This design allows the NoC to efficiently and
flexibly manage sudden spikes in high-volume traffic, ensuring smoother
communication and reduced congestion. It also contributes to mitigate
deadlock cases by cutting off the deadlock loop.
Fig. 4.2 Router microarchitecture and routing process
In operation, a neighboring router first verifies whether downstream
routers have available credits. If credits are available, the neighboring
router begins sending packets, which are written into the input buffers of
the receiving router. The receiving router then computes the routing to
determine the appropriate output port direction. Virtual channel allocation
modules manage local communication resources by dynamically assigning
VCs (buffers) to incoming packets. Following VC allocation and
arbitration, the packet undergoes switch allocation and proceeds to the
output stage via link traversal.

--- Strana 146 ---
In addition to the router, the network interface is another critical
component of NoC communication, serving as the initial point of
interaction [10]. The NI is responsible for packetizing data from the host
IPs connected to the local node and analyzing the communication
requirements specified by these host IPs [11]. Depending on whether the
packet needs to be sent via uni-casting, multi-casting, or broadcasting, the
NI adjusts certain fields within the packets to indicate the appropriate
communication mode for downstream nodes. Conversely, the NI is also
tasked with depacketizing incoming data from other nodes, converting it
into a format usable by the locally connected IPs. Due to the extensive
customization required for different applications and IP types, there is no
standard architecture diagram to represent the NI’s design.
Recent developments in NoC designs are increasingly supporting
specialized computing paradigms, particularly in machine learning as we
mentioned in the introduction. José Flich and Davide Bertozzi highlighted
the growing importance of NoCs in the era of nanoscale computing,
emphasizing that architectural decisions made today will shape the next
generation of NoCs [12]. One promising area of research is 3D network-on-
chip architectures, with Miltos Grammatikakis leading studies on the fault-
tolerant capabilities of 3D NoCs, which could revolutionize SoC design
[13]. NoC architectures are increasingly being adopted not only in
nanoscale computing but also in deep neural network (DNN) accelerators.
Traditional platforms like CPUs and GPUs face significant challenges
related to power consumption and performance when handling DNN
workloads. Beyond general-purpose processors, NoCs are more commonly
integrated into modern domain-specific accelerators (DSAs), such as DNN
accelerators, due to their superior scalability and high communication
efficiency. Chen et al. [14] proposes NoC-based DNN accelerators as an
effective solution, emphasizing their ability to reduce off-chip memory
accesses and enhance computational flexibility. By separating data
computation and communication, NoC-based designs offer improved
scalability and power efficiency (Fig. 4.3).

--- Strana 147 ---
Fig. 4.3 (a) The flattened DNN model; (b) clustering neurons; (c) mapping the clustered ANN
model to the target NoC platform
On the industrial front, several companies are actively incorporating
academic research findings into their technology stacks, transforming NoCs
from theoretical concepts into practical, real-world applications. These
advancements aim to deliver technologies that are efficient, robust, and well
suited to managing the computational complexities of modern systems.
To achieve the research objectives, a variety of modelling and
generation tools for NoC systems can be utilized, many of which have been
developed over the past decades. HORNET [15], Noxim [16], Gem5 Garnet
[17], and BookSim [18] are command-based tools, primarily developed in
C++ that provide comprehensive modelling capabilities for NoC systems.
Among these, Gem5 Garnet is particularly notable for enabling full-system
simulations, complete with a Linux OS, allowing researchers to assess
realistic performance and functionality in diverse use cases.
In addition to modelling tools, several RTL (register transfer level)
generators are available for NoCs, including ProNoC [19], RaveNoC [20],
OpenPiton [21], ESP [22], and Hermes [23]. These tools are instrumental in
rapidly implementing NoC-based SoCs or serving as design templates to
integrate IPs and customize systems according to specific requirements.
These RTL generators streamline the development process by providing
preconfigured NoC frameworks, enabling efficient hardware design and
validation. Most of these tools feature a GUI, providing users with a more
intuitive and visualized configuration experience. Additionally, they often
include early-stage simulation capabilities and brief performance
evaluations, even without requiring RTL code. Together, these tools offer a
robust suite of solutions for both simulating and building NoC-based

--- Strana 148 ---
systems, making them invaluable resources for researchers and developers
in the field.
4.4 Security in Network-on-Chips
4.4.1 Overview of Security Attacks in NoCs
NoC systems are essential for on-chip communication, making them an
attractive target for a variety of attacks. While online threats like malware
pose significant dangers, offline methods such as side-channel attacks also
compromise the integrity and confidentiality of NoC systems. Due to the
wide range and complexity of these threats, it is essential to understand
their classification. Charles et al. [24] provide a detailed taxonomy,
highlighting five primary types of attacks on NoCs: eavesdropping,
spoofing/data integrity, denial-of-service (DoS), buffer overflow/memory
extraction, and side-channel attacks.
NoC systems are vulnerable to various attacks that exploit on-chip
communication. Eavesdropping involves adversaries intercepting sensitive
data, often through hardware Trojans in routers along communication paths.
Spoofing attacks occur when malicious nodes impersonate trusted ones,
leading to data corruption, information leakage, or system collapse. DoS
attacks disrupt access by overwhelming nodes with excessive traffic, often
through flooding or tampering with packet destinations. Buffer overflow
attacks inject malicious code, causing hardware buffer overflows that
compromise shared memory in multicore systems, potentially impacting the
entire system. Lastly, side-channel attacks exploit physical variations like
power or electromagnetic signals to extract information, leveraging the high
communication density in NoC systems.
In this work, our primary focus is on DoS attacks targeting NoC
architectures. Among the various types of DoS attacks, traffic-based attacks
stand out as particularly prevalent. These attacks are especially harmful
because they compromise not only the security and integrity of NoC
systems but also lead to significant degradation in overall system
performance. This is particularly consequential in real-time and mission-
critical applications, where even minor performance disruptions can have
severe impacts. Our objective is to not only identify these attacks but also to
model them effectively, enabling the development of more robust security
solutions for NoCs. A more comprehensive discussion of DoS attacks,

--- Strana 149 ---
including their various forms and how they can be modelled within the
context of NoCs, will be provided in the following subsection.
DoS attacks, particularly flooding attacks, present significant challenges
to NoC-based systems, leading to severe performance degradation, reduced
quality of service, and increased power consumption. For instance, [25]
thoroughly examines the impact of such attacks on MPSoC performance,
identifying various attack paths, including overlaps and loop-based attacks.
Additionally, [26] provides a detailed categorization of flooding attacks,
introducing a conceptual framework that considers the number of attackers
and victims. This framework illustrates attack patterns involving malicious
IPs targeting victim IPs, offering valuable insights into how these attacks
are executed, as depicted in Fig. 4.4.
Fig. 4.4 Flooding attack simulation from Sinha et al.
Chaves et al. [27] introduced the term “Flooding DoS” to specifically
describe flooding attacks within NoC environments. This study offers a
unique perspective, highlighting that such attacks can be initiated not by
hardware Trojans but through malicious software operating on a processing
element. The research explores two FDoS implementation scenarios: one
involving an increase in the packet injection rate and the other focusing on

--- Strana 150 ---
extending the packet payload length. These findings highlight the
complexity and variety of DoS attacks, particularly flooding attacks, and
underscore the necessity for comprehensive and multilayered defense
strategies.
In this case, it developed a refined flooding DoS threat model for NoCs
by introducing an adjustable flooding injection rate. This innovation stems
from the observation that varying injection rates can produce different
impacts on NoC runtime performance and resource utilization. We
integrated this functionality into the synthetic traffic pattern benchmarks in
Gem5 Garnet, allowing users to control injection rates through newly
introduced command options.
The threat model includes two attack scenarios: (1) a single attacker
targeting a single victim and (2) two attackers simultaneously targeting a
single victim. The injected packets contain random, useless data. A key
novelty in this threat model is that normal communication across all nodes
continues without interruption, albeit at a reduced efficiency due to the
flooding attack. In this implementation, flooding traffic overlays normal
communication, degrading its performance to represent an “ideal” flooding
attack scenario, as illustrated in Fig. 4.5.

--- Strana 151 ---
Fig. 4.5 “Ideal” flooding DoS threat modelling
Additionally, this model ensures that adversaries do not compromise
network protocols or routing algorithms, maintaining the stealth of the
attack. For instance, router communication credits remain balanced because
all transactions, including those initiated by flooding attackers, are
legitimate, and malicious packets adhere to the system’s default routing
algorithm. Consequently, the NoC protocol permits the flooding attack to
persist, overwhelming the system with a surge of malicious packets without
violating protocol rules or engaging in unauthorized communications.
Consequently, system runtime characteristics, such as performance
degradation or complete system failure due to traffic congestion, vary
significantly based solely on the flooding injection rate (FIR). This
functionality was implemented within workload applications as a malicious
“Tick” function (in synthetic traffic pattern benchmark of gem5), with
adjustable input parameters for flexibility. It will be explained more in Sect.
4.4.2 by a tutorial of reproducing this threat. The right-side plot in Fig. 4.6
illustrates the relationship between latency and FIR in our flooding model.
As FIR increases, latency escalates dramatically, severely impacting system

--- Strana 152 ---
performance. For instance, latency rises from 1.1 to 60 times the normal
value as FIR increases from 0.1 to 0.9. At FIR=1, the system crashes
entirely. Lower FIR values, while more covert, still exert a sustained
negative impact on system performance.
Fig. 4.6 The increasing system latency with increasing flooding injection rate
4.4.2 Tutorial: Fine-Turning DoS Flooding Attack
Reproducing in Gem5 Garnet
4.4.2.1 Overview of Gem5 and Garnet
Gem5 is a modular, open-source, cycle-accurate computer system simulator
widely used in computer architecture research and education. It supports
multiple instruction set architectures (ISAs) such as x86, ARM, RISC-V,
and MIPS and offers various CPU models, including in-order, out-of-order,
and atomic models. Gem5 can simulate both full-system and syscall
emulation modes, making it versatile for different research needs [28].
Garnet is an on-chip network model within Gem5 that provides cycle-
accurate microarchitectural implementation of network components. It
allows detailed modeling of routers, links, and topologies, enabling

--- Strana 153 ---
researchers to study network-on-chip (NoC) behaviors and performance
[17].
We selected Gem5 with Garnet for our experiments due to its flexibility,
extensibility, and detailed modeling capabilities, which are essential for
accurately simulating and analyzing DoS attacks in NoC environments.
4.4.2.2 Setting Up the Gem5 Simulation Environment
To set up the Gem5 simulation environment with Garnet, follow these
steps:
1. Clone the Gem5 Repository:
git clone https://gem5.googlesource.com/public/gem5
cd gem5
 
2. Install Dependencies: Ensure that the necessary dependencies are
installed. This typically includes Python 3, SCons, and other libraries.  
3. Build Gem5 with Garnet Support: Use the following command to
build Gem5 with the Garnet standalone protocol:
scons build/NULL/gem5.debug
\ PROTOCOL=Garnet_standalone -j$(nproc)
This builds the Gem5 binary with the NULL ISA and Garnet
standalone protocol, suitable for network-only simulations.
 
4.4.2.3 Configuring the Hardware Architecture
Our experimental setup uses a 4×4 mesh topology with 16 nodes. Each
node is connected to a router, and the network operates with the following
parameters:
The configuration parameters listed in Table 4.1 define the structural
and functional setup of the simulated NoC environment:
Number of CPUs: The simulation includes 16 processor cores,
representing a typical multicore MPSoC platform. Each core is mapped

--- Strana 154 ---
to a corresponding node in the mesh topology.
Number of Directories: There are 16 directories used for cache
coherence. This ensures one-to-one mapping between CPU nodes and
directory nodes, simplifying coherence management.
Topology: The Mesh_XY setting specifies a 2D mesh interconnect
where routers are arranged in a grid. Communication is routed in the XY
order, meaning X-direction traversal is completed before moving in the
Y-direction.
Mesh Rows: The mesh has four rows and four columns, forming a 4×4
grid of routers. This structure ensures uniform communication distances
across the chip.
Simulation Cycles: The simulation runs for 10,000 clock cycles, which
is sufficient for observing traffic behavior and the impact of injected
attacks without incurring long simulation times.
Synthetic Traffic Pattern: The uniform_random pattern randomly
selects destination nodes for packet injection, creating a balanced load
across the network. It is commonly used for stress-testing NoC behavior.
Injection Rate: Set to 0.05, this parameter determines the probability of
packet injection per node per cycle. A moderate injection rate helps
evaluate performance under typical workloads without immediate
saturation.
Virtual Channels Per Virtual Network: Each input port supports four
virtual channels. Virtual channels are used to improve throughput, reduce
congestion, and prevent deadlock by allowing multiple logical flows over
the same physical link.
Table 4.1 Hardware architecture configuration
Parameter
Value
Number of CPUs
16
Number of directories
16
Topology
Mesh_XY
Mesh rows
4
Simulation cycles
10,000
Synthetic traffic pattern
uniform_random
Injection rate
0.05

--- Strana 155 ---
Parameter
Value
Virtual channels per virtual network 4
These configuration options provide a well-balanced and realistic NoC
simulation environment suitable for evaluating both performance and
security behavior under synthetic workloads and adversarial conditions.
4.4.2.4 Building the Hardware Architecture and Running Traffic
Pattern Tests
After configuring the hardware architecture, build the simulation
environment and run the traffic pattern tests using the command provided.
This will execute the simulation with the specified parameters, allowing
observation of network behavior under uniform random traffic.
These parameters can be set using the following command:
./build/NULL/gem5.debug
\configs/example/garnet_synth_traffic.py
--num-cpus=16
--num-dirs=16
--network=garnet
--topology=Mesh_XY
--mesh-rows=4
--sim-cycles=10000
--synthetic=uniform_random
--injectionrate=0.05
--vcs-per-vnet=4
4.4.2.5 Implementing the DoS Flooding Attack
To model the DoS flooding attack, we introduced two custom functions:
tick_attack() and flooding_pkt_gen().
The tick_attack() function builds upon the built-in tick() function
provided in the STP framework, serving as a parallel mechanism to
introduce the flooding behavior. This function is triggered on every
simulation cycle and acts as the primary controller for enabling or disabling
the malicious packet injection process via the flooding_pkt_gen() function.
The behavior is regulated by a user-defined flooding rate, which specifies
the number of malicious packets injected into the NoC per 1000 cycles.
This flooding rate is passed directly from the command line when

--- Strana 156 ---
initializing the Gem5 simulation, allowing for flexible control over the
attack intensity. Figure 4.7 presents the implementation of the tick_attack()
function, showcasing its interaction with the overall benchmark logic.
Fig. 4.7 “tick_attack()” function implementation
The flooding_pkt_gen() function performs the core tasks of generating
and injecting malicious packets. Users can configure the attack by enabling
it and specifying the attacker node(s) and victim node through command-
line inputs. Once configured, this function is invoked during each cycle,
injecting packets with the attacker node as the source ID and the victim
node as the destination ID. The injected packets, designed to be useless or
irrelevant to the regular system operations, flood the NoC at a predefined
rate. At the conclusion of the packet generation process, the
sendPkt(packet) function is called to successfully inject the customized
packets into the NoC. The implementation of the flooding_pkt_gen()
function is detailed in Fig. 4.8.

--- Strana 157 ---
Fig. 4.8 “flooding_pkt_gen” function implementation
Both functions were integrated into the file GarnetSyntheticTraffic.cc
within the Gem5 source code, ensuring their compatibility with the existing
Garnet framework. The updated file, along with instructions for its usage, is
available for public access on our GitHub repository.1
By introducing these functions, we have provided a flexible and
reusable mechanism for simulating DoS flooding attacks in NoCs, enabling
researchers to further explore and evaluate security vulnerabilities and
mitigation strategies in large-scale NoC designs.
4.4.3 Existing Countermeasures for NoC Security

--- Strana 158 ---
Extensive research has been conducted to understand the security
implications of DoS attacks in NoC systems, focusing on mechanisms for
identifying malicious activities, particularly in third-party IPs. These
security architectures typically adopt one of two design methodologies: in-
router monitoring/detection schemes and global detection frameworks. The
in-router approach involves implementing independent detection and
localization mechanisms directly within each router, allowing for localized
and real-time identification of anomalies. This method is well suited for
modular systems where individual router autonomy is essential [29, 30]. On
the other hand, global detection schemes operate by aggregating and
analyzing data from all nodes or traffic components across a defined area,
providing a comprehensive view of the system’s behavior. This collective
judgment approach enables the detection of larger, more distributed attack
patterns but often requires greater computational resources and coordination
[25, 31]. Both methodologies have their advantages and trade-offs,
underscoring the need for context-specific solutions to address the diverse
and evolving nature of DoS threats in NoC systems.
In the field of in-router security architectures, various innovative
methods have been proposed to address flooding attacks in NoC systems.
The Sniffer framework, introduced in [26], employs a lightweight
distributed machine learning model (perceptron) integrated into each router
node to accurately identify congestion caused by malicious traffic. These
perceptrons analyze multiple network parameters, including buffer waiting
time, inter-flit intervals, and virtual channel occupancy, to differentiate
between attack and non-attack scenarios. A collective decision-making
strategy further enhances accuracy by combining congestion data from
neighboring routers. Additionally, a priority mechanism ensures the smooth
traversal of probing packets during localization, minimizing disruption to
legitimate network traffic. The architecture is scalable, exhibits minimal
hardware and power overhead, and demonstrates high localization accuracy
across varied attack scenarios, including coordinated attacks with multiple
MIPs.
The AGAPE secure router architecture in [32] leverages a generative
adversarial network (GAN)-based anomaly detection framework to identify
and mitigate hardware Trojan (HT) attacks in NoC systems. The
architecture incorporates dynamic error correction hardware for fault-
injection HTs, bypass links for misrouting HTs, and channel buffers for

--- Strana 159 ---
packet-drop HTs. Using a semi-supervised GAN, AGAPE models time-
series data of various runtime NoC attributes, such as buffer utilization and
latency, to detect anomalies and classify HT types with high precision.
Instead of isolating infected routers entirely, AGAPE applies targeted
mitigation strategies, reducing performance degradation. The architecture
includes enhanced routing mechanisms, dynamic link bypassing, and state
tracking to maintain network functionality even under attack, achieving
improved detection accuracy (up to 91%) while reducing latency and power
consumption by 39% and 30%, respectively.
The secure router architecture proposed by Chaves et al. [30]. focuses
on mitigating low-and-slow denial-of-service (LSDoS) attacks in NoCs.
This architecture integrates a lightweight LSDoS monitor within the router,
positioned between the local FIFO buffer and subsequent router
components. The monitor employs a finite state machine (FSM) to detect
anomalies in packet inter-flit delays, triggering countermeasures when a
predefined threshold is exceeded. When an LSDoS attack is detected, the
monitor truncates malicious packets by injecting a tail flit, releasing
communication resources. Any remaining flits of malicious packets are
dropped, effectively neutralizing the attack. This approach ensures minimal
disruption to legitimate traffic while maintaining negligible area and
performance overheads. Experimental results demonstrate its robustness
against both single and multisource LSDoS attacks, offering an effective
solution for enhancing NoC security (Figs. 4.9, 4.10 and 4.11).

--- Strana 160 ---
Fig. 4.9 Sniffer architecture
Fig. 4.10 AGAPE router example

--- Strana 161 ---
Fig. 4.11 Router architecture with LSDoS monitor
Numerous studies have explored global detection methodologies for
addressing security threats in NoC systems, leveraging collective data
analysis for comprehensive monitoring. The secure router architecture
proposed in the study [33] introduces a lightweight and real-time
framework for detecting and localizing DoS attacks in NoC-based SoCs. It
relies on two key mechanisms: packet arrival curves (PACs) and destination
latency curves (DLCs). Each router profiles traffic during the design phase
to establish upper PAC bounds, which are used to monitor communication
behavior and flag anomalies in real time. The DLCs, recorded at each
destination IP, capture latency statistics and help identify potential
malicious sources by analyzing deviations from expected values. The
architecture integrates a leaky bucket algorithm to ensure efficient detection
with minimal overhead. Upon detecting a threat, the routers collaborate

--- Strana 162 ---
using diagnostic messages to localize the malicious IP, reducing false
positives by evaluating congestion along the network path. Experimental
results demonstrate the system’s ability to achieve timely detection and
localization with an area overhead of less than 6% and power overhead
below 4%. This framework highlights a distributed, efficient, and scalable
solution for improving NoC security against DoS attacks (Fig. 4.12).
Fig. 4.12 LSDoS Monitor
Expanding on these approaches, Atul Prasad Deb Nath and colleagues
proposed the ARK architecture in [34]. The ARK architecture introduces a
robust framework for ensuring the security resilience of NoC-based SoCs in
the presence of untrusted components. It consists of two primary
components: satellite units deployed at each router and IP, and a centralized
security policy engine (SPE). The satellite units monitor and detect critical
interface events, generate standardized frame-based packets containing
security metadata, and enforce real-time security policies by
communicating with the SPE. The SPE, acting as the “security brain,”
analyzes data received from satellite units, enforces fine-grained security
policies, and updates the system’s security state to mitigate threats such as

--- Strana 163 ---
packet misdirection, masquerading, and data tampering. This architecture is
scalable and adaptable to various attack scenarios, providing dynamic on-
the-fly detection and mitigation while incurring minimal area and power
overhead. Experimental results demonstrate its efficiency in detecting and
mitigating malicious NoC communications without significantly affecting
performance (Fig. 4.13).
Fig. 4.13 Security ARK NoC architecture

--- Strana 164 ---
4.5 Conclusions
This chapter provided a hands-on tutorial for simulating DoS attacks in
NoC systems using Gem5. Starting with an overview of NoC architecture
and security challenges, we focused on flooding-based DoS threats and
introduced a practical approach to reproducing these attacks. Readers were
guided through setting up the Gem5 simulation environment, configuring a
mesh-based NoC, and modifying traffic generation code to inject attack
packets at controlled rates. These steps enable systematic experimentation
and help assess the performance degradation caused by varying attack
intensities.
The goal of this tutorial is to equip readers with a reusable simulation
framework for studying NoC security. By integrating custom threat models
into Gem5 Garnet, users can replicate real-world vulnerabilities and
evaluate system responses. This chapter also discussed key
countermeasures from recent literature to offer broader context. We hope
this resource supports researchers and engineers in exploring, testing, and
advancing secure NoC design through accessible and replicable simulation
practices.

5. A Case Study of CNN Applications in
Network-on-Chip Security
Haoyu Wang1   , Ahmad Atamli1    and Basel Halak1  
School of Electronics and Computer Science, University of
Southampton, Southampton, UK
 
Haoyu Wang
Email: Haoyu.Wang@soton.ac.uk

Keywords CNN applications – Security monitoring – DoS attack detection
– DoS attack localization – Network-on-Chip security – CNN classifiers –
Feature extraction – CNN segmentation – Security analytics – Attack path
detection – Traffic monitoring – Victim localization – Attacker
identification – Real-time monitoring – Performance metrics
5.1 Introduction
In the previous chapter, we discussed the background and general
architecture of network-on-chip (NoC). With its highly efficient
communication structure and scalable design methodology, NoC has
emerged as one of the most promising solutions for on-chip interconnection
design. However, it is increasingly facing significant security challenges,
such as malicious third-party intellectual property (IP) introduction and

--- Strana 169 ---
malicious packet injection. These threats can lead to serious consequences,
including resource exhaustion, additional unwanted power consumption,
and even denial-of-service (DoS) attacks [1, 2]. As such, addressing NoC
security is no longer optional in the era of large-scale computing hardware.
To mitigate these risks, researchers and integrated circuit (IC) designers
have been actively proposing a variety of security countermeasures. These
solutions aim to monitor runtime performance, detect malicious data or
behaviors, localize malicious nodes or IPs, isolate attackers, and safeguard
critical communications within the network. For instance, [3] introduced a
reconfigurable NoC security architecture that dynamically adapts to
different threat scenarios. Weerasena et al. [2] proposed a topology-aware
detection and localization framework for distributed denial-of-service
(DDoS) attacks using graph neural networks. Additionally, [4] discussed
lightweight and trust-aware routing mechanisms to bypass malicious IPs,
enhancing the security of NoC-based systems.
This chapter focuses on applying deep learning models, specifically
convolutional neural networks (CNNs), to NoC security research. Deep
learning techniques offer unique advantages over conventional methods,
such as their ability to analyze complex patterns and adapt to dynamic
environments. The target of this chapter is threefold:
Highlighting Deep Learning in Hardware Security: We will explore
various applications of deep learning models in hardware security,
emphasizing why these models, particularly CNNs, are better suited for
this scenario compared to conventional methods.
Introducing the Framework in the Case Study: This chapter details
CNN-based attack detection and localization framework for NoC
security. We will provide a comprehensive explanation of the framework
and its implementation, including a step-by-step tutorial on reproducing
the DoS flooding attack scenario.
Performance Evaluation and Comparison: Finally, we will present the
performance and hardware overhead of the method in this chapter. This
includes a comparison with other state-of-the-art works, highlighting the
effectiveness and efficiency of the approach in addressing NoC security
challenges.
Through this chapter, we aim to demonstrate the potential of deep
learning techniques, particularly CNNs, in enhancing NoC security,

--- Strana 170 ---
providing both a theoretical foundation and practical insights for future
advancements in this critical field.

--- Strana 171 ---
5.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter introduces the application of CNN accelerators in addressing
security challenges in NoCs. It begins with an introduction to the broader
role of deep learning in hardware security, setting the stage for the
subsequent focus on CNN-based techniques. Section 5.3 provides a
comprehensive overview of deep learning applications in hardware security,
covering foundational concepts and highlighting notable examples of real-
world applications.
Section 5.4 introduces the CNN-based method for detecting and
localizing flooding denial-of-service (FDoS) attacks in NoCs. This includes
an overview of the case study, which integrates CNN models for
classification and segmentation, as well as techniques like multi-frame
fusion and victim completing enhancement for precise attack path and
victim/attacker localization.
Feature selection and model design considerations are addressed in
Sects. 5.5 and 5.6, focusing on the rationale behind choosing VCO (virtual
channel occupancy) and BOC (buffer operation counts) features and the
process of selecting an optimal CNN architecture for detection and
localization tasks.
In Sect. 5.7, the experimental setup and results evaluation are presented.
This includes a detailed discussion of detection and localization
performance under various benchmarks, alongside comparisons with related
works. Hardware overhead and scalability analyses are also provided.
Finally, Sect. 5.8 concludes the chapter by summarizing the
contributions and insights gained, reinforcing the significance of CNN-
based solutions for addressing NoC security challenges.
5.3 Deep Learning Model Application in
Hardware Security
5.3.1 Background
Since 2006, when Geoffrey Hinton coined the term “deep learning,” [5] this
transformative technology has been applied across a wide range of fields,
extending beyond scientific research to profoundly impact people’s daily
lives [6]. Scientists leverage deep learning to unravel complex, previously

--- Strana 172 ---
unknown principles of nature and science. At the same time, robots
equipped with deep learning models assist individuals with disabilities,
enabling them to lead more independent and barrier-free lives. In the field
of engineering, deep learning has demonstrated its potential to address
challenges in innovative ways. For instance, aerodynamics engineers
employ deep learning models to optimize designs for motors or airplanes,
creating more efficient and effective shapes.
However, there remains a critical engineering challenge in the field of
computer hardware that demands nonconventional solutions: hardware
security. Unlike traditional engineering problems, hardware security spans
the entire hardware supply chain [7], including every stage from circuit
design and functionality verification to manufacturing, packaging, testing,
and vending. The unpredictable nature of hardware vulnerabilities means
that no single stage can be deemed entirely secure. Engineers must maintain
equally high levels of vigilance throughout the hardware life cycle. The
growing globalization of the hardware supply chain has further heightened
the urgency of addressing these security concerns [7]. With components
often sourced, manufactured, and distributed globally, the risks of malicious
modifications, counterfeits, and other security threats have escalated.
Introducing smarter, automated, and less human-dependent security
mechanisms is critical to safeguarding hardware at every stage of its life
cycle. Deep learning, with its ability to identify patterns, analyze anomalies,
and adapt to dynamic environments, offers a promising approach to
enhancing hardware security in this interconnected and globalized era [8].
5.3.2 Deep Learning Application Examples in Hardware
Security
There are relatively few studies applying deep learning models to hardware
security, with most focusing on detecting hardware Trojans at various levels
of the IC design flow, including the register-transfer level (RTL), netlist,
gate, and layout levels. These works predominantly utilize deep learning for
static security detection, identifying malicious modifications or
vulnerabilities embedded during the design and manufacturing stages of the
IC supply chain. In contrast, other studies apply deep learning to detect
anomalies in runtime performance data or behaviors, addressing dynamic
security threats that emerge during the operational phase of IC products.
This distinction highlights the dual application of deep learning in hardware

--- Strana 173 ---
security: static detection focuses on ensuring integrity during development
and production, while dynamic detection safeguards IC products during
their deployment and usage, offering a comprehensive approach to
addressing security threats throughout the IC life cycle.
Li et al. [9] proposes a hardware Trojan detection method based on a
back-propagation (BP) neural network, leveraging its strong nonlinear
mapping and adaptive learning capabilities to enhance the detection
process. The approach focuses on extracting and analyzing side-channel
power consumption data from chips under test. A BP neural network with a
three-layer architecture (input, hidden, and output layers) is used to model
and classify subtle variations in power consumption data, distinguishing
between Trojan-free and Trojan-infected chips. The system was validated
on an AES encryption circuit implemented on an FPGA, where power
consumption data were collected and processed using MATLAB. The BP
neural network effectively retained key features of the power consumption
data, offering high classification accuracy. The experimental results
demonstrated that larger Trojans were easier to detect, while the
effectiveness decreased for smaller Trojans due to noise and waveform
alignment issues, highlighting areas for future improvement. This work
underscores the potential of neural networks for robust hardware security
applications.
Hasegawa et al. [10] presents a novel approach to hardware Trojan
classification in gate-level netlists using multilayer neural networks
(MLNNs). The proposed method extracts 11 distinctive Trojan-net features
from each net in a netlist and uses these features as input to the neural
network. The architecture employs multiple hidden layers in the MLNN to
classify nets as either Trojan affected or normal. By leveraging supervised
learning and applying a weighted cross-entropy loss function, the method
addresses the imbalance in Trojan and normal net instances. This approach
achieves high classification accuracy, maximizing both true positive rate
(TPR) and true negative rate (TNR), demonstrating superiority over
existing methods like SVM-based models in benchmarks. This contribution
highlights the potential of deep learning for automated and precise
hardware Trojan detection, significantly improving the reliability of IC
designs.
The paper [11] presents a post-deployment trust evaluation architecture
for wire-less cryptographic ICs, designed to detect dormant hardware

--- Strana 174 ---
Trojans that remain undetected during pre-deployment testing. The
architecture integrates an on-chip neural network-based classifier,
parametric measurement sensors, and a programmable stimulus generator to
assess trustworthiness dynamically. The deep learning component,
implemented through a neural network, analyzes side-channel parameters,
such as current measurements, to differentiate between trusted and
compromised behavior. By leveraging the adaptability of deep learning, the
classifier is capable of detecting anomalous patterns associated with Trojans
without requiring prior knowledge of their specific design or behavior. The
approach was validated using a wireless cryptographic IC and a
reconfigurable analogue neural network chip, demonstrating high detection
accuracy and low rates of false positives and negatives. The architecture is
efficient, introducing minimal overhead, and emphasizes robustness and
flexibility for real-world operational environments, making it a significant
advancement in post-deployment hardware security.
The paper [12] introduces a novel anomaly detection framework for
power-grid controllers using an enhanced temporal deep learning model. It
leverages long short-term memory (LSTM) networks to predict the normal
behavior of controllers based on hardware performance counter (HPC) data.
The system computes a reconstruction error distribution (RED), capturing
the deviations between predicted and observed controller behaviors. Using
statistical tests on RED, the method detects anomalies with high accuracy,
including zero-day attacks. This approach requires no prior knowledge of
attack data, making it effective against unknown threats. The experimental
results demonstrate nearly perfect detection rates and low latency,
emphasizing its capability to safeguard critical cyber-physical infrastructure
like power grids.
The paper [13] introduces RATAFIA, a ransomware detection
framework leveraging deep learning to identify and differentiate malicious
encryption processes in modern computing systems. RATAFIA employs
two sequential long short-term memory (LSTM) autoencoders: the first
autoencoder operates on time-domain data gathered from hardware
performance counters (HPCs) to model normal system behavior and detect
anomalies, while the second utilizes Fast Fourier Transformation (FFT) to
analyze the frequency domain characteristics of HPC data. This dual-layer
approach allows the framework to distinguish ransomware from benign
high-computation processes, such as disk encryption programs, with

--- Strana 175 ---
enhanced precision. The system achieves this by training the autoencoders
on normal system behaviors and correlating reconstruction errors for
anomaly detection. Furthermore, RATAFIA introduces a correlation module
that uses prestored templates of benign disk encryption processes to
minimize false positives, ensuring reliable ransomware detection without
requiring kernel modifications. This methodology showcases the effective
application of LSTM-based deep learning models for real-time, lightweight,
and robust security monitoring (Figs. 5.1 and 5.2).
Fig. 5.1 Architecture of IC integrating an on-chip trust evaluation module

--- Strana 176 ---
Fig. 5.2 A deep temporal model
The paper discussed in this section explores the application of various
deep learning models to address a range of hardware security issues using
innovative approaches. Compared to traditional detection methods, which
often rely on predefined algorithms or rule-based anomaly detection driven
by expert predictions, deep learning techniques offer significant advantages.
Deep learning models can automatically learn and extract meaningful
features directly related to security concerns, minimizing the need for
extensive human intervention. This capability allows for more accurate,
adaptable, and efficient detection of threats.
By leveraging deep learning, hardware security systems can swiftly and
intelligently respond to potential vulnerabilities or attacks, enhancing their
ability to safeguard target hardware systems. This automated and data-
driven approach not only improves detection accuracy but also reduces the
time and resources required for manual analysis, making it a powerful tool
in the fight against evolving hardware security threats.
5.4 CNN-Based Flooding DoS Detection and
Localization in NoCs

--- Strana 177 ---
This section demonstrates a deep learning-based framework for detecting
security issues in NoCs, with a particular focus on denial-of-service (DoS)
attacks, while the concept of DoS attacks was detailed in the previous
chapter, which emphasizes flooding attacks as one of the most pervasive
threats in NoCs. As such, this method prioritizes not only detecting these
attacks but also localizing their sources within the NoC.
Despite the growing interest in machine learning for hardware security,
the use of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) in this context remains
rare. To the date, CNNs have not been previously applied to NoC security.
This case study leverages CNN models for detecting and localizing
flooding attacks in NoCs, marking a significant advancement in the use of
deep learning for hardware security. By addressing this critical gap, we aim
to contribute a novel approach that enhances the robustness and reliability
of NoC systems against malicious threats.
5.4.1 Overview of the Case Study
Inspired by computer vision techniques such as pattern recognition and
semantic segmentation, a novel approach was designed to analyze runtime
global features of all routers in NoCs by visualizing them as image frames.
This approach transforms the problem of anomaly detection and
localization into an object detection and segmentation task, which is
particularly suitable for NoCs with regular topologies like 2D meshes or
even extended to 3D mesh configurations. We used the Gem5 simulator to
extract virtual channel occupancy (VCO) and buffer operation counts
(BOC) data from the input ports of routers in a 16×16 NoC at specific
runtime instances. The presence of a DoS flooding attack is clearly marked
by anomalously high local feature values in these frames. Additionally, the
BOC matrix frames reveal abnormal routing patterns, providing critical
insights into attack pathways. These findings validate the potential of
employing CNN models to effectively detect and localize anomalies in
NoCs. This framework consists of three core stages:
1. DoS Detector (CNN Classification)
 
2. DoS Profile Localizer (CNN Segmentation)
 

--- Strana 178 ---
3. Victim and Attacker Localization (multi-frame fusion (MFF)
combined with table-like method (TLM))
 
The framework operates as follows:
1. Periodically, the detector processes sampled VCO feature frames from
2–4 directions of each router, provided no previous attack was detected. 
2. If the detector identifies any anomalous frames, the corresponding
BOC feature frames are collected and sent to the segmentation model-
based localizer for detailed analysis. The input data at this stage can be
1–4 frames which represent the feature for different directions, aiming
to a higher-accurate flooding profiling.
 
3. The localizer reconstructs the attack routes by merging segmented
frames, identifying all victim routers. The TLM-based algorithm then
accurately determines the source of the attacks by pinpointing the
malicious nodes. This algorithm has the minimum computation and
latency cost to achieve a fast response.
 
4. The system promptly resumes the next round of VCO sampling and
detection/localization, ensuring rapid identification of any remaining
anomalies until no abnormal frames persist.
 
This framework ensures efficient and systematic detection and
localization of anomalies in NoCs, leveraging the power of CNNs for high
accuracy and scalability in managing complex network security challenges.
5.4.2 DoS Detector: CNN Classification
To detect DoS flooding attacks in NoCs, a CNN classification model was
trained using VCO features. The reasoning behind selecting VCO as a key
indicator is elaborated in Sect. 5.5. The model requires input frames
representing the four possible directional ports of routers in NoCs: east (E),
north (N), west (W), and south (S), as illustrated in Fig. 5.3. Central routers
in a 2D mesh NoC possess four directional ports, edge routers have three,
and corner routers are limited to two. Additionally, edge routers lack input
ports connected to external traffic. As a result, the input frame for the
system is structured as an R × (R−1) matrix, where R represents the row

--- Strana 179 ---
count of the 2D mesh NoC. The reason for having R-1 rows in the input
image is that routers at the edges of the NoC have at most three ports, as
there are no external connections beyond the NoC boundary. Since the VCO
value corresponds to each port, the correct size for the input frames is
R × (R−1). VCO values dynamically adjust to runtime traffic loads,
increasing when virtual channels are heavily utilized, making them suitable
for detecting anomalous traffic patterns.

--- Strana 180 ---

--- Strana 181 ---
Fig. 5.3 CNN classification model
To achieve a balance between hardware resource efficiency and
detection precision, a lightweight CNN architecture was developed, as
depicted in Fig. 5.3. The architecture comprises an input layer (gray block),
a convolutional layer with ReLU activation (pink block), a max-pooling
layer to reduce dimensionality (blue block), a flatten layer (orange block) to
transform the data, and a dense output layer (green block). During the
training phase, the model processes directional input frames, learning
critical features by optimizing its weights and biases. These features allow
the model to generalize and make accurate predictions when it encounters
similar traffic patterns during operation. By maintaining a compact design,
this model ensures effective real-time attack detection while minimizing
hardware overhead, making it a practical solution for NoC security.
5.4.3 DoS Localizer: CNN Segmentation
Another CNN model, specifically designed for segmentation tasks, has been
trained to process BOC frames collected alongside VCO frames when
abnormalities are detected. Leveraging deep learning-based semantic
segmentation techniques, such as those popularized by the U-Net model in
medical imaging, this segmentation model aims to localize flooding attacks
within the NoC and reconstruct the entire attacking route. The high
precision offered by CNN segmentation models makes them ideal for this
task.
The input layer of the DoS localizer, as illustrated in Fig. 5.4, receives
abnormal “E || N || W || S” frames, representing input from the four
possible directions of the routers. This selective input strategy minimizes
computational overhead while maintaining effectiveness. The feature
frames, representing buffer operation counts, capture the number of reads
and writes at each input port of the routers, providing a detailed snapshot of
network activity.

--- Strana 182 ---

--- Strana 183 ---
Fig. 5.4 CNN segmentation model
The segmentation model, shown in the Fig. 5.4, consists of a compact
yet efficient architecture: an input layer, two convolutional layers, and an
output layer. The first and second convolutional layers progressively
abstract key spatial and operational features from the input frames. These
layers are followed by the output layer, which generates segmentation
maps, highlighting regions of abnormal behavior. The model’s training
process is guided by dice accuracy, which iteratively adjusts parameters to
minimize discrepancies between the predicted segmentation results and the
ground truth.
While additional convolutional layers could further enhance
segmentation precision, they would also significantly increase the hardware
overhead. To balance accuracy and hardware resource constraints, the
model architecture can be dynamically tuned, ensuring adaptability to
different application scenarios. Ultimately, the segmentation results are
passed to subsequent components of the detection framework, enabling
precise localization of attackers and victims in the NoC.
5.4.4 DoS Attack Path, Victims, and Attacker Localization
The first critical step in attacker localization is reconstructing the flooding
attack routes, known as routing-path victims (RPV). This process is guided
by Algorithm Fig. 5.5 and visually detailed in Fig. 5.6. Victim localization
integrates the multi-frame fusion (MFF) technique, enhanced by an optional
victim complementing enhancement (VCE) function. The VCE function
refines and completes RPV localization under specific conditions, making it
highly effective when initial detection accuracy is sufficient. Before
applying the MFF technique, segmentation results are preprocessed into a
standard binary 16×16 matrix format through binarization and padding. In
the final MFF result frame, any node with a pixel value of 1 is identified as
a victim. Drawing inspiration from the RPV deduction strategy in [14], the
VCE function employs a pseudo-source ID, adjacent to the attacker, and a
specific destination ID under the reverse XY-routing algorithm to deduce all
RPVs between these points. This ensures a more accurate and complete
identification of attack paths.

--- Strana 184 ---
Fig. 5.5 Localization algorithm

--- Strana 185 ---
Fig. 5.6 DoS attacking router and victim localization: Multi-frame fusion
The final stage in the framework is attacker localization. By leveraging
the XY-routing algorithm, followed by both attackers and regular traffic, the
framework identifies specific attack patterns. Using the directional insights
from feature frames, a table-like method (TLM) was developed to
enumerate single or multi-attacker scenarios for each victim. The TLM
structure, detailed in Fig. 5.7, systematically lists all detected abnormalities,

--- Strana 186 ---
attack patterns, and directional frame combinations. Each row outlines
identified attack routes, predicted attacker numbers, and directional
abnormalities (e.g., E=1 signifies anomalies in data from east input ports).
The results, located between rows 4 and 7, include attacker IDs calculated
through a combination of abnormal input port directions and the XY-routing
algorithm.

--- Strana 187 ---

--- Strana 188 ---
Fig. 5.7 TLM algorithm
For example, if a single attacker floods from the east, the framework
would detect an abnormal east VCO feature frame. With the directional
information from “east,” the attacker is pinpointed as being to the right of
the victim. Their ID is then computed as the maximum RPV ID in the east
direction plus 1. The TLM framework optimizes the localization process to
minimize detection rounds, efficiently localizing all attackers in as few
cycles as possible. For simple attack patterns, such as single- or dual-
attacker scenarios, the framework handles localization in a single round.
However, more complex scenarios involving three or more attackers may
require multiple rounds. The approach ensures robustness by systematically
accounting for all potential attacker configurations while maintaining high
efficiency and accuracy.
5.5 Feature Selection
Earlier work, such as [15], utilized average link utilization to train models
sensitive to DoS attacks. While effective in detecting anomalies, this
approach lacked granularity and directional detail, making it insufficient for
localized identification of attack routes. To overcome these limitations, this
approach incorporates VCO as a dynamic feature from the input ports of
routers. VCO captures real-time information about the occupancy ratio of
virtual channels, expressed as a floating-point value between 0 and 1. To
provide a more cumulative perspective, we also include BOC, which record
the total number of buffer write and read operations, offering deeper
insights into sustained traffic behavior.
The suitability of VCO and BOC for different tasks—detection and
localization—is evaluated through design considerations and empirical
results. VCO proves to be highly effective for detection tasks due to its
instantaneous nature and the ability to process it without normalization. Its
floating-point format allows for seamless identification of anomalies even
in high-traffic scenarios. However, VCO has limitations in attack
localization because it does not provide a full view of the attacking routes,
as illustrated in Fig. 5.8. On the other hand, BOC offers a complete and
detailed perspective, excelling in localizing attacks with higher accuracy.
Yet, its integer-based format necessitates normalization to ensure
consistency across different workloads and configurations.

--- Strana 189 ---
Fig. 5.8 Localization examples (on STP benchmark)
To strike a balance between computational efficiency and performance,
the framework adopts a hybrid approach: VCO is used for the detection
stage, leveraging its real-time insights without requiring normalization,
thereby minimizing hardware complexity. For the localization phase, BOC
is employed after normalization, ensuring accurate reconstruction of attack
routes. This dual-feature strategy optimizes accuracy for both tasks while
maintaining a manageable computational load. Additionally, for lighter
workloads, such as those from PARSEC, VCO can effectively handle both
detection and localization, reducing the need for feature switching. The
figure highlights how VCO effectively pinpoints congestion areas, while
BOC maps the full attack path, ensuring reliable and precise detection and
localization.
To tackle the detection and localization tasks, we initially selected a
straightforward CNN architecture due to its widespread use in hardware
accelerators and its demonstrated effectiveness in similar applications.
While binarized neural network (BNN) models are known for their low

--- Strana 190 ---
computational overhead and strong performance in specific domains [16],
we encountered challenges in training BNN models on the VCO and BOC
feature sets. The training process proved less stable compared to CNNs,
resulting in inconsistent outcomes.
5.6 CNN Model Design and Selection
We further explored various CNN architectures, experimenting with
adjustments such as increasing the number of convolutional filters and
adding layers to enhance model capacity. Although these modifications
yielded slight improvements in accuracy, the additional computational and
power costs far exceeded the performance gains, making these approaches
impractical for hardware implementation. Based on this evaluation, we
adopted two lightweight CNN models, each designed with minimal
complexity to balance performance and efficiency. Specifically, these
models feature only eight convolutional kernels per layer, a design choice
that ensures reliable accuracy without imposing significant hardware
overhead. This architecture, as illustrated in Figs. 5.3 and 5.4, effectively
supports both detection and localization tasks while maintaining a low
resource footprint, aligning with the requirements of NoC-based systems.
For larger NoC scales like 32 × 32, this case study is using the MobileNet
architecture as the CNN model structure to improve localization accuracy.
However, for smaller sizes, such as 8 × 8 to 16 × 16, the current CNN
structure offers a better balance between accuracy and hardware overhead.
5.7 Experiment Setup and Result Evaluation
Effectiveness is verified through quantitative analysis based on
experimental results. This section presents the experimental setup, including
the simulation platform, hardware configuration, dataset preparation,
evaluation metrics, detection and localization performance, and assessment
of hardware resource overhead.
5.7.1 Experiment Setup
The threat model and framework were initially implemented using the
Garnet [17] interconnection model integrated within the Gem5 simulator
[18]. A 16×16 Mesh-XY NoC topology was constructed, featuring one

--- Strana 191 ---
VNET and default virtual channels, representing the largest scale explored
in this research area to date. To evaluate the framework and the flooding
DoS model, six synthetic traffic pattern (STP) benchmarks and three
PARSEC workloads were employed. However, due to limitations in the
Gem5 simulator, PARSEC workloads were only executed on a smaller 8×8
NoC. The system was clocked at 2 GHz, with feature sampling intervals set
at every 1000 cycles for STP and every 100,000 cycles for PARSEC. It is
worth noting that shorter monitoring cycles could be implemented in
higher-frequency systems, depending on the specific security sensitivity of
the SoC.
A global performance monitoring system was designed to collect
datasets, simulating 18 unique attack scenarios under a flooding injection
rate (FIR) of 0.8 across the six STP and three PARSEC benchmarks,
resulting in a total of 162 datasets. From these, 12,960 feature frames were
extracted from the four directional input ports of the routers. With each
frame comprising 256 pixels, the final dataset amounted to an impressive
3.3 million pixel values. To train the machine learning models, the
TensorFlow 2.0 framework was used for offline training of lightweight
CNN-based detection and localization models.
To evaluate the hardware overhead of the method, lightweight CNN
detection and localization accelerators were synthesized, and ProNoC-
generated NoC architectures [19] were used. The framework was tested for
both DoS detection and localization tasks using each of the two feature sets:
VCO and BOC (as detailed in Tables 5.1, 5.2, 5.3 and 5.4). These
experiments were conducted to identify the most effective feature
combination. Ultimately, the results of using the combined VCO+BOC
feature set are summarized in Tables 5.5 and 5.6, showcasing the robustness
and scalability of the solution.

5.7.2 Evaluation Metrics
To assess the performance of the model employed, four widely used
evaluation metrics are employed: precision, recall, accuracy, and F1 score.
These metrics provide a comprehensive understanding of the model’s
ability to correctly classify both positive and negative samples.
Precision measures the proportion of correctly predicted positive
samples among all samples that are predicted as positive. It reflects how
accurate the model is when it predicts a positive outcome. The formula is
given in Eq. 5.1:
(5.1)
Here, TP (true positives) refers to the number of correctly predicted
positive samples, and FP (false positives) refers to the number of negative
samples that are incorrectly predicted as positive.
Recall represents the proportion of actual positive samples that are
correctly predicted by the model. It indicates the model’s ability to identify
all relevant positive cases. The formula is shown in Eq. 5.2:
Precision =
TP
TP+FP

--- Strana 194 ---
(5.2)
Here, FN (false negatives) refers to the number of positive samples that
were incorrectly predicted as negative.
Accuracy evaluates the overall correctness of the model. It calculates
the proportion of all correctly predicted samples, including both positive
and negative classes, over the total number of samples. The formula is
provided in Eq. 5.3:
(5.3)
Here, TN (true negatives) represents the number of correctly predicted
negative samples. Although accuracy gives a general overview of
performance, it may be misleading in imbalanced datasets.
F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall. It is particularly
useful when both false positives and false negatives carry significant
consequences. The formula is given in Eq. 5.4:
(5.4)
A higher F1 score indicates that the model achieves a good balance
between precision and recall, which is important for applications where
identifying true positives while minimizing errors is essential.
5.7.3 Detection Performance
The detection capabilities of the CNN models using the VCO feature are
summarized in the left portion of each column in Table 5.1. For synthetic
traffic pattern (STP) benchmarks, the CNN achieves an average detection
accuracy of 0.98 and precision of 0.99, as highlighted in the last column. In
contrast, detection performance for PARSEC benchmarks shows slightly
reduced metrics, with an average accuracy of 0.93 and precision of 0.96.
The BOC feature demonstrates stronger detection performance, as
presented in Table 5.3, with average accuracies consistently reaching or
exceeding 0.99 and precision of 1 across both STP and PARSEC workloads.
Although the VCO feature underperforms slightly compared to BOC, it
remains the preferred choice for detection tasks due to its instantaneous
values, which eliminate the need for normalization, and its lower memory
overhead compared to the accumulated nature of BOC values.
5.7.4 Localization Performance
Recall =
TP
TP+FN
Accuracy =
TP+TN
TP+TN+FP+FN
F1 = 2×Precision×Recall
Precision+Recall

--- Strana 195 ---
Localization tasks present notable differences in performance between the
VCO and BOC features. For STP benchmarks, the VCO feature
underperforms in localization, with an average accuracy gap of 0.45
compared to BOC, as indicated in the right side of each column in Tables
5.1 and 5.2. The BOC feature, on the other hand, proves highly effective for
localization tasks, achieving average accuracies of 0.973 and 0.917 in STP
and PARSEC workloads, respectively, as shown in Tables 5.3, 5.4, 5.5 and
5.6. The enhanced clarity of attacking routes in BOC data is exemplified in
Fig. 5.8, where BOC matrices highlight abnormal routes with greater
precision.
Localization performance on the PARSEC benchmarks benefits from
the lower traffic density during the region of interest (ROI) period, where
computational activity is higher and network congestion is reduced. This
scenario enables the framework to effectively detect and localize flooding
denial-of-service (FDoS) attacks within the ROI. Notably, for PARSEC
workloads, the potential to utilize VCO for simultaneous detection and
localization emerges as a viable scheme, particularly for less traffic-
intensive scenarios, where its computational efficiency aligns well with
workload demands. This adaptive feature selection ensures the framework
remains flexible and resource efficient across diverse workloads.
5.7.5 Hardware Overhead and Related Work Comparison
Two lightweight CNN accelerators were meticulously designed with a
pipeline architecture using three convolutional kernels, prioritizing minimal
logic utilization. These accelerators were evaluated for hardware overhead
on a standalone NoC, comprising routers, network interfaces, and links, to
eliminate inaccuracies from SoC tiles. As shown in the hardware overhead
trend (Fig. 5.9), the models in this case study exhibit overheads of 1.9% on
8x8 NoCs and just 0.45% on 16x16 NoCs, indicating a significant 76.3%
reduction as the NoC scale increases. This decreasing trend highlights
approach’s efficiency and scalability, making it adaptable for various SoC
demands.

--- Strana 196 ---
Fig. 5.9 Hardware overhead decreasing with larger NoCs
In comparison to previous works, such as the perceptron model in [20]
and the SVM-based model in [21], this case study demonstrates superior
scalability and efficiency, achieving 42.4% less overhead than [20] while
maintaining comparable detection accuracy. Table 5.7 summarizes the
comparative performance of DoS detection and localization techniques
across varying NoC scales, with the framework achieving detection
precision of 98.5% and localization precision of 99.3% for 16x16 NoCs.
This significantly outperforms other models that either lack scalability or
incur higher hardware overhead.

The framework’s ability to adapt to realistic workloads is further
highlighted by its exceptional performance in PARSEC benchmarks. Unlike
other works that primarily evaluate under synthetic traffic patterns, the

--- Strana 197 ---
approach demonstrates a robust capability to detect and localize flooding
attacks in computationally intensive workloads, where the communication
bottlenecks are more prominent. By focusing on both detection precision
and efficient hardware implementation, the framework establishes itself as a
state-of-the-art solution, particularly excelling in scenarios demanding
stringent NoC communication performance.
5.8 Conclusions
This chapter presents a comprehensive security framework in NoC, a robust
deep learning-based framework designed for flooding denial-of-service
(FDoS) detection and localization, incorporating a finely tuned FDoS
model. The framework leverages two CNN models, one for feature frame
classification (DoS detector) and another for segmentation (DoS profile
localizer), ensuring effective and accurate anomaly detection. Additionally,
it introduces advanced techniques such as multi-frame fusion (MFF), victim
completing enhancement (VCE), and a table-like method (TLM) to achieve
precise attacker and victim localization.
It demonstrates exceptional performance, achieving a detection
accuracy of 95.8% and a localization accuracy of 91.7% on a 16 × 16 mesh
NoC, with nearly 99% precision in both tasks, rivalling state-of-the-art
approaches. A notable advantage of scheme in this case study is its
scalability and efficiency, reducing hardware overhead by 76.3% when
scaling from 8 × 8 to 16 × 16 NoCs. For larger NoCs, such as those with
over 32 × 32 nodes, we will incorporate a MobileNet-based CNN model to
enhance localization accuracy while maintaining an estimated hardware
overhead of less than 2.5%. This scalable and efficient design ensures the
framework’s applicability to modern, high-performance, and large-scale
NoC systems.
