--- Strana 201 ---
6.1 Introduction
We have extensively discussed the CNN-based security countermeasures for
NoC security in Chap. 5, demonstrating their significant potential in detecting
abnormal data while achieving a commendable balance between security
performance and hardware overhead. These insights underscore CNN’s
suitability for addressing specific challenges in NoC security. However, CNN
models represent just one facet of the machine learning landscape. Many
other machine learning models, with their unique strengths and capabilities,
remain to be explored for conducting security tasks in NoC contexts.
In this chapter, we highlight two representative works that effectively
employ diverse machine learning models in NoC security. These examples
illustrate how combining different models can outperform single-model
approaches by leveraging their complementary strengths. Furthermore, the
strategic utilization of different models at various stages of data processing
can ensure swift and efficient responses once an attack is detected. Such
dynamic approaches not only enhance detection accuracy but also optimize
resource utilization.
Building upon these insights, we discuss a case study that shows a novel
secure NoC microarchitecture designed to integrate more advanced machine
learning models, particularly those excelling in time-series analysis. By
incorporating these models, the architecture aims to improve anomaly
detection capabilities, enabling real-time responses and enhanced adaptability
to evolving security threats. This chapter not only showcases the versatility of
machine learning models in NoC security but also sets the stage for further
innovations in secure hardware design.

--- Strana 202 ---
6.2 Chapter Overview
In Sect. 6.3 Scheme 1: Cascaded Machine Learning Model for NoC Security,
we introduced a cascaded machine learning approach designed to mitigate
general DoS attacks in NoC systems. In Sect. 6.4: Graph-Based Early
Defense Scheme for NoCs, an advanced graph-based defense mechanism is
introduced for real-time detection and mitigation of refined flooding DoS
attacks in NoCs. In Sect. 6.5, it concludes with a summary of insights into the
integration of machine learning models for NoC security and mentions
directions for future research.
6.3 Scheme 1: Cascaded Machine Learning Model
for NoC Security
6.3.1 Extensive DoS Attacks
DoS attacks are common attack methods in NoC systems, aimed at exploiting
shared resources to block the transmission of legitimate traffic [1]. Attackers
exhaust the target system’s resources by injecting a large number of request
packets or malicious packets, thereby preventing legitimate users from
receiving service. In NoC environments, DoS attacks are typically achieved
by injecting unnecessary traffic [2], occupying communication bandwidth
[1], or blocking routers.
Detecting and preventing DoS attacks in NoC systems is crucial,
especially in real-time and safety-critical applications. Research has shown
that DoS attacks can have severe consequences for network transmission,
primarily by causing significant performance degradation [3]. Malicious
third-party IPs (M3PIPs) can send a large volume of packets to the network,
interfering with the availability of on-chip resources, which may lead to a
severe drop in system performance. Additionally, DoS attacks can also
involve multiple infected IPs attacking one or more components
simultaneously, exacerbating the impact of the attack [1]. In multicore SoC
systems based on NoCs, DoS attacks caused by malicious IP cores may
increase packet transmission delays, leading to task deadline violations [1].
As the communication backbone of multiprocessor SoCs (MPSoCs),
NoCs are particularly vulnerable to DoS attacks because multiple cores and
IP blocks share critical resources such as communication links, router buffers,
and arbitration units [4]. Attackers can exploit these shared resources to

--- Strana 203 ---
prevent task completion or degrade system performance. Common
implementations of DoS attacks on NoC systems include traffic diversion
attacks, traffic loop attacks, and flooding attacks.
 
(a) Traffic Diversion (TD) Attack
When a NoC router is subjected to a traffic diversion (TD) attack and is
located along the packet transmission path, the destination address of
the packet may change, resulting in packet loss or misdelivery. For
example, Fig. 6.1a shows a 6 × 6 Mesh_XY router topology, where the
source router is R0 and the destination is R34. If router 22 (R22), which
lies along the path between the source and the destination, is attacked by
a TD attack, the packet will first reach router R22. Following the NoC’s
routing rules, the packet will then be incorrectly forwarded to router
R23.
Fig. 6.1 Three DoS attack scenarios
 
(b) Routing Loop (RL) Attack
When a NoC router is subjected to a routing loop (RL) attack, the
attacked router will send the packets back to the source router, resulting
in packet loss and resource consumption in the NoC network.
Additionally, the attacked router will lose the ability to transmit packets
to other routers. For example, Fig. 6.1b shows a 6 × 6 Mesh_XY router
topology with a uniform random traffic pattern. When router R0 is
attacked, all packets injected from router R0 will be redirected back to
R0.
 

--- Strana 204 ---
(c) Flooding Attack
In a NoC, one or more routers may be simultaneously attacked and
transmit packets to a designated router, which is known as a flooding
attack. A flooding attack causes redundant packets to appear along the
transmission path, leading to NoC congestion and packet loss or
misdelivery. For example, as shown in Fig. 6.1 2(c), when routers R0,
R5, R30, and R35 are simultaneously attacked, their traffic pattern will
no longer follow the predefined uniform random but will instead
uniformly send packets to the designated router (e.g., R15).
 
6.3.2 Correlation Analysis and Attribute Selection
Selecting the appropriate NoC hardware performance attributes as the dataset
is the first crucial step in developing a supervised machine learning model.
We selected attributes closely related to NoC performance from the Gem5
computer architecture simulator, including packets injected, packets received,
average packet queueing latency, average packet network latency, average
packet latency, average link utilization, average hops, and average power. To
reduce redundant attributes and consequently lower model complexity while
improving computational efficiency, we performed correlation analysis using
Pearson’s correlation coefficient and generated a correlation heatmap. Figure
6.2 shows the results of the correlation analysis for the selected features. We
observed that packets injected and packets received have a high correlation,
so we decided to keep only one of them. Similarly, average packet queueing
latency, average packet network latency, and average packet latency are
highly correlated, and we retained one of these. Furthermore, average link
utilization and average hops are strongly correlated, so we chose to keep one
of them. Additionally, due to the high correlation between average link
utilization and both packets injected and packets received, we ultimately
selected average link utilization and average packet latency as the input
features for the machine learning model (Fig. 6.3).

--- Strana 205 ---
Fig. 6.2 Correlation heatmap
Fig. 6.3 Extracting features from a mesh NoC

--- Strana 206 ---
6.3.3 Cascaded Model Scheme
For the three classic DoS attacks mentioned above, we introduce a case study
that shows a machine learning-based cascaded detection-classification model
for their detection and classification. Inspired by the field of computer vision,
we adopt the basic idea of first detection [5] and then classification for NoC
attacks. This approach not only improves detection efficiency and reduces
computational load but also gradually enhances classification accuracy.
As shown in Fig. 6.4, the cascaded model scheme employs support vector
machine (SVM) for the detection phase. SVM is effective at finding
hyperplanes for classification in high-dimensional data, particularly well
suited for complex, nonlinear datasets. This makes it an ideal choice for NoC
attack detection. For attack classification, we choose K-nearest neighbors
(KNN), which is advantageous due to its simplicity, intuitive computation,
and the absence of a training process. KNN classifies data by calculating the
distance between test samples and training samples, making it suitable for
handling high-dimensional and complex datasets. Since KNN does not rely
on assumptions about data distribution, it can flexibly handle various types of
data and quickly process new samples. In real-time NoC attack classification
applications, KNN provides fast classification and adapts to different attack
patterns, especially when dealing with small-scale datasets or situations
where boundaries between features are not clearly defined.
Fig. 6.4 Cascaded ML models
Through this cascaded model, KNN offers higher classification accuracy
and real-time performance in the classification phase, ensuring the system’s
efficiency and robustness when facing multiple DoS attacks.

--- Strana 207 ---
6.3.4 Experiment Results and Evaluation
In this experiment, we used the Gem5 architecture simulator to construct a
NoC system consisting of 64 routers and 64 cores, with a Mesh_XY topology
that offers high scalability and balance. To ensure the comprehensiveness and
realism of the experiment, we set the injection rate to 0.02 and selected four
typical traffic patterns (uniform random, tornado, shuffle, and bit reverse) as
benchmarks. The simulation ran for 100,000 cycles, and data was collected
every 100 cycles to obtain the average link utilization and average packet
latency for different traffic patterns. Additionally, three types of DoS attacks
including traffic diversion, traffic loop, and flooding were injected, which
were implemented by modifying the “RoutingUnit.cc” file in the Garnet
module, in order to test the performance of the NoC under different attack
scenarios.
During the dataset preparation process, we selected average link
utilization and average packet latency under different traffic patterns as
training attributes and applied normalization to reduce the impact of
dimensional differences. Next, we constructed training and test datasets for
the SVM detector, with two labels, “normal” and “attacked,” and for the
KNN classifier, we created four labels, “normal,” “TD,” “RL,” and
“flooding.” To enhance the realism of the simulation and reduce assumptions,
we built a synthetic mixed dataset that combines different types of attacks
and normal data. The purpose of this approach is to make the simulation
closer to the complex scenarios found in the real world. In reality, network
attacks may occur in multiple types simultaneously, so using only a single
attack pattern or normal traffic pattern in the simulation might not fully
reflect actual situations. By mixing different attack types and traffic patterns,
we can better simulate possible attack scenarios, reducing the reliance on
idealized assumptions. This improves the diversity and representativeness of
the dataset, making the model training more realistic. Finally, we validated
the framework using the synthetic mixed dataset, which consisted of HT-
infected and normal data, across all benchmarks (a total of 16,000 data
points) to account for any potential attack scenarios. The validation results
were evaluated using a unified and formal performance matrix.
We first evaluated the performance of the SVM detector. As shown in
Fig. 6.5, the SVM detector achieved a precision between 85 and 91% across
all five traffic patterns, with recall fluctuating between 85 and 88%. This
indicates a good balance between precision and recall, and it also overcomes

--- Strana 208 ---
the traditional limitation of requiring both high precision and high recall in a
single machine learning model.
Fig. 6.5 SVM detection results
Next, we evaluated the performance of the KNN classifier. As shown in
Fig. 6.6, the KNN classifier achieved high precision and recall across all five
traffic patterns, with excellent balance. The performance evaluation of the
uniform random pattern is particularly noteworthy, as it most closely
resembles the behavior of actual hardware NoCs transmitting random
packets, triggering all three types of attacks. As observed, the KNN classifier
performed best under the uniform random traffic pattern, achieving the
expected precision and excellent recall.

--- Strana 209 ---
Fig. 6.6 KNN classification results
Finally, we tested the precision of the three attacks under different traffic
pattern benchmark cases, as shown in Fig. 6.7. It can be observed that the
performance of the KNN model varies under different traffic patterns.
Fig. 6.7 Precision results of attack classification
For example, the precision of the RL attack is highest at 100% in uniform
random, but around 65% in Tornado. This result indicates that the ML model
exhibits varying adaptability across different application scenarios. However,

--- Strana 210 ---
we found that the trained ML model shows strong applicability in uniform
random and the fully mixed dataset, which closely simulate hardware NoC
applications, achieving high detection accuracy.
To validate the effectiveness and innovation of the cascaded model
designed in this case study, several related classic studies were compared, as
shown in Table 6.1.

In terms of detection and classification methods, compared to other single
machine learning models, we cascaded SVM and KNN models to achieve
accurate attack detection and classification. Additionally, we created a hybrid
dataset that includes all attack types, rather than handling each attack
individually. The approach achieved 91% accuracy in the detection phase,
with 100% accuracy in classifying RL and TD attacks, and an average
accuracy of 93% for flooding attacks.
6.4 Scheme 2: Graph-Based Early Defense Scheme
for NoCs
In this section, we place a strong emphasis on flooding denial-of-service
(FDoS) attacks, as they are among the most prevalent and stealthy threats
targeting NoC systems. FDoS attacks have significant implications, as they

--- Strana 211 ---
can disrupt critical communication paths and degrade the overall performance
of NoC architectures.
To address these concerns, we begin by introducing a more refined FDoS
threat model, building upon the foundational concepts detailed in Sect. 4.​4.​2.
This advanced model incorporates more realistic and dynamic attack
scenarios, enabling a deeper understanding of the vulnerabilities in NoC
systems.
Following this, we deliver the motivation for leveraging graph theory in
the context of FDoS detection. Graph theory provides a robust framework for
analyzing NoC topologies and communication patterns, making it an ideal
tool for identifying anomalies caused by malicious activities. We also outline
some of the preliminary works that laid the foundation for this approach,
showcasing how these studies informed the design of solution in this case
study.
The comprehensive security detection architecture we developed is
detailed in Sect. 6.4.3. This architecture integrates advanced techniques for
identifying and mitigating FDoS attacks, leveraging both graph-based
methodologies and time-series related machine learning models to achieve
high detection accuracy and scalability.
Finally, we present the results and performance evaluations of the novel
architecture. These results demonstrate its effectiveness in detecting and
localizing FDoS attacks under various scenarios, highlighting its potential for
enhancing NoC security in both academic and industrial applications.
6.4.1 Threat Modelling for Refined Flooding DoS
This study centers on the detection and mitigation of FDoS attacks initiated
by one or more malicious third-party intellectual properties (M3PIPs). These
malicious entities are integrated into the NoC through network interfaces and
routers, as illustrated in Fig. 6.8. The primary attack vector involves the
malicious IP leveraging various methods, such as hardware Trojan (HT)
insertion or malware execution, to launch the FDoS attacks. Consequently,
our focus narrows to the node housing the malicious IP, aiming to achieve
security by identifying and isolating the compromised node.

--- Strana 212 ---
Fig. 6.8 The location of malicious third party IP in NoC system
HTs represent a significant mechanism for realizing malicious IP
functionality. HTs are covert modifications introduced into integrated circuits
during either the design or manufacturing phase. These modifications are
particularly insidious as they can evade traditional testing methods and
remain dormant until triggered. HTs can infiltrate various NoC components,
such as third-party IP cores, network interfaces, or even the ports and buffers
of routers. Once activated, these HTs are capable of flooding the NoC with an
overwhelming volume of data packets, disrupting the normal flow of
communication, creating bottlenecks, and ultimately leading to a DoS
scenario.
By focusing on detecting and isolating nodes compromised by M3PIPs or
HTs, this study aims to ensure the reliability and security of NoC-based
systems. This approach not only addresses the immediate threat of FDoS
attacks but also highlights the broader implications of integrating third-party
IPs into critical hardware infrastructures, emphasizing the need for robust and
proactive security measures in the design and operation of modern NoC
architectures.

--- Strana 213 ---
These flooding attacks inundate the victim with an extraordinarily high
number of continuous packets, leading to severe network congestion,
potential service denial, and the risk of prolonged disruptions impacting
multiple nodes within a NoC system [9]. The assumption is that the flooding
behavior persists for hundreds of cycles at an abnormally high injection rate;
sporadic or fewer flooding packets can typically be managed by the system
and do not result in significant consequences. It is crucial to highlight that
both primary and collateral victims are affected—the primary victim being
the intended target, while collateral victims are routers inadvertently
impacted along the attack path. Our threat model thoroughly examines
diverse DoS scenarios, focusing on those triggered by flooding attacks.
Several definitions and clarifications are outlined below:
1. M3PIP Attackers and Victims: M3PIPs can target a single victim or, in
cases involving multiple M3PIPs, target various victims either
independently or through coordinated efforts.
 
2. Attack Path Profiling: The attack path is characterized by congestion
levels observed at the router’s input units. Multiple routing-path victims
can exist along a single attack path if the attacker is not directly adjacent
to the victim. The framework in this case study is specifically designed
to localize these attack paths and identify all affected victims.
 
3. Attack Strategies: Flooding attack strategies fall into two categories:
collaborative and non-collaborative. Collaborative strategies involve
multiple M3PIPs synchronizing their efforts to attack a single victim,
resembling a distributed DoS attack. Non-collaborative strategies consist
of individual M3PIPs targeting separate victims independently.
 
The patterns of flooding attacks detectable and localizable by the method
are visualized in Fig. 6.9. In scenario (a), a single M3PIP launches an attack
on a single victim. When multiple M3PIPs are involved, they may target
either a single victim or several victims, as depicted in scenarios (b), (c), and
(d). Specifically, scenario (d) demonstrates overlapping attack paths, where
multiple M3PIPs simultaneously target multiple victims, with their routes
partially or completely intersecting. Scenario (c) showcases a coordinated
attack, where two attackers from opposite corners converge on a victim in
another corner. To provide further clarity, Fig. 6.10 illustrates specific
examples of scenarios (a) and (c). Both scenarios begin with the NoC system

--- Strana 214 ---
operating under normal conditions, where each pixel represents the buffer
occupancy for an input port of a router in one direction. Once the flooding
attack is initiated, buffer occupancy values shift dramatically from normal to
highly skewed, even when averaged over a monitoring window of 100
cycles. The sequential frames display the mean occupancy values over the
first, second, and third 100-cycle intervals, respectively. These examples
highlight that not only the intended target suffers but also the intermediate
routers along the attack path experience substantial performance degradation.
Fig. 6.9 Four flooding DoS attack scenarios

--- Strana 215 ---
Fig. 6.10 Two real attack examples on buffer occupancy: scenarios a and c in the Fig. 6.9
As shown in Fig. 6.11, the latency of packets increases drastically
following the activation of the flooding attack, reaching up to 13 times the
normal level under regular workloads. While this specific example involves a
single attacker targeting a single victim, it is clear that simultaneous attacks
involving multiple M3PIPs would result in even more severe traffic
congestion and system instability.

--- Strana 216 ---
Fig. 6.11 The traffic congestion caused by flooding DoS attacks at different packet injection rates, as
reflected by the latency
6.4.2 Graph Theory for Malicious Traffic Detection
Researchers have extensively studied and modelled DoS attacks, focusing
primarily on traffic analysis at the network level to identify anomalies and
potential threats. For instance, the work referenced in [10] explores a novel
approach by utilizing graph-based techniques to evaluate node behavior
across the network, moving beyond the conventional focus on detecting
individual malicious connections. By integrating advanced neural network
and graph convolutional network techniques, this method leverages spatial
feature extraction, temporal profiling, and data preprocessing to classify
network node behaviors with greater precision. This approach aims to
uncover more nuanced insights into security threats, offering a richer
understanding of anomalous traffic patterns and network vulnerabilities.
Graph neural networks (GNNs) are widely recognized for their ability to
effectively represent spatial features by aggregating information from
neighboring nodes and edges, thereby providing a holistic view of message

--- Strana 217 ---
passing and data aggregation within a network. Building on these
capabilities, Zhilong Lu et al. [11] introduced an innovative framework called
graph long short-term memory (graph LSTM), which combines the spatial
proficiency of GNNs with the temporal processing strengths of LSTM
networks. This integrated model is particularly adept at capturing intricate
topological relationships in road networks while simultaneously accounting
for the temporal variations in traffic patterns. The authors highlight the
novelty of their approach in merging GNNs and LSTMs for the specific task
of traffic speed prediction. Utilizing an unweighted directed graph
representation of road networks, the model incorporates encoder-decoder
architecture and message-passing strategies during training. Experimental
results demonstrate that the GNN+LSTM model outperforms conventional
techniques, achieving significantly improved accuracy in traffic prediction.
6.4.3 Traffic Flow and Routing Dependencies Graph in NoCs
NoC architecture is essential for enabling accurate and efficient data
communication in system-on-chip (SoC) environments, utilizing routing
protocols such as the static Mesh-XY algorithm to guide packet movement.
Figure 6.12 illustrates how a 16-tile NoC traffic system can be mapped to a
routing dependency graph, emphasizing the influence of upstream data flow
on downstream channels due to the algorithm’s static characteristics. In this
graph representation, nodes correspond to communication endpoints, while
directed edges signify the routing dependencies between them, establishing
an orderly sequence for packet movement. By framing traffic flow as directed
links within a network, this approach abstracts the complexity of
bidirectional communication into a more streamlined model, allowing for a
detailed analysis of traffic dependencies without overwhelming the
monitoring process.

--- Strana 218 ---
Fig. 6.12 Traffic flow and routing dependencies graph in NoC
To evaluate traffic congestion within a NoC, metrics such as buffer
occupancy (BO) and virtual channel occupancy (VCO) play a pivotal role. As
depicted in Fig. 6.12, these metrics are dynamically determined during
runtime by measuring the proportion of used buffers and virtual channels
relative to their preconfigured capacities. Both metrics exhibit comparable
patterns in reflecting congestion levels, making them interchangeable for
monitoring purposes in subsequent discussions. A noticeable increase in
utilization or packet buildup at a specific router signals significant forwarding
delays, serving as a clear indicator of congestion hotspots within the network.
This approach not only provides an intuitive way to visualize and understand
data flow bottlenecks but also exemplifies a proactive method for identifying
and addressing potential transmission inefficiencies in NoC systems.
6.4.4 Graph-Based Method
The novel secure NoC system architecture integrates three essential
components: the traffic monitor, isolator, and global detector, with the traffic
monitor and isolator embedded within custom routers. This architecture is
specifically designed to detect and neutralize malicious activities originating
from M3PIPs by isolating compromised nodes and attack paths involving
harmful third-party IPs. Operating as an independent security layer, the

--- Strana 219 ---
global detector oversees chip-wide security threats without interfering with
the primary NoC-based MPSoC functionality. Figure 6.13 provides an
architectural overview of the graph-based secure NoC, modelled on a 4 × 4
Mesh NoC system, showcasing its hierarchical structure. This includes
router-level traffic monitors, a standalone global detection unit equipped with
GNN and LSTM accelerators, and router-integrated isolators. The diagram
also illustrates the workflow between components: the traffic monitors
extract localized traffic data and send it to the global detector, which analyzes
it for threats and relays this information to the isolators for targeted security
actions. Employing a centralized global detection unit offers significant area
efficiency compared to decentralized, in-router models. The subsequent
sections explain the design details, microarchitectures, and algorithms that
power these modules.

--- Strana 220 ---

--- Strana 221 ---
Fig. 6.13 Graph-based secure NoC architecture: A custom router consisting of traffic monitor and
isolator, and global detector consisting of GNN accelerator and LSTM accelerator
6.4.4.1 Traffic Monitor
We employed employs buffer occupancy at each router’s input port as its
primary monitored feature, chosen for two key reasons. First, as an
immediate metric, it enables rapid identification of anomalies or attacks.
Second, its association with input buffers makes it simple to measure and
compute. To enhance the analysis with spatial-temporal context, timestamps
are assigned to each frame of feature data. These timestamps, generated by an
integrated counter within the detector, capture runtime system dynamics
while minimizing communication bandwidth between traffic monitors and
the global detector. Only buffer occupancy data is transmitted via 16 bus
groups, streamlining the process.
Each router incorporates a lightweight traffic monitor to track buffer
occupancy, leveraging a low-overhead design. A look-up table is employed to
store and update occupancy values based on buffer indices, eliminating the
need for complex calculations. Since buffer configurations are predefined,
occupancy values can be determined efficiently by referencing the buffer
index. This look-up table approach offers the quickest method for retrieving
values, ensuring efficient operation.
Additionally, calculating the mean value of two buffer occupancy
readings is simplified through a bitwise left shift operation, avoiding the
computational cost of division. The final buffer occupancy values are output
every 100 cycles, as regulated by a local counter, providing a timely and
resource-efficient mechanism for monitoring traffic in the NoC system.
6.4.4.2 Global Detector: GNN+LSTM
The integrated GNN+LSTM architecture was designed for detecting and
classifying traffic congestion in NoC systems by harnessing spatial and
temporal data insights. This section outlines both the conceptual design and
the corresponding hardware implementation. As visualized in Fig. 6.14, the
process involves four key stages:

--- Strana 222 ---

--- Strana 223 ---
Fig. 6.14 The algorithm structure of GNN and LSTM detection model in the global detector
1. Graph Representation: The NoC is abstracted as a graph where nodes
symbolize traffic links and edges denote spatial dependencies. An
adjacency matrix is constructed to map these dependencies, reflecting the
routing hierarchy and directional relationships of data flow.
 
2. Spatial Analysis: Through GNNs, spatial features are extracted from
nodes across consecutive time intervals. Mean pooling aggregates data
from each node and its connected neighbors, providing a snapshot of
localized traffic behavior within the network.
 
3. Temporal Analysis: The aggregated spatial features are processed by
LSTM layers to capture the evolution of traffic patterns over time. This
temporal analysis identifies trends and recurring anomalies in the data
flow, enabling dynamic behavioral modelling.
 
4. Traffic Classification: Temporal outputs from the LSTM layers are
passed to a fully connected layer that categorizes the traffic. The
classifier discerns between normal network operation and congestion-
induced anomalies.
 
This hybrid GNN+LSTM architecture effectively combines localized
spatial insights and temporal flow analysis, making it a robust solution for
identifying traffic anomalies in NoC systems. Its modular design ensures
adaptability to varying NoC configurations and traffic complexities.
The hardware implementation of the framework aligns closely with the
algorithmic stages, as illustrated on the right side of Fig. 6.13. The GNN
module processes the NoC’s graph representation, where nodes symbolize
traffic links and edges denote inter-router dependencies and data flow
pathways (as shown in the top-right of Fig. 6.14). By employing
“embedding,” the GNN reduces multidimensional features of network nodes
into simplified vectors. This embedding process incorporates average
pooling, which merges a node’s intrinsic features with those of its upstream
neighbors, capturing both localized and spatial characteristics. The resulting
adjacency matrix encapsulates the hierarchical relationships between
upstream and downstream nodes, essential for NoC routing mechanisms like
the deterministic Mesh-XY algorithm. This structured representation, as
visualized in Fig. 6.14, provides a clear depiction of traffic flows, enabling

--- Strana 224 ---
efficient analysis of communication patterns and the identification of
anomalous or malicious activity.
(a) GNN Algorithm: The impact of applying GNN processing to virtual
channel occupancy (VCO) data is demonstrated in Fig. 6.15,
showcasing two distinct trends. The blue curve, representing raw VCO
data, exhibits sharp fluctuations with numerous peaks that could
mislead conventional machine learning models relying on static
thresholds, resulting in false positives. On the other hand, the red curve
illustrates VCO data after GNN processing, presenting a smoother
trajectory with reduced peaks while still preserving the crucial upward
trend signaling an attack initiation. Red markers on both curves
highlight checkpoints identified by GNN+LSTM framework and a
baseline model lacking smoothing and temporal analysis. For instance,
checkpoint P0 on the blue curve is mistakenly flagged as abnormal by
models using fixed thresholds, despite being a typical peak unrelated to
any attack. In contrast, the same point on the red curve, refined by GNN
processing, avoids misclassification, demonstrating the GNN’s ability to
eliminate noise and enhance feature clarity for improved detection
accuracy. This comparison underscores the advantages of GNN in
preprocessing NoC data, enabling more reliable anomaly identification.
Fig. 6.15 Demonstration for early detection mechanism
 

--- Strana 225 ---
(b) GNN Hardware Implementation: To implement the GNN model
efficiently, we utilized a systolic processing element (PE) array
architecture [12], as depicted in Fig. 6.13. The interconnect structure of
the PE array was tailored to align with the abstracted graph of traffic
flow nodes presented in Fig. 6.12, ensuring optimized data processing
and transfer efficiency. The GNN accelerator activates upon receiving a
complete graph representation frame, leveraging mean pooling kernels
within the PE array to process the data. The resulting computations are
then stored across three buffer arrays, ready to be accessed by the
LSTM module for subsequent temporal analysis.
 
(c) LSTM Algorithm: The LSTM module is composed of a cell state and
three gates: input, forget, and output. Each gate performs specific tasks,
such as integrating relevant information, discarding unnecessary data,
and generating final predictions. To ensure compactness and efficiency,
three LSTM units share parameters across their hidden layers. These
units play a vital role in analyzing complex temporal sequences, such as
patterns in network traffic, to effectively identify anomalies. The
internal structure and connectivity of these gates, illustrated in the lower
right corner of Fig. 6.14, demonstrate their ability to dynamically
process sequential feature data. Optimized using Python and the
PyTorch framework and implemented through the Gem5 [13] simulator,
the LSTM model employs two sets of weights and biases to handle
input vectors and recurrent hidden states. The design refines 12 neural
network parameters associated with the gates, enabling precise and
adaptive temporal analysis.
 
(d) LSTM Hardware Implementation: The LSTM classification is
executed every 100 cycles, adhering to a predefined schedule, allowing
sufficient time for data preparation in the mean buffer arrays.
Consequently, its operational latency is less critical compared to the
GNN accelerator. To optimize resource efficiency, a five-stage pipeline
structure was implemented for the LSTM module, enabling it to handle
one node from the GNN’s output frame at a time. This design
effectively reduces hardware complexity while maintaining
functionality. The identified anomalies are subsequently fed into the
localization algorithm, which, in tandem with the adjacency matrix,
pinpoints the attacker and informs subsequent actions.
 

--- Strana 226 ---
When applied to real-time feature data, the LSTM model operates
continuously, resembling the functionality of a moving average filter. It
dynamically captures new data for analysis while selectively discarding
outdated information, enabling a seamless and adaptive evaluation of
incoming traffic patterns. Figure 6.15 illustrates LSTM’s performance on
feature data, highlighting its advantages over traditional machine learning
approaches such as SVM, random forest, logistic regression, and decision
trees. Unlike conventional models, which rely on fixed threshold
classifications and lack temporal analysis, LSTM incorporates an advanced
mechanism through its input, forget, and output gates. These gates allow
LSTM to analyze feature data dynamically within a defined temporal
window, minimizing false positives caused by transient peaks that exceed
preset thresholds.
The green and red vertical bars in Fig. 6.15 indicate the detection periods
identified by LSTM. For instance, point P0 on the blue curve within the
green bar, previously misclassified as abnormal by traditional models, is
accurately identified by LSTM as a benign fluctuation. Point P1, missed
entirely by conventional methods, is effectively detected by LSTM,
showcasing its superior sensitivity. Point P2, a significant peak detected by
all models, further demonstrates LSTM’s ability to distinguish genuine
threats from routine traffic variations. Notably, LSTM identifies P1
approximately 100 cycles earlier than P2, a feat unachievable by other
models, emphasizing its early-detection capabilities. This aligns with the
concept of predictive anomaly detection, as discussed in [14], where one-
step-ahead prediction proved essential. In the secure NoC architecture, LSTM
extends this approach by functioning as a multi-step-ahead prediction tool,
significantly enhancing its ability to identify and respond to emerging threats
in real time.
6.4.4.3 Localization Algorithm
The final stage in the operation of the global detector is the localization
algorithm module, activated only when the LSTM model flags an abnormal
classification. Algorithm Fig. 6.16 describes a process involving three key
steps to identify malicious nodes within the NoC traffic graph. First, the
algorithm identifies victim nodes by analyzing signs of upstream congestion.
Next, it applies a breadth-first search (BFS) approach to trace attack paths
originating from these victim nodes, prioritizing routes with significant traffic

--- Strana 227 ---
anomalies. Finally, it pinpoints the source nodes responsible for the attack by
locating the origins of the congested paths.
Fig. 6.16 Algorithm of localizing M3PIPs
Figure 6.17 provides a visual representation of the localization process,
which begins with the detection of congestion in specific traffic nodes. This
detection triggers the localization algorithm to analyze the attack path
systematically. By examining the adjacency matrix that outlines the NoC’s
network topology, the algorithm tracks congestion upstream from the affected
nodes. Using a reverse traversal with BFS, it evaluates traffic loads at each
upstream node to identify those with the highest congestion levels. This
process helps delineate the paths impacted by malicious traffic and attributes
the traffic flow to the flooding attack.

--- Strana 228 ---
Step 1:
Step 2:
Step 3:
Fig. 6.17 M3PIP localization demonstration in 4 × 4 NoC: tile 1 is an attacker; tile 11 is a target
victim
Nodes are classified based on their role in the attack path. Victim nodes,
for example, are characterized by congestion in their own traffic without
corresponding downstream congestion. Conversely, source nodes initiating
the attack are identified by locating the first congested node upstream of a
non-congested area.
As shown in Fig. 6.17a, the algorithm starts by detecting traffic
nodes experiencing congestion, which indicates the likelihood of malicious
activity within the network.
It then identifies “root nodes,” defined as nodes without any
upstream congestion, which are potential sources of the traffic anomalies.
From these root nodes, the algorithm tracks the propagation of congestion
downstream to uncover the impacted paths. The process concludes when a
node without downstream congestion is encountered, designating it as the
attack’s target, as depicted in Fig. 6.17b.
Utilizing the BFS traversal method, the algorithm maps out attack
paths by working upstream from victim nodes, thereby outlining the entire
attack route. Figure 6.17c illustrates this process. BFS evaluates adjacent
nodes at each level, sequentially queuing them for further exploration. This

--- Strana 229 ---
systematic traversal, depicted in Fig. 6.17f, ensures accurate path tracing,
even in scenarios involving multiple simultaneous attacks.
Result
The source IPs of the attackers are identified by pinpointing the
origins of these attack paths, effectively tracing the roots of the DoS attack,
as illustrated in Fig. 6.17d.
Once attackers and their corresponding victims are identified, the secure NoC
system activates a credit manipulation mechanism to isolate the attackers
from the NoC. This mechanism blocks malicious packet injections by cutting
off the attackers’ communication pathways. Additionally, the secure NoC
system continuously monitors the network load and can extend isolation to
additional attackers sharing the same path, ensuring comprehensive
protection against DoS attacks and safeguarding the NoC’s overall
performance.
6.4.4.4 Defense: Credits Interposing Manipulation
In the secure NoC router architecture introduced in Sect. 6.4.4, a credit
interposition mechanism is introduced to isolate potentially malicious third-
party IPs and regulate traffic during DoS attacks. Traditionally, credits in
routers are used to indicate buffer availability and manage data flow, but here,
they are repurposed to enhance security. By assigning a zero-credit value to
untrusted data sources, the system effectively blocks their ability to transmit
packets. This approach not only signals a loss of trust but also halts data
reception from the offending source. Importantly, this enhancement is
seamlessly integrated into the router’s operation, preserving its primary
function of forwarding legitimate data. Furthermore, the isolation mechanism
operates locally, targeting only the port connected to the suspicious IP while
keeping other ports functional, ensuring the uninterrupted routing of normal
traffic.
Figure 6.18 illustrates the isolation circuit embedded within the router, a
key component of the secure NoC security framework. Controlled by an
“iso_control” signal from the global detector, these circuits distinguish
between authorized data and malicious traffic. Upon detecting suspicious
activity, it triggers the isolation mechanism via a single-bit control signal to
the affected router’s isolator. This swift intervention disconnects the
problematic IP from the network, mitigating potential damage with minimal
delay. The design’s simplicity avoids the need for complex modifications to

--- Strana 230 ---
routing algorithms or additional hardware, thereby reducing overhead. This
streamlined approach ensures rapid and efficient network defense while
maintaining the overall integrity and performance of the NoC.
Fig. 6.18 The credits interposing manipulation in routers for M3PIPs isolation
6.4.5 Experiment and Evaluation
To evaluate the effectiveness of the secure NoC system in the case study, we
employed the Garnet NoC simulator, integrated within the Gem5 framework
[13], to simulate network activities over time. The experimental setup,
detailed in Table 6.2, involved generating flooding DoS attacks by a
malicious node that injected excessive, nonessential packets into the NoC,
while synthetic traffic pattern (STP) and PARSEC benchmarks were running.
Each experiment spanned 5 million clock cycles, and the collected data was
split into 70% for training, 20% for validation, and 10% for testing. This data
preparation pipeline is illustrated in the top section of Fig. 6.19.

The figure’s middle section outlines the process for compiling, training,

the Garnet framework for runtime detection evaluation. For hardware-level
assessments, ProNoC [15] was utilized as a versatile simulation platform
tailored for MPSoC architectures. This allowed us to configure and analyze a
16-core system with a mesh topology, enabling detailed simulations of traffic
patterns. The experiments focused on multiple aspects: the classification
accuracy of the detection model, the precision of localization efforts, and the
associated hardware overhead.
The operational workflow and implementation details of the secure NoC

evaluation framework provides insights into the system’s robustness in
detecting and localizing malicious activities while also assessing its hardware
efficiency in practical MPSoC environments.
6.4.5.1 Flooding DoS Detection Evaluation
Accurately detecting network traffic congestion and evaluating binary
classification models are critical for securing NoC systems. This study
introduces the GNN+LSTM framework while also comparing its
performance against several traditional machine learning methods, including
SVM, random forest, logistic regression, decision tree, Gaussian NB, and
perceptron. Key metrics such as test accuracy, precision for Class 0
(representing normal, non-attack states) and Class 1 (indicating attacks),
recall, and F1 score were used to assess the effectiveness of each model.

--- Strana 233 ---
The experimental setup involved testing 100 scenarios with varying
combinations of attackers and victims, allowing for diverse evaluation
conditions. A time-averaging window of 100 clock cycles was applied to
extract traffic behaviors, using the mean buffer occupancy over these cycles
as a representative feature of the network’s status. This approach ensures
detection is performed every 100 cycles rather than at each individual cycle,
balancing security demands with computational efficiency. Table 6.3 provides
a detailed comparison, highlighting the performance of the detection
algorithm alongside the evaluated machine learning models.

The results highlight the secure NoC system’s strong capability to detect
congestion and accurately identify attackers, even in challenging situations
involving multiple attackers and intersecting attack paths. In scenarios where
several sources contribute to network congestion, the system prioritizes
isolating the node causing the most significant disruption. During subsequent
detection cycles, the system continues to identify and disable previously
concealed malicious nodes, effectively reducing the overall impact of the
attack.
In a comparative analysis of detection models, the secure NoC system,
utilizing a GNN+LSTM algorithm, demonstrated superior performance with
a test accuracy of 0.98. Unlike traditional models such as SVMs and random

--- Strana 234 ---
forests, this approach effectively captures spatial relationships and temporal
patterns, making it particularly adept at rapid anomaly detection. This
capability is critical for addressing traffic congestion caused by flooding
attacks, which evolve from normal to congested states. The model excels in
identifying these transitions early, allowing for timely mitigation of potential
disruptions and preventing severe congestion. When benchmarked against
other advanced ML models, including Gaussian NB and random forest, the
GNN+LSTM consistently outperforms in accuracy and other metrics,
highlighting its reliability and robustness. This architecture sets a new
benchmark for analyzing data with complex spatial and temporal
dependencies, offering a valuable framework for future research.
The detection capabilities of the system were further validated using real
workload PARSEC benchmarks during periods of simulated flooding attacks.
As shown in Table 6.4, the system achieved an average accuracy and
precision of 0.99 for class 0 cases but a slightly reduced precision of 0.97 for
class 1 cases. The x264 workload contributed to this reduction due to its
pipelined media processing, which exhibits high data dependencies distinct
from workloads like Black-Scholes and Bodytrack. These differences,
characterized by both high data sharing and extensive data exchange, present
unique challenges for detection accuracy.

6.4.5.2 Malicious Node Localization Performance
We carried out an extensive analysis to evaluate the potential impact of DoS
attacks across all nodes within a network. In a 16-core multiprocessor
system, each node was considered capable of launching up to 15 distinct
malicious activities, targeting each of the remaining nodes as victims. This

--- Strana 235 ---
configuration resulted in the identification of 240 unique attack behavior
patterns for testing. The results demonstrated that the secure NoC system
achieved perfect localization accuracy (100%) for flooding-based DoS
attacks, contingent on the accurate detection of malicious traffic to facilitate
source tracing. These findings validate the effectiveness and reliability of the
framework in identifying and mitigating such security threats.
6.4.5.3 Timing and Hardware Overhead Analysis
Table 6.5 summarizes the average results from detection and localization tests
across 240 distinct attack scenarios. For standard attack patterns without
overlapping, the localization of attackers was completed within an average of
29.1 cycles. In cases where multiple attackers initiated overlapping attack
patterns, the secure NoC system demonstrated enhanced effectiveness. When
faced with multiple attackers, the system prioritized identifying and isolating
the most disruptive attacker first. After isolating the primary source of the
attack, an additional sampling period was employed to detect and neutralize
other hidden malicious nodes along the attack path. As a result, handling
overlapping attack patterns required an average of 53.3 cycles. The overall
mean for detection and localization across all scenarios was 35.1 cycles. This
structured approach ensures thorough identification and isolation of all
attackers, significantly bolstering the network’s security.

6.4.5.4 Comparison with Related Works
Table 6.7 provides a comparative analysis of our introduced secure NoC
system against other solutions that integrate detection and localization
capabilities. Our framework offers a holistic approach to managing DoS
attacks by detecting malicious traffic paths, pinpointing M3PIP attackers, and
isolating them effectively. This comprehensive design allows our system to
outperform related works in both detection and localization accuracy while
maintaining the lowest hardware overhead among the compared approaches.

Although our timing overhead is moderate in comparison, with
localization times slightly exceeding those in [16], our framework
compensates with its robust early detection capabilities. As shown in Fig.
6.15, the system can detect potential attacks hundreds or even thousands of
cycles in advance. This early detection capability minimizes the impact of
extended localization time by ensuring timely responses to security threats.
Such foresight allows for preemptive action, preserving the overall system

--- Strana 237 ---
responsiveness and reliability. Future improvements could focus on further
optimizing localization speed to enhance the system’s overall performance.
6.5 Conclusions
This chapter explored the transformative potential of machine learning in
enhancing the security of NoCs. Building upon CNN-based approaches
discussed earlier, this chapter highlighted two advanced schemes that
integrate diverse machine learning models to address a broader range of NoC
security challenges. The first scheme demonstrated how cascading machine
learning models can improve detection and response to general DoS attacks,
emphasizing the power of combining different algorithms for improved
accuracy and efficiency. The second scheme introduced a graph-based
method, leveraging GNNs and LSTMs, to proactively detect and localize
refined flooding DoS attacks with high precision.
By systematically analyzing threat modelling, proposing innovative
architectures, and rigorously evaluating performance, this chapter
demonstrated the versatility and adaptability of machine learning techniques
in tackling complex NoC vulnerabilities. The integration of graph theory and
advanced time-series models provided a robust framework for early detection
and real-time response to dynamic security threats.
The findings in this chapter underscore the importance of adopting
multilayered machine learning strategies to safeguard NoCs in an era of
increasing cyber threats. These insights pave the way for future research,
emphasizing the need for scalable, efficient, and adaptive security solutions
to meet the growing demands of large-scale NoC systems. Through these
efforts, the foundation is set for designing more resilient hardware
architectures that can preemptively address evolving security challenges.
