Functional testing risks – Post-deployment threats – Security analysis –
Risk assessment – Model archiving security
7.1 Introduction
The deployment of artificial intelligence (AI) models into production
environments marks a critical transition from a theoretical construct to a
functional application, interacting with real-world data and users. This
stage, however, is fraught with a unique set of security vulnerabilities that
can be exploited by malicious actors [1]. As AI systems become more
integrated into critical infrastructure, finance, and autonomous systems, the
consequences of a security breach extend far beyond data loss, posing risks
to physical safety and economic stability [2]. The choices made regarding

--- Strana 242 ---
the software implementation or hardware accelerator implementation each
introduce distinct threat vectors that must be meticulously addressed [3].
This chapter provides a comprehensive review of the security threats
inherent to each stage of the AI production process, from initial data
acquisition to post-deployment archiving. By dissecting both the software
and hardware implementation pipelines, we will explore the multifaceted
attack surface, supported by recent academic literature, and examine a
range of proposed mitigation strategies designed to build more secure and
resilient AI systems.

--- Strana 243 ---
7.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter provides a comprehensive examination of the security threat
landscape across the entire AI system production life cycle, beginning with
a high-level overview of the distinct software and hardware implementation
pathways and their inherent vulnerabilities in Sect. 7.3. The discussion then
proceeds chronologically in Sect. 7.4, first focusing on the AI model
development stage, detailing threats such as data poisoning, backdoor
insertion during model training, and evasion attacks during evaluation.
Subsequently, Sect. 7.5 analyzes the software implementation stage,
exploring risks associated with deploying models on cloud services,
dedicated inference accelerators, and edge devices, including model
extraction, side-channel attacks, and privacy leakage. Attention then shifts
to the complex supply chain threats within the hardware accelerator
implementation pipeline in Sect. 7.6, outlining vulnerabilities that arise
during IP core licensing, SoC integration, fabrication, PCB assembly, and
functional testing, with a particular focus on hardware Trojans. Section 7.7
addresses the post-deployment stage, covering both interactive attacks
during the consumption phase and latent risks, such as intellectual property
theft and vulnerability propagation that persist during model archiving and
recycling. Finally, Sect. 7.8 provides a summary of this chapter.
7.3 Threat Landscape Across the AI System
Production Process
7.3.1 AI Production Process Overview
An analysis of the security vulnerabilities in the AI system production
process landscape reveals a complex and multifaceted threat surface. The
provided diagram Fig. 7.1, which illustrates both software and hardware
implementation pathways for AI systems, serves as a critical roadmap for
identifying potential points of compromise. From data acquisition to final
deployment and consumption, each stage presents unique security
challenges that can be exploited by malicious actors, potentially leading to
model theft, data poisoning, system failure, or the execution of unseen,
harmful functionalities. This chapter will discuss these vulnerabilities

--- Strana 244 ---
within the two primary implementation pipelines: software implementation
and hardware accelerator implementation.

--- Strana 245 ---

--- Strana 246 ---
Fig. 7.1 AI system production process landscape
The “software implementation” pipeline, while seemingly more
straightforward, is fraught with peril. The process begins with “data
acquisition and annotation,” a stage critically vulnerable to data poisoning
attacks [4]. An adversary could intentionally introduce mislabeled or
malicious data into the training dataset. If undetected, this compromised
dataset can lead to the training of a model with embedded biases or
backdoors. For instance, a model trained on poisoned data might
misclassify specific inputs, leading to denial-of-service or targeted attacks
once deployed. The reliance on a “pretrained model vendor” introduces
another significant risk. These models, often sourced from third-party
repositories, can harbor hidden backdoors or Trojan attacks [5]. An attacker
could publish a seemingly high-performing pretrained model that contains a
trigger; when the deployed model encounters a specific, unusual input, it
executes a malicious payload. The subsequent stages, including “model
training” and “model evaluation,” are also susceptible. The integrity of the
training process itself can be compromised through attacks on the
underlying software frameworks and libraries.
Following the model’s creation, the “model deployment” phase presents
another set of critical vulnerabilities. When deploying to a “cloud service
vendor,” the security of the cloud infrastructure itself becomes paramount.
Misconfigurations, insecure APIs, or insider threats within the cloud
provider can expose the AI model and its associated data [6]. The
“inference accelerator vendor” for cloud services also adds a layer of
abstraction and potential risk; vulnerabilities in their hardware or firmware
could be exploited [7]. Similarly, deploying to an “edge device vendor”
introduces risks associated with the physical security of the device and the
integrity of its operating system and software stack. An attacker with
physical access to an edge device could potentially extract the model,
reverse-engineer it, or tamper with its functionality [8]. The “consumption”
and “recycling/model archiving” phases are not exempt from security
concerns. Insecure communication channels during consumption can lead to
data interception, while improper model archiving or disposal can result in
the leakage of sensitive intellectual property [9].
The “hardware accelerator implementation” pipeline, while offering
performance advantages, introduces a more complex and opaque supply
chain, significantly expanding the attack surface. This pathway inherits all

--- Strana 247 ---
the vulnerabilities of the software implementation’s initial stages (“data
acquisition” to “AI model”). However, the subsequent steps, beginning with
“IP core licensing and delivery,” introduce profound hardware-level threats.
Malicious actors could insert hardware Trojans into the licensed Intellectual
Property (IP) cores. These Trojans can be incredibly difficult to detect
through standard testing, remaining dormant until activated by a specific
condition, at which point they could leak sensitive data, degrade
performance, or cause a complete system failure [10].
The integration of these potentially compromised IP cores during the
“SoC integration” phase, facilitated by “EDA tool providers,” presents
another vector of attack. The electronic design automation tools themselves
can be compromised, leading to the insertion of malicious logic during the
chip design process without the knowledge of the designers [11]. The
“fabrication” stage, often outsourced to external foundries, is a well-
documented source of security risks, including chip counterfeiting,
overproduction, and the physical insertion of malicious circuitry [12]. Even
after a chip is fabricated, the “PCB assembly” and “functional testing”
stages can be targeted. A compromised component could be introduced
during assembly, or testing procedures might be manipulated to overlook
certain hardware-level vulnerabilities [13]. By the time the hardware
accelerator is deployed and reaches the “consumption” stage, it may contain
deeply embedded threats that are virtually impossible to detect through
software-based security measures alone, making the entire AI system
susceptible to manipulation or failure [14]. The security of the AI system is,
therefore, only as strong as the weakest link in this intricate production and
deployment chain.
7.4 Security Analysis of AI Model Development
Stage
During the AI model development stage, significant security threats arise,
primarily categorized into data acquisition and annotation, model design
and training, and model evaluation.
7.4.1 Data Acquisition and Annotation

--- Strana 248 ---
One of the main threats during data acquisition and annotation is data
poisoning, where adversaries manipulate training data to influence the
model’s predictions adversely. For instance, Shafahi et al. demonstrated
clean-label poisoning attacks, which subtly alter data without changing
labels, making detection difficult [15]. Chen et al. showed that attackers
could inject manipulated images into datasets, drastically degrading the
performance of facial recognition systems [16]. Additionally, Schwarzschild
et al. presented recent methods of data poisoning in federated learning
environments, showing increased sophistication in distributed settings [17].
Mitigation strategies for data poisoning include using robust training
methods such as outlier detection, filtering suspicious data points, and
anomaly detection techniques. For example, Steinhardt et al. proposed
methods to systematically detect poisoned data by identifying statistical
anomalies [18].
Another significant threat is biased annotation, either intentional or
inadvertent, causing model bias. For example, Mor Geva et al. analyzed
how annotation bias could severely affect the fairness and accuracy of
object detection models [19].
To mitigate biased annotations, auditing and correcting annotated
datasets using fairness-aware annotation guidelines and diverse annotator
teams is recommended. Buolamwini and Gebru demonstrated the necessity
of diverse datasets in reducing bias in facial recognition systems [20].
Mehrabi et al. recommended standardized bias assessment frameworks to
systematically reduce bias in data annotation [21].
7.4.2 Model Design and Training
During model design and training, adversarial attacks and backdoor
insertion pose significant threats. Goodfellow et al. identified adversarial
examples as crafted inputs that mislead machine learning models despite
minor perturbations invisible to human observers [22]. Additionally, Gu et
al. demonstrated that attackers could insert backdoors into neural networks
during training, triggering malicious behaviors when specific conditions are
met [23].
Adversarial training, which incorporates adversarial examples during
the training phase, is a popular mitigation technique. Madry et al.
demonstrated that adversarial training significantly improves model
robustness against adversarial examples [24]. To mitigate backdoor

--- Strana 249 ---
insertion, Wang et al. proposed neural cleanse, a technique to detect and
mitigate backdoors by reverse engineering potential triggers [25].
Furthermore, model theft or extraction attacks, where adversaries
attempt to replicate proprietary AI models, have grown prominent. Tramer
et al. highlighted techniques for model extraction via query attacks,
showing the vulnerability of models to unauthorized duplication [26].
Krishna et al. explored model extraction attacks in cloud-based machine
learning deployments [27].
Mitigation strategies for model theft include employing model
watermarking and output perturbation. Uchida et al. introduced
watermarking techniques to securely embed identification markers within
models, allowing detection and legal protection against theft [28].
7.4.3 Model Evaluation
In the evaluation phase, threats such as evaluation metric manipulation and
evasion attacks become critical. Carlini et al. illustrated how adversarial
attacks could specifically target evaluation metrics, misleading assessments
of model robustness [29].
Additionally, evasion attacks, wherein attackers manipulate inputs
during evaluation to bypass security checks, are a significant concern.
Papernot et al. provided evidence of evasion attacks capable of degrading
the effectiveness of malware detection systems by subtly altering malware
features to evade detection [30].
These threats underscore the critical need for robust security
mechanisms at each stage of AI model development to mitigate potential
risks effectively.
7.5 Security Analysis of Software Implementation
Stage
The deployment stage is when a trained AI model is made available for use
in a production environment. At the deployment stage, AI systems
encounter numerous security threats that can compromise their
functionality, integrity, and privacy. The security of this stage is paramount,
as a compromised model can lead to erroneous outputs, service disruption,
and data breaches, with potentially severe consequences. This stage

--- Strana 250 ---
presents a unique set of security vulnerabilities, as the model is exposed to
various inputs and potential attackers. The specific threats and viable
solutions often depend on the deployment environment, which can broadly
be categorized into cloud services, inference accelerators, and edge devices.
7.5.1 Deploying Models on Cloud Service
Deploying AI models on cloud platforms offers scalability and cost-
effectiveness but also introduces a broad attack surface. The multi-tenant
nature of the cloud and the reliance on third-party infrastructure create
unique security challenges.
7.5.1.1 Threats
One of the most significant threats in a cloud environment is the model
extraction attack. In this scenario, an attacker with query access to the
deployed model can effectively steal the model’s intellectual property by
creating a functionally equivalent replica. For instance, a malicious user
could repeatedly query a deployed machine learning-as-a-service (MLaaS)
API to observe the input-output behavior and then use this information to
train a substitute model. This was demonstrated by Tramèr et al., who
successfully extracted models from commercial cloud ML platforms [26].
The economic and intellectual property losses from such an attack can be
substantial.
Another prevalent threat is the evasion attack, where an adversary
intentionally crafts inputs to deceive the deployed model into making
incorrect predictions. These “adversarial examples” are often imperceptible
to humans but can cause the model to misclassify data with high
confidence. For example, a carefully perturbed image of a stop sign might
be classified as a speed limit sign, with catastrophic implications for
autonomous driving systems deployed in the cloud. Papernot et al. explored
the practical aspects of crafting such attacks against models hosted on cloud
platforms, highlighting their real-world viability [31].
7.5.1.2 Mitigation Strategies
To counter model extraction attacks, several defensive strategies have been
proposed. One approach is watermarking, where a unique identifier is
embedded into the model during training. This watermark can later be used
to prove ownership if a suspect model is discovered. Adi et al. proposed a

--- Strana 251 ---
method for watermarking deep neural networks that is resilient to various
model modification attacks, providing a means of recourse in cases of
model theft [32]. Another mitigation is to limit the information provided in
the model’s output. For instance, instead of providing detailed confidence
scores for each class, the API could only return the top predicted label. This
reduces the amount of information an attacker can glean from each query,
making model extraction more difficult [26].
For mitigating evasion attacks, adversarial training has emerged as a
robust defense. This technique involves augmenting the training dataset
with adversarial examples, thereby teaching the model to be resilient to
such perturbations. Madry et al. demonstrated that models trained against a
strong adversary are more robust to a wide range of adversarial attacks [24].
Another effective technique is input sanitization, where potentially
malicious inputs are preprocessed to remove adversarial perturbations
before they are fed to the model. For example, an image could be slightly
blurred or compressed to smooth out adversarial noise. Guo et al. showed
that simple image transformations can effectively defend against a variety
of adversarial attacks [33].
7.5.2 Deploying Models on Inference Accelerator
To meet the high computational demands of modern AI models, specialized
hardware accelerators like GPUs and TPUs are often used for inference.
While these accelerators boost performance, they also introduce a new layer
of hardware-specific vulnerabilities, such as side-channel attacks and
hardware Trojan insertion attacks.
7.5.2.1 Threats
Side-channel attacks pose a significant threat to inference accelerators. By
observing physical characteristics such as power consumption,
electromagnetic emissions, or memory access patterns, an attacker can infer
sensitive information about the model, including its architecture and
parameters. Adversaries can analyze an accelerator’s side-channel
emissions to reconstruct a proprietary neural network. For instance, Zhan et
al. demonstrated how attackers could exploit electromagnetic side-channel
information from NVIDIA and AMD GPUs to infer sensitive user activities,
such as identifying visited webpages or inferring keystroke timings [34].

--- Strana 252 ---
This allows for direct intellectual property theft without any software
access.
Another concern involves hardware Trojan insertion, where malicious
actors embed covert circuitry into accelerators to degrade performance or
leak confidential data. While not exclusively focused on GPUs, the
principles of hardware Trojan attacks on neural networks, as demonstrated
by Clements et al., are directly applicable to GPU-accelerated deep learning
systems [35]. Yang et al. demonstrated a realistic scenario in which such
Trojans were used to induce undetected computational errors in hardware
accelerators [36]. A related but distinct threat is targeted misclassification,
or backdoors, where Trojans are designed to remain inactive until triggered
by a specific condition—such as a small, invisible patch on an input image.
Once triggered, the Trojan forces the accelerator to produce an incorrect
output predetermined by the attacker. Clements et al. showed how a
hardware Trojan could be inserted to create such a backdoor, causing
specific misclassifications while the system otherwise appears to function
normally [35].
7.5.2.2 Mitigation Strategies
Defending against side-channel attacks on inference accelerators often
involves a combination of hardware and software techniques. At the
hardware level, hardware isolation techniques can securely partition GPU
resources and establish strong security boundaries between different users
or applications. Secure memory partitioning and hardware random number
generators also help reduce the attack surface [37]. Lungu et al. proposed a
classification of side-channel attacks targeting the NVIDIA GeForce RTX
3090 GPU and outlined mitigation measures, including these hardware and
software techniques, to effectively reduce power, timing, and
electromagnetic side-channel leakage [37]. Constant-time execution, a
software-based approach, ensures that the time taken for a computation is
independent of the input data, thereby thwarting timing-based side-channel
attacks. Maji et al. propose a neural network accelerator design based on
threshold implementation, which incorporates power and electromagnetic
side-channel countermeasures to enhance hardware security [38].
Mitigating hardware Trojan attacks in AI accelerators requires a
multilayered defense that combines reactive detection with proactive,
secure design principles. While initial security layers include pre-

--- Strana 253 ---
deployment screening via side-channel analysis to find physical anomalies
and runtime monitoring to catch real-time faults, the most robust strategies
focus on making the hardware inherently resilient to tampering. This
proactive approach is exemplified by recent work in tamper-resistant
design, which makes the accelerator’s internal structure unpredictable to an
adversary in the supply chain. For instance, by systematically applying
techniques like hardware component shuffling and substitution during the
design phase of a CNN accelerator, as demonstrated by Yu et al., the fixed,
predictable layout that an attacker relies upon is fundamentally disrupted.
This makes it extremely difficult to insert a functional and stealthy
hardware Trojan that can reliably trigger a backdoor, thus providing a
foundational layer of security against malicious modifications [12].
Additionally, GPUs enhance protection for models and data through
hardware-assisted security features like memory encryption and secure boot
[39].
7.5.3 Deploying Models on Edge Device
Deploying AI models on edge devices, such as smartphones, IoT sensors,
and embedded systems, offers advantages in terms of low latency and data
privacy. However, the resource-constrained nature of these devices and their
physical accessibility make them susceptible to a different set of security
threats.
7.5.3.1 Threats
When deployed in the real world, models on edge devices face a diverse set
of potent threats that exploit their accessibility. A primary concern is the
integrity of the model’s predictions, which can be undermined by
adversarial attacks. These attacks use carefully crafted, subtle perturbations
to an input to cause the model to make an incorrect prediction. For instance,
Su et al. demonstrated a “one-pixel attack” where changing a single pixel in
an image tricked advanced neural networks into misclassifying it,
highlighting a simple yet effective method for bypassing AI-based visual
recognition systems [40]. Beyond manipulating inputs, adversaries also
seek to compromise the confidentiality of the training data through
techniques like model inversion attacks. These attacks aim to reconstruct
sensitive data from the original training set by repeatedly querying the
model; Fredrikson et al. successfully reconstructed recognizable facial

--- Strana 254 ---
images from a facial recognition model using only API access, thereby
leaking private biometric data [41]. Similarly, membership inference attacks
pose another significant privacy risk by allowing an adversary to determine
if a specific data record was part of the model’s training set. Shokri et al.
showed that an attacker could train “shadow models” based on a target
model’s outputs to infer membership with high accuracy [42]. In addition to
privacy breaches, the intellectual property of the model itself is at risk from
model stealing attacks, where an attacker systematically queries a
proprietary model to train a functional replica. Tramèr et al. demonstrated
that even black-box models from major cloud providers could be stolen
with a limited number of queries, allowing for the illicit replication of
valuable intellectual property [26].
7.5.3.2 Mitigation Strategies
To counter the threats faced by edge-deployed models, researchers have
developed several defense mechanisms. To protect model integrity against
adversarial attacks, the most prominent defense is adversarial training. This
technique makes a model more robust by augmenting its training dataset
with adversarial examples, thereby teaching it to ignore malicious
perturbations, a method shown by Madry et al. to be highly effective [24].
To address the privacy breaches from inversion and inference attacks,
statistical methods are employed, with differential privacy being a leading
approach. By adding carefully calibrated noise during training, it becomes
computationally infeasible for an attacker to reconstruct training data or
confirm an individual’s participation, as demonstrated by Abadi et al. who
developed a method to train deep learning models with these formal privacy
guarantees [43]. Finally, to protect the model as intellectual property against
theft, model watermarking has been proposed. This technique embeds a
unique, hidden signature into a model that can be used to prove ownership.
Adi et al. introduced a method to “backdoor” a model with a specific trigger
—a set of inputs that produces a unique, predefined output—which serves
as an undeniable watermark if the model is stolen [32].
In conclusion, the deployment phase of the AI life cycle is a critical
juncture where the security of the model is put to the test. A thorough
understanding of the unique threats posed by different deployment
environments, coupled with the implementation of robust and multilayered

--- Strana 255 ---
defense mechanisms, is essential for ensuring the safe and reliable operation
of AI systems in the real world.
7.6 Security Analysis of Hardware Accelerator
Implementation Stage
7.6.1 IP Core Licensing and Delivery
IP core vendors play a critical role in the semiconductor supply chain by
providing reusable design blocks, such as processors, memory controllers,
and interfaces, which are licensed by chip manufacturers to accelerate
development and reduce costs [1]. Prominent examples include Arm cores,
Synopsys IP, and Cadence libraries. Despite their importance, IP vendors
face several risks and threats that can undermine the integrity and security
of the supply chain [44].
Key risks include counterfeit or unauthorized IP, which may introduce
reliability or security vulnerabilities, and Trojaned IP, where malicious
modifications can result in data leakage, backdoor access, or operational
malfunctions [10]. Additionally, IP theft or cloning through reverse
engineering poses a significant threat, as does the obsolescence of vendor-
supported IP, leaving designs susceptible to unaddressed vulnerabilities
[45]. Mitigation strategies to address these challenges involve sourcing
certified IP from trusted vendors, conducting static and dynamic analyses of
IP prior to integration, leveraging hardware assurance tools for integrity
verification, and employing hardware obfuscation techniques to protect
against cloning and reverse engineering [1]. These practices collectively
enhance the security and reliability of IP within the supply chain.
7.6.2 SoC Integration
SoC integration entails combining multiple IP blocks, often sourced from
various vendors, into a single chip. Given its complexity, this process is
frequently outsourced to design houses or third-party electronic design
automation (EDA) tools, which play a critical role in chip design and
verification. However, the integration process is not without risks and
threats that can compromise the security and functionality of the final
product [46].

--- Strana 256 ---
Notable risks include integration errors, such as insecure interconnects
or race conditions, which may introduce unintentional vulnerabilities [47].
The use of third-party EDA tools further raises concerns, as compromised
toolchains could embed backdoors or Trojans during design [11].
Incorporating IP from untrusted vendors amplifies the likelihood of
vulnerabilities, while improper design practices can expose side-channel
vulnerabilities, allowing sensitive data leakage through power analysis or
electromagnetic emissions [48]. Mitigation strategies to address these issues
include applying zero-trust principles by validating and isolating IP
components, employing formal verification and runtime monitoring to
detect anomalies, and securing the EDA toolchain by using licensed and
updated software while verifying tool outputs [49]. Additionally, designing
SoCs with side-channel resistance, such as through noise injection and
balanced data paths, helps enhance their resilience to data leakage [50].
7.6.3 Fabrication
In the production life cycle of an AI hardware accelerator, the fabrication
stage represents one of the most critical and vulnerable points in the entire
hardware supply chain. During this phase, the entity that designed the chip
relinquishes direct physical control over its intellectual property, sending
the final design file—typically in GDSII format—to a semiconductor
foundry for mass production. This handoff to a potentially untrusted third
party exposes the sensitive design to a range of sophisticated hardware-
level attacks. A primary threat is the insertion of a hardware Trojan, which
is a malicious and stealthy modification to the circuit design. An adversary
within the foundry can introduce logic that remains dormant during
standard testing but activates under specific trigger conditions, at which
point it can leak secret data, degrade performance, or cause a catastrophic
system failure [10]. Furthermore, IC counterfeiting and overproduction
pose a severe economic and security threat. A malicious foundry can
produce more chips than authorized (overproduction) or sell defective, out-
of-spec chips as genuine articles on the gray market. This not only leads to
significant revenue loss for the original design house but also risks
introducing unreliable or insecure hardware into critical AI systems [51].
Finally, the design itself is vulnerable to theft through reverse engineering.
The GDSII file provides a complete blueprint of the chip, but even without
it, an adversary can acquire a finished chip and use advanced imaging and

--- Strana 257 ---
de-layering techniques to reconstruct the full transistor-level layout, thereby
stealing valuable and hard-won intellectual property [52].
To counter these profound threats at the fabrication level, several
innovative “design-for-trust” countermeasures have been developed. One
effective physical security technique is split manufacturing, which divides
the fabrication process across two separate foundries. The front-end-of-line
(FEOL) processes, which create the core transistors, are performed at a
state-of-the-art but potentially untrusted facility. The wafers are then
shipped to a trusted, albeit lower-tech, facility to add the final back-end-of-
line (BEOL) metal layers that connect the transistors into a functional
circuit. Without the secret BEOL design, the FEOL wafers are useless, thus
obscuring the chip’s functionality and preventing both IP theft and Trojan
insertion [53]. Another powerful cryptographic approach is logic locking,
which transforms the original circuit by adding “key” inputs so that the chip
will only function correctly if a secret digital key is provided. This renders
any chips overproduced by the foundry useless, as the adversary does not
possess the secret key required for activation [54]. To provide a robust and
unique identity for each chip, physically unclonable functions (PUFs) are
integrated into the design. A PUF leverages the random, uncontrollable
variations inherent in the chip manufacturing process to generate a unique
and unclonable device “fingerprint.” This fingerprint can be used as a root
of trust for cryptographic operations or as a unique identifier to authenticate
the device, making it impossible for even the original manufacturer to
create a clone and thereby preventing counterfeit chips from being used in
secure systems [55].
7.6.4 PCB Assembly
Printed circuit board (PCB) assembly is the stage where individual
electronic components, including the newly fabricated AI accelerator SoC,
are mounted onto a PCB to create a functional electronic system. This
process involves sourcing numerous components from a global supply
chain, soldering them to the board, and programming the necessary
firmware. The distributed and complex nature of this stage exposes the final
product to significant security risks, particularly from counterfeit
components and supply chain compromises [56].
A primary threat at this stage is the infiltration of counterfeit electronic
components. These can range from recycled and remarked components to

--- Strana 258 ---
out-of-spec or cloned parts that do not meet quality and reliability standards
[57]. Such components can introduce unpredictable behavior, degrade
performance, or create exploitable vulnerabilities in the AI hardware. A
more malicious threat is the introduction of modified components or
circuitry at the board level. An attacker with access to the assembly process
could substitute a legitimate component with a malicious one containing a
hardware Trojan or discreetly add, remove, or alter components on the PCB
to create a backdoor or a kill switch [58]. For instance, a compromised
microcontroller on the board could be used to manipulate the AI
accelerator’s operations or exfiltrate data. The supply chain for PCB
assembly is often opaque, making it difficult to verify the integrity of every
component and process, a vulnerability that attackers can exploit to inject
malicious hardware without the manufacturer’s knowledge [13].
To counter these threats, a multilayered defense strategy is essential.
Rigorous component authentication and screening is the first line of
defense. Techniques such as X-ray inspection, decapsulation, and statistical
analysis of physical and electrical characteristics can help identify
counterfeit and tampered components before they are mounted on the board
[59]. For securing the supply chain itself, blockchain technology has been
proposed as a means to create a transparent and immutable ledger for
tracking components from their origin to the assembly line, ensuring
provenance and preventing unauthorized substitutions [60]. To protect
against unauthorized modifications during and after assembly, physical
inspection and imaging of the assembled PCB can be compared against a
golden reference design to detect discrepancies [61]. Furthermore,
techniques like conformal coating can be applied to PCBs, which makes
physical tampering difficult without leaving obvious evidence [62]. Finally,
employing secure programming protocols at trusted facilities ensures that
the firmware and software loaded onto the hardware have not been
tampered with and originate from a legitimate source [63].
7.6.5 Functional Testing
Functional testing is a critical quality assurance step where the fully
assembled hardware accelerator is tested to verify that it operates according
to its specifications. This process, often conducted by a third-party test
facility, involves running a suite of test vectors and checking for correct

--- Strana 259 ---
outputs. While designed to detect manufacturing defects, the testing stage
can itself be a vector for attack or fail to detect sophisticated threats.
A fundamental threat is hardware Trojan evasion. Malicious circuits are
often designed to be stealthy and are only activated by rare trigger
conditions (e.g., a specific input sequence or an internal state) that are
unlikely to be covered by standard functional test patterns [10].
Consequently, a trojaned accelerator can pass all functional tests yet contain
a potent backdoor. Another threat involves the test infrastructure itself. A
malicious test facility could leverage its access to inject faults, clone
devices, or, more subtly, use high-precision probes to conduct side-channel
attacks. By analyzing power consumption or electromagnetic emissions
during testing, an adversary can extract sensitive on-chip assets like
cryptographic keys or even elements of the proprietary AI model being
executed [64]. The test process can also be exploited to insert faults that
cause accelerated aging, degrading the accelerator’s performance and
reliability over time in a difficult-to-attribute manner [65].
Mitigation strategies for this stage extend beyond simple pass/fail
functional checks. To detect stealthy Trojans, side-channel analysis, often
enhanced with machine learning algorithms to improve accuracy and reduce
false positives, is used as a powerful detection method. By generating a
power or EM signature from a trusted “golden” device, deviations in the
signatures of devices under test can reveal the presence of extra, malicious
circuitry not caught by logic tests [66]. Another approach is the use of
Trojan-aware test pattern generation, which uses static analysis of the netlist
to identify suspicious rare-net switching that could correspond to a Trojan
trigger and then generates vectors specifically to activate it [67]. To counter
test-based information leakage and tampering, runtime monitoring and
built-in self-test (BIST) circuits are crucial. These on-chip monitors can
continuously check for anomalous behavior (e.g., unusual power draws,
invalid computations) during operation and can be designed to attest to the
integrity of the device, providing a trusted execution environment that is
secure even if the external test infrastructure is compromised [68].
7.7 Security Analysis of Post-Deployment Stage
The security posture of an artificial intelligence (AI) system is not solidified
upon its creation; rather, it remains a critical concern throughout its entire

--- Strana 260 ---
life cycle. While significant attention is often directed toward vulnerabilities
in the data acquisition and model training phases, the post-deployment
stages, namely, “consumption” and “recycling/model archiving,” present a
distinct and equally perilous attack surface. During consumption, the model
transitions from a static, internal asset to a dynamic, interactive service,
exposing it to external probing. In the archiving and recycling phase, the
model becomes a latent risk, a stored representation of intellectual property
and potential vulnerabilities that can be stolen directly or propagated into
future systems. Compromises during these final stages can lead to
intellectual property theft, severe privacy breaches, systemic erosion of
trust, and the propagation of deeply embedded flaws. This section provides
a detailed analysis of the security threats endemic to these stages and
discusses corresponding mitigation strategies, supported by recent
literature.
7.7.1 Consumption Stage
The consumption stage, where a deployed model serves live predictions via
an application programming interface (API) or on an edge device, is the
primary interface between the AI system and its users, including potential
adversaries. This interactive nature is its greatest strength and its most
significant weakness. Unlike attacks on the training pipeline, which are
often difficult to mount without internal access, attacks on the consumption
endpoint can be performed by any user with query access. A primary threat
in this stage is the model extraction attack, where adversaries effectively
steal a proprietary model by repeatedly querying it and using the resulting
input-output pairs to train a functionally equivalent substitute. This
constitutes a direct theft of intellectual property, allowing attackers to
bypass expensive API fees or reverse-engineer a competitor’s technology,
with research demonstrating the feasibility of creating clones with over
99% fidelity to the original [26]. Furthermore, a profound privacy risk
emerges in the form of membership inference attacks. These attacks aim to
determine if a specific data record was part of the model’s training set by
exploiting the subtle differences in how a model responds to “seen” versus
“unseen” data. A successful attack in a healthcare context, for instance,
could leak a patient’s sensitive medical status [69]. The most widely
discussed threat at inference is the adversarial attack, where an adversary
introduces carefully crafted, often human-imperceptible perturbations to an

--- Strana 261 ---
input to cause a confident misclassification. The physical manifestations of
these attacks are particularly alarming, such as placing small stickers on a
“stop” sign to fool an autonomous vehicle’s perception system, highlighting
a critical risk to safety-relevant applications [70].
In response to this multifaceted threat landscape, a range of defensive
strategies has been developed to harden models at the point of inference.
Differential privacy stands as the formal, mathematical countermeasure to
membership inference, providing a guarantee of privacy by injecting
calibrated noise either during model training or into the model’s outputs at
inference time, making it computationally infeasible for an attacker to glean
information about any single training record [71]. To deter model theft,
model watermarking provides a method for proving ownership. This is
often achieved by embedding a secret backdoor into the model, causing it to
produce a specific, unique output in response to a secret set of inputs, a
behavior that would be statistically impossible to replicate by chance and
can be used as forensic evidence of theft [32]. To combat adversarial
evasion, the most proven defense is adversarial training, a process that
enhances model robustness by augmenting the training data with adversarial
examples and explicitly teaching the model to classify them correctly [24].
This is often supplemented by complementary defenses such as input
sanitization, which aims to filter out perturbations before the input reaches
the model [72], and certified defenses, which provide mathematical
guarantees of robustness against certain classes of attack [73]. Together,
these strategies form a layered defense to protect the active, deployed
model during its consumption phase.
7.7.2 Recycling/Model Archiving Stage
The final phase of the AI life cycle, involving the archiving of models for
compliance and the recycling of models for new tasks, is characterized by
latent risks. Here, the model is an inactive, stored digital asset, yet it
represents a concentration of intellectual property and potential
vulnerabilities. The threats in this stage are not interactive but are instead
focused on asset theft and the propagation of hidden flaws through an
insecure model supply chain. The most direct threat is unauthorized access
and leakage, where the model files themselves are exfiltrated from storage
due to misconfigured cloud services, compromised credentials, or insider
threats. The stolen asset is not merely a file but a complete blueprint of a

--- Strana 262 ---
research and development effort, including the model’s architecture,
weights, and embedded knowledge, the loss of which can be devastating to
a company’s competitive advantage [9]. A more subtle but equally
pernicious threat is vulnerability injection via improper recycling. When an
archived model is used as a foundation for a new project via transfer
learning, it inherits the security posture of its predecessor. If the original
model contained a hidden backdoor or hardware Trojan, instilled through
data poisoning during its initial training, this vulnerability will be carried
silently into the new system, ready to be activated by an adversary [74].
Beyond malicious backdoors, other flaws like inherited bias from the
original training data can be propagated, creating significant ethical and
legal risks in the new application domain [75].
Mitigating these latent threats requires a dual focus on robust asset
security and the establishment of a trustworthy model supply chain. The
foundational layer of defense is the application of strong, end-to-end
cryptography for model files at rest, coupled with a zero-trust access control
policy. This means that access to encrypted models and their corresponding
decryption keys is never trusted by default and is governed by the principle
of least privilege, with all actions subject to strict authentication and
auditing [74]. Beyond securing the asset, ensuring its integrity before reuse
is paramount. This requires a proactive process of model integrity
verification and sanitization. Before an archived model is recycled, it
should be subjected to model scanning using tools designed to detect hidden
backdoors by reverse-engineering their potential triggers [76]. If a
vulnerability is found, sanitization techniques such as fine-pruning or
unlearning can be employed to attempt to remove the malicious behavior
from the model [77]. To operationalize this security-conscious approach to
the model life cycle, organizations should adopt rigorous governance
practices, chief among them the use of Model Cards and an AI Bill of
Materials (AI BOM) [78]. These documents provide essential, transparent
information about a model’s provenance, training data, architecture, and
known limitations, creating a chain of trust and enabling developers to
make informed risk assessments before reusing an archived asset [79].
7.8 Conclusions

--- Strana 263 ---
The journey of an AI system from conception to consumption is paved with
a diverse and evolving landscape of security threats. This chapter has
illuminated the critical vulnerabilities present at every stage of the
production process, spanning both software and hardware implementation
pathways. From the initial phase of data acquisition, where poisoning and
bias can corrupt a model at its source, to the final stages of consumption
and archiving, where intellectual property theft and latent vulnerabilities
pose significant risks, no part of the life cycle is immune to attack.
The analysis underscores a fundamental principle: AI security is not a
feature to be added post hoc but a continuous and holistic process. The
software pipeline is vulnerable to threats like model extraction, evasion
attacks, and privacy breaches through membership inference, which can be
mitigated by defenses such as adversarial training, differential privacy, and
model watermarking. Simultaneously, the hardware implementation
pipeline introduces a more opaque and challenging set of threats, including
hardware Trojans embedded in IP cores, compromised EDA tools, and
supply chain attacks during fabrication and assembly. Countering these
requires a different class of solutions, from trusted hardware verification
and split manufacturing to physical inspection and tamper-resistant designs.
Ultimately, the security of an AI system is only as strong as its weakest
link. A vulnerability introduced by an untrusted IP vendor, a subtle data
poisoning attack, or an insecure cloud deployment can undermine the entire
system, regardless of the strength of other defenses. Therefore, creating
truly robust AI requires a multilayered, defense-in-depth strategy that
combines technical solutions with rigorous governance, such as the
adoption of model cards and secure supply chain management. As
adversaries continue to develop more sophisticated attack methods, ongoing
vigilance, research, and a security-first mindset are paramount to ensuring
these powerful technologies are deployed safely, securely, and ethically.

8. A Case Study on Shuffle and
Substitution-Based Defense Mechanism
Haosen Yu1   , Peiyao Sun1    and Basel Halak1  
School of Electronics and Computer Science, University of
Southampton, Southampton, UK
 
Haosen Yu
Email: Haosen.Yu@soton.ac.uk

Keywords Hardware Trojans – CNN accelerators – Shuffle mechanisms –

8.1 Introduction
In hardware security research, numerous HT attacks and their
corresponding countermeasures have been delineated. However, a
significant portion of these countermeasures are tailored to specific
segments of the supply chain or particular attack modalities. This
specialization has engendered an important challenge: the absence of a

--- Strana 271 ---
holistic countermeasure capable of concurrently defending against diverse
Trojan attacks, thereby maximizing hardware protection [1].
In response to this challenge, an innovative countermeasure has been
proposed in our previous work [2]. This approach preprocesses input image
data using shuffling and substitution techniques and reorders the sequence
of output neurons at the hardware level. Termed the “shuffle and
substitution-based defense mechanism (SSDM),” this dual-layered
countermeasure is meticulously architected to counter threats emanating
from both the third-party dataset provisioning phase and the fabrication
phase. Consequently, it offers robust resistance against a wide range of
CNN HT insertion attacks by adversaries and backdoor attacks by third-
party dataset providers.
This chapter presents the design, principles, and implementation of the
shuffle and substitution-based defense mechanism (SSDM), attack
experiments are also set up to verify the defense effectiveness of SSDM,
and the performance is discussed.

--- Strana 272 ---
8.2 Chapter Overview
This chapter introduces the shuffle and substitution-based defense
mechanism (SSDM), a novel countermeasure designed to enhance the
security of convolutional neural network (CNN) accelerators against
sophisticated hardware Trojan (HT) attacks and backdoor vulnerabilities.
Section 8.3 begins by thoroughly examining the threat landscape posed by
HT attacks in CNN accelerators, detailing various attack vectors and
classification methods based on their triggering mechanisms. Building upon
this understanding, Sect. 8.4 delineates the comprehensive design of the
SSDM, elucidating its core components: pixel-level shuffling, neuron-level
shuffling, and the substitution mechanism. The subsequent Sect. 8.5
elaborates on the practical hardware implementation aspects of the SSDM.
Finally, Sects. 8.6 and 8.7 present the experimental configuration and an
exhaustive evaluation of the SSDM’s performance against diverse HT
insertion attacks and patch-based backdoor attacks, demonstrating its
efficacy in improving the robustness and security of CNN accelerators,
followed by Sect. 8.8 concluding remarks that summarize the key findings
and emphasize the importance of integrated security measures in CNN
accelerators.
8.3 Threat Modelling for HT Attacks in CNN
Accelerators
8.3.1 Hardware Trojan Security Threats in CNN Accelerators
CNN accelerators are specialized hardware designed to enhance the
computational efficiency of convolutional neural networks (CNNs). Given
the critical applications of CNNs, such as autonomous driving, medical
imaging, and defense, ensuring the security of these accelerators is
paramount.
In recent years, various hardware Trojans (HTs) have been deliberately
engineered to exploit vulnerabilities in CNN accelerators [3–10]. These
attacks primarily target critical components, including processing units
(PEs) [3, 4], memory subsystems [5, 6], prediction outputs [7], bias buffers
[8], activation parameters [9], and reconfigurable interconnection networks
[10].

--- Strana 273 ---
The first study to explore hardware Trojan threats in CNN-based image
classification involved embedding a Trojan into the Multiplier and Adder
Tree (MAT) module of an FPGA accelerator [3]. Experimental results
revealed precise manipulation of CNN classification outcomes with
minimal system overhead and modifications. Another approach introduced
a Trojan into the rectified linear unit (ReLU) function block, successfully
achieving adversarial objectives [4]. This study highlighted both the stealth
and effectiveness of such hardware Trojans. Building on these methods, a
memory Trojan was designed to attack a neural network accelerator,
departing from techniques requiring detailed knowledge of the model and
toolchain. This Trojan executed an accuracy degradation attack by
accessing data on the memory bus and was triggered by specific input
images [5].
Further, a sequence-triggered hardware Trojan was developed to
manipulate the prediction outcomes of neural network systems. Unlike
pixel-based triggers, this Trojan relied on a sequence of input images,
making it resistant to image preprocessing techniques and undetectable by
human observers. Experiments using MNIST, CIFAR100, and ISLVRC
datasets demonstrated the Trojan’s ability to activate selectively with
minimal hardware overhead [7].
Additionally, Int-Monitor, a hardware Trojan targeting DNN
accelerators, was specifically designed to exploit the global bias buffer. By
embedding an interrupt monitor between the host processor and the DNN
accelerator, this Trojan disrupted neuron activation, invalidated forward
propagation, and rendered the accelerator nonfunctional [8].
A hardware Trojan attacks targeting deep neural network (DNN)
hardware accelerators implemented on FPGAs have been introduced [9].
These Trojans are designed to manipulate activation parameters within
DNN accelerators. Experimental results demonstrate that these Trojans
achieve high levels of stealth, making detection difficult, while significantly
degrading DNN inference accuracy when activated.
To mitigate such threats, various countermeasures have been proposed,
including hardware attestation, Trojan detection techniques, and secure chip
manufacturing processes [1].
Hardware Trojans in CNNs can be broadly classified based on their
activation mechanisms into always-on and trigger-required types. Always-
on Trojans remain perpetually active, making them particularly stealthy due

--- Strana 274 ---
to the absence of additional circuits. This lack of extra hardware makes
them challenging to detect using bitstream reverse engineering or pre-
silicon analysis methods [11]. However, their continuous activation
necessitates careful design of malicious functionality; overt changes to
outputs can make them detectable during testing.
In contrast, trigger-required Trojans activate only upon receiving
specific low-probability signals [12, 13]. While this activation mechanism
allows them to remain undetected during standard testing, their reliance on
additional circuitry, such as a trigger recognition model (TRM) for
detecting signals and processing payloads, introduces anomalies in the
circuit network table. These discrepancies increase their susceptibility to
detection through reverse engineering and pre-silicon analysis [11].
8.3.2 Classification of Triggering Methods for the HTs in CNN
Accelerators
According to the different trigger methods of hardware Trojans in CNN
accelerators, they can be categorized into four types: position-specific and
value-specific triggered HTs [3, 9, 14], neuron-specific triggered HTs [15,
16], pattern-specific triggered HTs [3, 5, 6], and sequence-specific triggered
HTs [7]. More details are introduced next (Fig. 8.1).
Fig. 8.1 Classification of triggering methods for the HTs in CNN accelerators
8.3.2.1 Position-Specific and Value-Specific Triggered HTs
This type of hardware Trojan (HT) operates by embedding malicious
information at specific positions within the input image. When the CNN
accelerator detects that the pixel values at these designated positions match

--- Strana 275 ---
predefined values, the HT is triggered [3]. The working process of this
attack is illustrated in Fig. 8.2.
Fig. 8.2 Position-specific and value-specific triggered HTs
8.3.2.2 Neuron-Specific Triggered HTs
This type of hardware Trojan (HT) operates by monitoring the output value
of a processing element (PE) in a specific layer of the CNN accelerator. The
HT is triggered when the output value exceeds a threshold k, predefined by
the adversary, and the cumulative number of exceedances surpasses a
specified value m [16]. The working process of this attack is illustrated in
Fig. 8.3.

--- Strana 276 ---
Fig. 8.3 Neuron-specific triggered HTs
8.3.2.3 Pattern-Specific Triggered HTs
This type of hardware Trojan (HT) employs a trigger recognition unit,
typically implemented as a tiny malicious CNN. The HT is triggered when
this miniature CNN detects a specific pattern, predefined by the adversary,
within the designated area of the input image [6]. The working process of
this attack is illustrated in Fig. 8.4.

--- Strana 277 ---
Fig. 8.4 Pattern-specific triggered HTs
8.3.2.4 Sequence-Specific Triggered HTs
This type of hardware Trojan (HT) is triggered by a specific sequence of
input images fed into the CNN accelerator. The accelerator processes these
images sequentially, outputting classification results as index values
corresponding to the output neurons associated with each image’s class. By
analyzing the sequence of these index values, the HT is triggered if the
observed sequence matches the adversary’s predefined sequence [7]. The
working process of this attack is illustrated in Fig. 8.5.

--- Strana 278 ---
Fig. 8.5 Sequence-specific triggered HTs
8.4 Design of Defense Mechanism
Hardware Trojan (HT) insertion represents a significant hardware-level
threat, predicated on an adversary’s capacity to maliciously alter design
files. Such attacks are primarily executed during the intellectual property
(IP) vending, system-on-chip (SoC) integration, and fabrication stages of
the integrated circuit (IC) supply chain [17]. It is posited that adversarial
actors are situated within the fabrication stage, where they can introduce
illicit modifications to the design files. These alterations are intended to
embed malicious functionalities, thereby compromising the integrity of
classification algorithms executed on the affected hardware.
In the domain of convolutional neural networks (CNNs), the
procurement of training data from third-party providers is a prevalent
practice. Although this approach serves to augment the available data pool,
it concurrently introduces a significant risk of backdoor vulnerabilities. The
sheer volume of contemporary datasets renders exhaustive image-by-image
inspection infeasible, thus creating opportunities for the surreptitious
embedding of such vulnerabilities [18, 19].

--- Strana 279 ---
In light of these distinct but related threats, this research broadens the
conventional threat model. The expanded framework, as depicted in Fig.
8.6, is augmented to encompass not only adversaries at the fabrication stage
but also those operating as third-party image providers. This holistic
approach allows for a more comprehensive analysis of the security
landscape for CNNs, from the underlying hardware to the training data.
Fig. 8.6 Threat modelling
Contemporary CNN systems are vulnerable to HTs that activate by
leveraging embedded activation cues within input images. These cues often
correlate with the malicious data’s location, sequence, and value, as
substantiated by a plethora of studies [3, 5–7, 9, 14–16]. In response to this
vulnerability, the “shuffle-substitution defense mechanism” (SSDM) is
developed [2]. SSDM is strategically formulated to curtail an adversary’s
capability to manipulate malicious data, thereby impeding HT activation
and neutralizing potential threats. The defense strategy involves shuffling

--- Strana 280 ---
and substituting training and testing images and shuffling output neurons of
CNN at the software and hardware level. These preprocessed images are
utilized to train the CNN models (Figs. 8.7 and 8.8).
Fig. 8.7 The structure of the CNN accelerator with SSDM and four types of HTs Preprocessing
block shows the hardware structure of the shuffle mechanism and substitution mechanism related to
the input images. The HTs in the CNN accelerators show the different hardware structures of trigger
recognition units of HTs
Fig. 8.8 The structure of shuffle and substitution-based defense mechanism
Additionally, the SSDM framework demonstrates potential robustness
against backdoor attacks, wherein adversaries covertly modify specific

--- Strana 281 ---
training images and labels to implant a backdoor during the CNN’s training
phase [19–22]. By perturbing the spatial attributes of these altered images,
the SSDM mechanism aims to render the covertly introduced backdoors
ineffectual.
8.4.1 Pixel-Level Shuffle Mechanism
The shuffle mechanism enhances the security of CNN accelerators against
both hardware Trojans (HTs) and backdoor attacks by disrupting the spatial
arrangement of input data. For HTs, this mechanism obfuscates the location
of malicious information, preventing the precise trigger conditions required
for activation, as adversaries at the fabrication stage cannot predict the
runtime shuffle patterns. In the case of backdoor attacks, altering the shuffle
order for noncritical regions of images disrupts the spatial coherence that
backdoor mechanisms rely on. This disruption effectively mitigates the
threat by preventing the CNN from detecting malicious patterns during the
inference stage when the arrangement of training and testing data is
inconsistent.
The pixel-level shuffle mechanism, inspired by approximate computing
concepts in image processing [23], divides training and test images into
important and unimportant regions, as illustrated in Fig. 8.9. This
segmentation is consistent across all images within a dataset due to the
similar data distribution within the dataset. Shuffling is applied with
varying granularity: at the pixel level within unimportant regions and at the
region level within important regions. Additionally, within unimportant
regions, pixel-level shuffle strategies remain consistent within a single
dataset but differ between datasets (i.e., between training and testing
datasets). For important regions, the region-level shuffling sequences are
identical across both training and test datasets.

--- Strana 282 ---
Fig. 8.9 Pixel-level shuffle mechanism
These strategies maintain the consistency of information distribution
within important regions across training and test datasets, ensuring minimal
degradation of classification accuracy. Simultaneously, the disorder
introduced in unimportant regions effectively mitigates the potential impact
of embedded malicious information. This approach enhances the model’s
resilience against adversarial attacks triggered by malicious information in
the input images, thereby improving the overall security of CNN
accelerators.
8.4.2 Neuron-Level Shuffle Mechanism
Neuron-level shuffling prevents the activation of neuron-specific triggered
hardware Trojans (HTs). In CNN accelerators, classification results are
represented as output neuron indices, which require a decoder for category
mapping. By modifying the sequence of these output neurons at the
hardware level, the accelerator’s output is altered, thereby confusing
adversaries who lack knowledge of the new order. This approach effectively

--- Strana 283 ---
defends against HTs that rely on predetermined output sequences for
activation.
To counter sequence-specific triggered HTs, it is essential to reorder the
output neurons. In convolutional neural networks (CNNs), the sequence of
output neurons can be altered by modifying the arrangement of weights in
the final fully connected layer. For example, in the GTSRB dataset [24],
which includes 43 output neurons corresponding to 43 unique sets of
weights in the final fully connected layer, the computation within this layer
can be expressed as shown in Eq. 8.1:
(8.1)
In Eq. 8.1, X represents the input array of the final fully connected
layer, Wi denotes an array of one weight set, and oi refers to the value of a
single output neuron. The equation illustrates that the sequence of the 43
weight sets directly corresponds to the sequence of the output neurons. By
reordering these weights, as shown in Eq. 8.2, the sequence of the output
neurons is similarly reordered. With the implementation of an appropriate
decoding mechanism at the software level, it becomes feasible to modify
the order of output neurons at the hardware level while still achieving
accurate classification outcomes:
(8.2)
8.4.3 Substitution Mechanism
The substitution mechanism effectively prevents the activation of hardware
Trojans (HTs), particularly those triggered by solid-colored images. It
achieves this by executing pixel-level substitutions using distinct
substitution maps for different regions of an image.
In this strategy, substitution maps are generated to provide a unique
mapping for each pixel value, ensuring that every pixel in a map is assigned
a distinct substitute while maintaining the overall relationship among pixel
[

--- Strana 284 ---
values. The process begins by segmenting the pixel value range (0–255)
into equivalent partitions. These segments are then randomly paired, with
elements from one segment mapped to elements in the paired segment.
Furthermore, the input image is divided into smaller regions, with each
region assigned a unique substitution map. The substitution process is
illustrated in Fig. 8.10. This approach modifies pixel values in such a way
that solid-colored images are eliminated, thereby preventing the activation
of HTs, while ensuring that the CNN processes the image data accurately
and effectively.
Fig. 8.10 Sectionalized pixel substitution strategy
For generating substitution maps for the non-important regions of the
training dataset, the pixel value range (0–255) is divided into fewer blocks,
with each block containing a substantial number of elements. Multiple
substitution maps are required for these regions to introduce sufficient
randomness. Conversely, when creating substitution maps for the important
regions of the training dataset and for the test dataset, the range is divided

--- Strana 285 ---
into numerous smaller segments, each containing fewer elements. In these
cases, the pairing among segments is not random but instead follows a
symmetrical mapping scheme to preserve critical information. Additionally,
only a few substitution maps are needed for these areas to maintain the
integrity of the important regions.
This differentiation ensures that important regions retain their detail and
relevance while the non-important regions are sufficiently obfuscated to
enhance randomness and disrupt potential patterns. Furthermore, using
fewer substitution maps for the test dataset reduces complexity and
facilitates efficient hardware implementation.
8.5 Hardware Implementation
The implementation of the SSDM within hardware is divided into three
principal components: substitution, pixel-level shuffle, and neuron-level
shuffle, as shown in Fig. 8.11.
Fig. 8.11 Hardware implementation of SSDM
The substitution phase is facilitated using a lookup table (LUT)
methodology. In this approach, each pixel value from the original image
serves as an index to retrieve a corresponding value from registers
containing substitution maps. This retrieved value then replaces the original
pixel value. To accommodate the use of multiple substitution maps for
different regions of the image, a multiplexer (MUX) is employed. The

--- Strana 286 ---
MUX allows for dynamic selection among different substitution maps,
ensuring adaptability and flexibility in substitution across varied image
regions.
The pixel-level shuffle mechanism requires pre-computation of a shuffle
order, which maps each pixel’s original position to its new shuffled
position. After the substitution phase, the pixel data is stored in memory
addresses according to this predetermined shuffle sequence, effectively
executing the shuffling process. This pre-computation ensures a seamless
and efficient pixel rearrangement while maintaining data consistency
throughout the process.
The neuron-level shuffle involves reordering the sets of weights in the
final fully connected layer. This reordering does not necessitate changes to
the memory address of the weights. Instead, the weight reading sequence is
altered, which effectively changes the order in which weights are stored in
the buffer. This method achieves the same performance as modifying
memory addresses without increasing complexity. Simultaneously, the
decoder is updated to correctly interpret the reordered outputs. This
implementation supports online shuffling of weight sets while preserving
memory access capabilities and overall system efficiency.
8.6 Evaluation Experiment Configuration for the
Approach
In this section, four different types of HTs will be introduced and evaluated
by inserting them into the CNN hardware accelerator. Afterward, the same
HTs are inserted into a CNN accelerator with a preprocessing module to be
tested and evaluated. A typical patch-based backdoor attack by third-party
dataset providers is then introduced, and the success rates of the backdoor
attack before and after the application of the SSDM countermeasures are
calculated. This validates the effectiveness of the new countermeasures in
preventing HT insertion attacks and backdoor attacks by third-party dataset
providers.
8.6.1 Hardware Trojan Insertion Attacks
The testing of hardware Trojan (HT) insertion attacks involves designing
various HTs based on known attack mechanisms and embedding them into

--- Strana 287 ---
CNN accelerators. This approach allows for a comprehensive evaluation of
the vulnerabilities and effectiveness of defensive strategies. A controlled
datasets of 18207 and 4320 gray-scale speed-limit traffic sign images from
GTSRB [24] are utilized for experimentation. The primary objective is to
assess the probability of successful HT activation under different scenarios,
providing insights into the robustness of CNN accelerators against such
attacks.
1. Position-specific and value-specific triggered HT: This type of
hardware Trojan (HT) operates by embedding malicious information at
specific positions within the input image. Activation is triggered when
the CNN accelerator detects pixel values at these positions matching
predefined criteria. In the experiments, the malicious data is positioned
at coordinates (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), and (2,2) in the input image, with
corresponding activation pixel values of 132, 121, 144, and 73. A
comparison of the images before and after the injection of the trigger
signal for position-specific and value-specific triggered HTs by
adversaries is illustrated in Fig. 8.12.
Fig. 8.12 The comparative of images before and after the implementation of position-specific
and value-specific triggered HTs
 
2. Neuron-specific triggered HT: This type of hardware Trojan (HT) is
triggered when the number of times the output value of a single
processing element (PE) exceeds a predefined threshold k surpasses  

--- Strana 288 ---
another threshold m. The architecture and operational design of the
neuron-specific HT are depicted in Fig. 8.13. For the MobileNet V2
model with the GTSRB dataset, the parameters are set to k = −1.745
and m = 1, 000, 000. For the ShuffleNetV2 model with the GTSRB
dataset, the parameters are k = −1.915 and m = 1, 000, 000.
Similarly, for the GhostNet model with the GTSRB dataset,
k = −1.645 and m = 1, 000, 000. The selected PE for triggering is
located in the second convolutional layer (2nd CONVL) of the
respective networks.
Fig. 8.13 The CNN accelerator with neuron-specific triggered HTs
3. Pattern-specific triggered HT: The activation mechanism for this
hardware Trojan (HT) employs a miniature malicious CNN that
identifies a predefined pattern within a designated area of the input
image. In this case, a 3 × 3 pixel “L” pattern is used as the trigger,
positioned within a 6 × 6 pixel area in the top-left corner of the image.
The visual differences in images before and after the injection of the
trigger information for pattern-specific triggered HTs by adversaries are
illustrated in Fig. 8.14.
 

--- Strana 289 ---
Fig. 8.14 The comparative of images before and after the implementation of pattern-specific
triggered HTs
4. Sequence-specific triggered HT: This type of hardware Trojan (HT) is
activated by feeding a sequence of images into the CNN accelerator,
which produces a series of outputs following a predefined sequence.
The operational flow of this attack is depicted in Fig. 8.15. In the
experiment, the predefined trigger sequence consists of the following
categories: category 2, category 1, category 6, category 4, category 9,
category 3, category 10, category 3, and category 4, corresponding to
the sequence (2, 1, 6, 4, 9, 3, 10, 3, 4).
Fig. 8.15 The attack’s operational flow of sequence-specific triggered HTs
 

--- Strana 290 ---
8.6.2 Backdoor Attacks from Third-Party Dataset Providers
In addition to hardware Trojan (HT) insertion attacks, adversarial backdoor
attacks on third-party datasets also pose significant threats. In such attacks,
the CNN neural network is trained on a poisoned image dataset, resulting in
a network embedded with a backdoor [19–22, 25]. When an image
containing a specific trigger is input into the backdoored CNN, the network
executes the adversary’s intended malicious behavior.
To investigate backdoor attacks, patch-based backdoor attacks on CNN
networks trained with poisoned datasets are analyzed. The experimental
methodology involves poisoning 10% of images in selected categories
within the 540 gray-scale speed-limit traffic sign images of 9 categories
from GTSRB. For testing, 50% of the images in the attacked categories are
poisoned to ensure data consistency and minimize randomness, enabling a
more controlled evaluation of the attacks’ effectiveness.
For the patch-based backdoor attack, adversaries embed a specific patch
into the original image [20, 21]. The location of the patch remains
consistent across all modified images in both the training and testing
datasets. This strategy leverages the CNN’s ability to learn and memorize
patterns, enabling the malicious patch to act as a backdoor. A visual
comparison between an original image and its modified counterpart
following a stable patch-based backdoor attack is illustrated in Fig. 8.16.
Fig. 8.16 The comparative of images before and after the implementation of stable patch-based
backdoor attacks
8.6.3 Defense Configuration

--- Strana 291 ---
In this section, the configuration of the implementation of the SSDM is
outlined. This configuration contains several critical steps:
 
1. Image Region Classification: Images are divided into regions of
varying significance based on their dataset. For GTSRB images, the
central 16 × 16 area is designated as important. The precise boundaries
of these important areas are depicted in Fig. 8.17.
Fig. 8.17 The division of the important area and unimportant area of GTSRB
 
2. Substitution Map Generation: For generating substitution maps in the
unimportant regions of the training dataset, the pixel value range (0–
255) is divided into two segments, each containing 128 pixel values, to
form a single substitution map. To meet the requirements of the SSDM
mechanism, a total of 30 such substitution maps are created. For the
important regions in both the training and test datasets, four
substitution maps are generated. Each map consists of 32 groups, with
each group containing eight elements. These substitution maps are also
applied to the unimportant regions of the test dataset, ensuring
consistency and alignment with the defense strategy.
 
3. Important Area Segmentation for Shuffling: The important regions
across all datasets are divided into four approximately equal parts using
a lateral segmentation approach.
 

--- Strana 292 ---
8.7 Experiment Results and Evaluation
8.7.1 Evaluation of Defense Performance
The results, summarized in Table 8.1, provide a comprehensive evaluation
of SSDM’s impact on five specified attack methods: position-specific and
value-specific triggered HTs, neuron-specific triggered HTs, pattern-
specific triggered HTs, sequence-specific triggered HTs, and patch-based
backdoor attacks.
Table 8.1 Attack success rate of HT or backdoor attacks (using MNIST
dataset)
 
MobileNet V2
Type
SSDM SSDM-free
Position-specific and value-specific triggered HTs 0%
100%
Neuron-specific triggered HTs
100%
100%
Pattern-specific triggered HTs
2%
98%
Sequence-specific triggered HTs
0%
100%
Patch-based backdoor attacks
12%
94%
The findings in Table 8.1 demonstrate a significant increase in
robustness following the implementation of SSDM. For sequence-specific,
pattern-specific, position-specific, and value-specific triggered HTs, as well
as patch-based backdoor attacks, SSDM reduces the attack success rates
from nearly 100% to around 10%. However, in the case of neuron-specific
triggered hardware Trojans, the CNN accelerator equipped with the
preprocessing module showed no mitigation effect, as the trigger success
rate remained consistently at 100%.
These results underscore SSDM’s efficacy as a robust defense
mechanism, effectively mitigating the threats posed by various hardware
Trojan and backdoor attack methods, while maintaining a reasonable trade-
off between classification accuracy and security.
8.8 Conclusions

--- Strana 293 ---
This chapter introduces the shuffle and substitution-based defense
mechanism (SSDM). SSDM addresses security vulnerabilities in CNN
accelerator by introducing innovative techniques, such as pixel-level
shuffling, neuron-level reordering, and substitution mechanisms. These
measures aim to disrupt malicious activation triggers while preserving
model performance. The effectiveness of SSDM is validated through
extensive experiments, demonstrating its ability to significantly reduce the
success rates of HTs and backdoor attacks.
